Markdown source

Preserved historical original. Statements below reflect this source document; they are not a current implementation or a new measurement.

HIVE AI — 100 Similar Projects: Succeeded, Failed, or In Progress, and Why

Scope: Projects similar to HIVE AI as described in HIVE_AI_Core_Architecture_0_39e1.md — modular / composable intelligence, multi-agent orchestration, mixture-of-experts and modular LLMs, specialist and adapter models, cognitive architectures, bio-inspired / neuromorphic / swarm systems, memory / verification / self-improvement, and enterprise orchestration.

Research date: 18–19 November 2026. Each project was researched individually against primary sources (official site/docs, GitHub, Wikipedia, company blog, reputable news). Status definitions used throughout:

  • Succeeded — shipped, widely adopted, commercially or academically sustained, or acquired for its tech and still in use.
  • In Progress — actively developed / maintained in 2025–2026, with releases, commits, or users.
  • Failed — shut down, abandoned, pivoted away, or superseded with no active maintenance.
  • Stalled/Abandoned — no meaningful release, commit, or news in 12+ months, but no formal shutdown.

Coverage: 100 / 100 researched. No unverified entries.

Headline counts

  • Succeeded: 44
  • In Progress: 29
  • Failed: 14
  • Stalled/Abandoned: 13

That is 44% succeeded, 29% still in progress, and 27% failed or stalled. The field is not a graveyard — but it is not a safe bet either. Roughly 1 in 4 comparable attempts are dead or dormant, and most of the survivors are either a narrow specialist, a platform owned by a large vendor, or a deterministic orchestration layer — not a free-form autonomous multi-agent swarm.

By category

  • Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm (10): Succeeded 2, In Progress 7, Failed 1, Stalled 0
  • Cognitive architecture/AGI (12): Succeeded 3, In Progress 6, Failed 0, Stalled 3
  • Memory/Verification/Self-improvement (7): Succeeded 5, In Progress 1, Failed 0, Stalled 1
  • MoE/Modular LLM (17): Succeeded 8, In Progress 2, Failed 6, Stalled 1
  • Multi-agent framework (24): Succeeded 9, In Progress 7, Failed 4, Stalled 4
  • Orchestration/Enterprise (20): Succeeded 10, In Progress 4, Failed 3, Stalled 3
  • Specialist/Adapter (10): Succeeded 7, In Progress 2, Failed 0, Stalled 1

The pattern — why they succeed or fail

What the winners have in common

  1. A narrow, measurable job first. AlphaFold, DeepSeek-Math / Llemma, Med-PaLM / MedGemma, AlphaCode, and the MoE models that shipped (Mixtral, DeepSeek-V3, Qwen-MoE, Llama 4 Maverick) all won on one benchmarkable task before anyone talked about generality. HIVE's Research Question 1 — how small can an expert stay while staying excellent on a precisely defined task — is the right first test.
  2. Deterministic control around the agents, not autonomy alone. LangGraph (GA 1.0, Oct 2025, no-breaking-changes commitment), CrewAI Flows, Google ADK, and Temporal / Ray survived by giving developers graphs, state, retries, and observability. Pure free-form agent loops — AutoGPT Classic, BabyAGI, AgentGPT — went viral, then stalled or were archived.
  3. A platform or distribution owner. MCP, OpenAI Agents SDK, Google ADK / A2A, Microsoft Semantic Kernel / Agent Framework, NVIDIA Triton / NIM, and vLLM are in progress or succeeded because a large vendor or the Hugging Face ecosystem keeps them alive. Standalone frameworks without that backing (AutoGen, AgentVerse, OpenAgents) were superseded or went dormant.
  4. Adapters and routing, not retraining everything. LoRA / PEFT, AdapterHub, Mixture-of-Agents, and LLM routing (FrugalGPT / RouteLLM) succeeded as infrastructure: cheap specialization, swappable skills, and a router that picks the cheapest good-enough expert. That is the closest proven analogue to HIVE's dynamic-specialization layer (Section 5 of the HIVE doc) — and the evidence says it works when the adapters are compatible and trained, not when a role label is just reassigned.
  5. Verification as a first-class layer. Reflexion, Self-Refine, STaR, and the critic / teacher / reviewer pattern in ChatDev and MetaGPT survived academically and in products because generation without a critic does not compound. HIVE Layer 8 (Verification and Evolution) is not optional garnish; in the winners it is the product.

Why the failures failed

  1. Coordination overhead ate the gains. Multi-agent conversation in natural language is slow, lossy, and expensive. Projects that forced every specialist to talk in English, with no domain-native representation or structured Task / Evidence / Control channels, burned tokens and diverged. This is exactly HIVE's Research Question 3 — and it is unproven until measured against a natural-language baseline.
  2. Breaking rewrites fragmented the community. AutoGen's v0.2 → v0.4 rewrite, and similar re-architectures elsewhere, split users and forks (AG2). A hive that cannot keep a stable orchestration API while specialists change will lose its own users the same way.
  3. Viral demo, no production path. AutoGPT (188k GitHub stars), AgentGPT (100k+ users in week one), and BabyAGI proved demand for autonomous agents and then had no guardrails, no cost control, no observability, and no enterprise buyer. Stars are not survival.
  4. Hardware without software, or biology as metaphor only. Loihi, TrueNorth, SpiNNaker, BrainScaleS, and DishBrain are scientifically real and mostly still in progress or niche — but none became a general AI platform, because the programming model and the killer application never arrived together. Bio-inspired coordination (HIVE Layer 6) needs a concrete programming model and a benchmark, not just an analogy.
  5. Cognitive architectures that never shipped a product. SOAR, ACT-R, CLARION, LIDA, NARS, and OpenCog Hyperon have decades of research and real ideas, but most are stalled or academic-only. The lesson is not that they were wrong — it is that an architecture without a shipped, measured specialist community does not survive.
  6. Consumer hardware bets. Rabbit R1 and Humane AI Pin failed on the same axis: a specialist device / LAM that could not beat a phone app on reliability, latency, or price. HIVE should not tie its fate to a single device form factor early.

The uncomfortable middle

  • MoE is the proven modular win, and the cautionary tale. Switch Transformer, GLaM, Mixtral, DeepSeek-MoE, and Qwen-MoE succeeded as engineering: sparse activation, cheaper inference at scale. But several MoE launches were also superseded within a year by a denser or better-trained model. Modularity alone does not beat a better monolith — HIVE's Research Question 5 (quality / latency / memory / cost vs. strong conventional baselines) has to be answered head-to-head, not assumed.
  • IBM Watson is the classic warning. A famous demo (Jeopardy, 2011), then Watson Health collapsed on messy real-world data, cost, and overpromising. A hive that claims a domain community before it has a verified specialist in that domain will repeat it.
  • Superseded is not the same as wrong. AutoGen and Swarm were both 'failed' in this table because their vendors replaced them with a successor (Microsoft Agent Framework, OpenAI Agents SDK). The ideas survived; the brand did not. HIVE should design for that: a stable protocol / memory format outlives any one orchestrator.

What this means for HIVE — the short version

  • Prove one specialist community end-to-end (e.g. mathematics, as in HIVE §4) with a critic, a teacher, and a measured baseline before adding more domains.
  • Ship the deterministic layer first: routing, state, retries, ALL-STOP-style control, and observability. The autonomous layer sits on top of it, not instead of it.
  • Measure domain-native communication vs. natural-language agent chat on latency, cost, and information loss. If it does not win, cut it.
  • Keep adapters compatible and trained; do not assume a 1B core can swap algebra for story by relabelling it.
  • Do not build the bio-inspired layer as metaphor. Give it a programming model, a benchmark, and a fallback to ordinary orchestration.

Full table — all 100

# Project Category Status What it is Key dates Why
1 Microsoft AutoGen Multi-agent framework Failed Microsoft Research's open-source framework for building multi-agent AI applications in which LLM agents collaborate through structured conversation. GitHub repo created 2023-08-18; first released fall/October 2023; v0.4 ground-up async/event-driven rewrite announced 2025-01-14; last feature release python-v0.7.5 on 2025-09-30; maintenance mode from 2025-10-02; successor Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA 2026-04-03; AutoGen README changed to 'archived and no longer maintained' on 2026-10-16 per secondary reports, confirmed in the live README fetched 2026-11-19. AutoGen succeeded as research and popularised conversational multi-agent orchestration, but its v0.2-to-v0.4 breaking rewrite fragmented users and its original creators left to continue the old design as the AG2 fork. Microsoft then consolidated AutoGen with Semantic Kernel into a single enterprise-supported Microsoft Agent Framework with stable APIs and long-term support, so the standalone project was superseded by design rather than collapsing from a single technical failure.
2 CrewAI Multi-agent framework In Progress CrewAI is an MIT-licensed open-source Python framework for orchestrating role-based teams of autonomous AI agents (Crews) within deterministic, event-driven workflows (Flows), plus the commercial CrewAI AMP platform for deploying, observing, and governing them in production. Repo created 27 Oct 2023; first PyPI release 4 Dec 2023; company launched Jan 2024; $18M seed+Series A announced 22 Oct 2024; CrewAI Enterprise launched late 2024; 1.0 GA and CrewAI AMP (formerly AOP) launched Oct 2025; v1.15.22 stable release 16 Sep 2026; active releases/docs through Nov 2026. It succeeded by lowering the barrier to multi-agent prototyping: describing agents by role, goal, and backstory is an intuitive mental model, the MIT-licensed open source seeded wide community and enterprise adoption, and Flows added the deterministic control production use required. Monetization followed that adoption via an enterprise control plane rather than paywalling the framework. Remaining risk is coordination overhead and reliability of autonomous crews, illustrated by the March 2026 security flaws and the removal of its $25/month mid-tier, suggesting enterprise pricing and governance are still being tuned.
3 LangGraph (LangChain) Multi-agent framework Succeeded LangGraph is LangChain's open-source, MIT-licensed low-level graph/state-machine framework and runtime for building long-running, stateful single- and multi-agent applications with checkpointing, branching/loops, and human-in-the-loop control. Introduced Jan 2024; v0.1 stable release 27 Jun 2024; LangGraph 1.0 GA 17-22 Oct 2025 (tagged 17 Oct, announced 22 Oct); LangGraph Platform renamed LangSmith Deployment Oct 2025; v1.2.21 released 12 Nov 2026 It succeeded because it solved the reliability problem of opaque early agent abstractions (notably LangChain's AgentExecutor) by giving developers explicit control over state, control flow, persistence, and approvals, with durable checkpointing and time-travel/resume. It became the execution layer under LangChain's own create_agent in the 1.0 release, locking in adoption, while the free MIT core drove developer uptake and paid LangSmith observability/deployment monetized production use. Its timing matched enterprise demand for controllable, auditable agents in 2024-2025, backed by $125M Series B funding at a $1.25B valuation.
4 MetaGPT Multi-agent framework Stalled/Abandoned An open-source Python multi-agent framework from DeepWisdom/FoundationAgents that simulates a software company — Product Manager, Architect, Project Manager, Engineer and QA agents collaborating via codified SOPs — to turn a one-line requirement into documents and code. Repo created 2023-06-30; arXiv paper posted 2023-08-01; public release late Aug 2023; ICLR 2024 oral (top 1.2%); MGX launched 2025-02-19; latest release v0.8.2 on 2025-03-09; last commit 2026-01-21; MGX rebranded Atoms 2026-01-13; no shutdown/archival as of 2026-11-18. Its rigid SOP/company-role structure produced impressive demos but was brittle, hard to customise and debug, and expensive in coordination and LLM calls per run, limiting production adoption despite ~71k stars. The team shifted focus and funding to the commercial MGX/Atoms product and to OpenManus, leaving the framework with no release since Mar 2025 and no commit since Jan 2026. Star count therefore reflects historical influence, not current maintenance.
5 AutoGPT Multi-agent framework In Progress An open-source project, now the AutoGPT Platform, in which a coordinating agent ('Otto') directs a team of specialist AI 'Experts' and visual-block workflows to carry out recurring multi-step work. Repo created 16 Mar 2023; released 30 Mar 2023; $12M funding Oct 2023; AutoGPT Platform launched 24 Sep 2024; Classic marked unsupported Nov 2024; latest platform beta v0.11.2, 14 Nov 2026 The original 2023 autonomous GPT-4 loop went viral but was unreliable in practice: it hallucinated, got stuck in loops, accumulated errors, and incurred high API costs with no measurable success criterion. The team therefore rebuilt it in 2024 as a structured, human-authored platform of typed workflow blocks, scheduled routines, and supervised experts, which is more reliable but still in beta more than two years later — so its 188k stars largely reflect 2023 hype for a product that no longer exists in that form, not proven commercial adoption of the current platform.
6 BabyAGI Multi-agent framework Stalled/Abandoned An open-source experimental autonomous-agent framework by Yohei Nakajima, originally a ~140-line Python script looping task creation, execution, and reprioritization with GPT-4 and a vector store, later rebuilt as a 'self-building' function framework. 28 Mar 2023: first posted by Yohei Nakajima; 3 Apr 2023: GitHub repo created; Sep 2024: original task-loop archived to babyagi_archive; 2024: repo rebuilt as 'functionz' self-building agent framework; 11 Oct 2024: last code change; 31 Jan 2026: last commit (docs-only); early 2026: 'BabyAGI 3' mentioned in secondary sources but not evidenced by code activity in the main repo. It was a viral demo, not a product: the unbounded task loop racked up API costs, hallucinated tasks, and looped without verification, guardrails, or a stopping condition. It stayed a solo, nights-and-weekends experiment with no team, funding, or commercial model, while the field moved to structured frameworks with tools, evals, and human-in-the-loop control. The original loop was archived in September 2024 and the rebuild has had no code change since October 2024, leaving it an explicitly non-production idea repository rather than a sustained system.
7 SuperAGI Multi-agent framework Succeeded A dev-first open-source autonomous AI agent framework (Python/FastAPI + GUI, tool marketplace, memory, workflows) whose company pivoted to a commercial AI sales/GTM platform and was acquired by QuestionPro in November 2026. Company founded 2021 as Contlo Inc. (Palo Alto); open-source framework repo created May 13, 2023; last release v0.0.14 Jan 2024; last framework commit Jan 22, 2025; $10M Series A Mar 2024; acquired by QuestionPro Nov 4, 2026. The open-source framework succeeded as a 2023 hype-era hit (~17.7k stars) but stalled: no release since Jan 2024 and no commits since Jan 2025, with publicly disclosed unpatched vulnerabilities, because the company redirected all effort to its paid SaaS GTM product. That pivot was the economically rational move — the framework alone had no revenue model — and it ended in an acquisition, with QuestionPro buying the company (Contlo/SuperAGI) for its agentic platform to power agentic commerce. So the framework is effectively abandoned while the company succeeded via pivot and exit.
8 CAMEL (Communicative Agents for Mind Exploration) Multi-agent framework Succeeded An open-source Python multi-agent framework from KAUST that lets LLM agents autonomously cooperate via role-playing with inception prompting. Repo created 2023-03-17; arXiv paper submitted 2023-03-31 (v2 Nov 2023); accepted at NeurIPS 2023; OWL spin-off gained prominence in 2025; still actively released in 2026 (camel-ai 0.2.90, Oct 2026). It succeeded because its core idea was simple, general, and immediately usable — two agents with assigned roles collaborating with minimal human input — and because it grew beyond a single paper demo into a sustained open-source community (Apache 2.0, 100+ researchers) that kept adding Workforce, RAG, data generation, and large-scale simulation (OASIS, OWL). Academic adoption and continuous releases, rather than venture funding or a commercial product, sustained it while many 2023-era agent frameworks froze or went dormant.
9 ChatDev Multi-agent framework In Progress An open-source multi-agent framework from OpenBMB/Tsinghua that originally simulated a virtual software company (CEO, CTO, programmer, tester) collaborating through chat chains, now pivoted to ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll), a zero-code YAML/visual multi-agent orchestration platform. 2023-06-30 initial repo; 2023-07-16 arXiv preprint (2307.07924); 2023-08-17 v1.0.0; 2023-08-28 public release; 2023-11-15 SaaS launch; 2024 ACL 2024 publication; 2026-01-07 ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll) release, v1.x moved to chatdev1.0 maintenance branch; 2026-08 ChatDev 2.0 accepted to EMNLP 2026 Demo Track The original rigid waterfall role-play was a strong research demo — fast and cheap on small toy apps — but fixed roles and chat-only coordination limited real-world code quality and adaptability as single-agent coding tools improved. Rather than dying, the team pivoted in January 2026 to a general-purpose zero-code platform (DevAll), keeping the v1 software-company pipeline only as a maintenance-branch demo. Sustained academic output (ACL 2024, NeurIPS 2025 puppeteer work, EMNLP 2026 Demo) and 34.8k stars kept it alive, though stars have not translated into verified commercial adoption.
10 AgentVerse Multi-agent framework Stalled/Abandoned AgentVerse is an open-source multi-agent framework that dynamically recruits LLM expert agents and loops through collaborative decision-making, action execution, and evaluation to solve tasks or run simulations. Launched 1 May 2023; repo created 6 May 2023; arXiv paper 21 Aug 2023 (revised 23 Oct 2023); ICLR 2024 acceptance announced 17 Jan 2024; last commit 9 Sep 2024; no shutdown or pivot announced It succeeded as research — ICLR 2024 and 5.1k GitHub stars — but had no product owner, commercial model, or maintainer to carry it past the paper, so development stopped in September 2024. It was superseded by actively maintained frameworks such as AutoGen/LangGraph/CrewAI and Microsoft's Magentic-One/Agent Framework, while its flat dynamic-recruitment design carried high coordination and token costs and documented emergent harms such as conformity and destructive criticism that are hard to make reliable in production.
11 OpenAgents (XLang Lab; listed in task as Tsinghua / OpenBMB) Multi-agent framework Stalled/Abandoned An open-source platform for using and hosting language agents in everyday life, with three demo agents: a Data Agent, a Plugins Agent with 200+ tools, and a Web Agent for autonomous browsing. Created/demos launched 8 Aug 2023; paper submitted 16 Oct 2023 and published at COLM 2024; reached 3,000 users 26 Oct 2023; last code push 18 Nov 2024; no releases or meaningful activity since. It was an academic proof-of-concept and demo platform, not a sustained product: it had no releases, commercial model, or ongoing maintainer commitment, and its hosted demo and 200+ third-party plugins were brittle and costly to maintain. The same lab moved on to successor research, notably OSWorld (NeurIPS 2024) and OpenCUA (2025-2026), while LangGraph, CrewAI, and built-in ChatGPT-style agents superseded its approach. With no code push in 24 months and a critical vulnerability left unpatched, it was effectively abandoned without a formal shutdown notice.
12 AgentGPT (Reworkd) Multi-agent framework Failed AgentGPT was Reworkd's open-source, browser-based platform that let users name an autonomous AI agent, give it a goal, and watch it decompose the goal into subtasks and execute them in a loop using GPT-3.5/GPT-4. Launched April 2023; v1.0.0 November 2023 (development effectively stopped); Reworkd pivoted to web-scraping agents July 2024; GitHub repository archived 28 January 2026 The general-purpose autonomous loop was too broad and unreliable for production use — agents went off-track, hallucinated completion, and had no evaluation or observability — while free viral usage cost ~$2,000/day in API calls without converting to paying customers. Reworkd therefore pivoted in July 2024 to the one concrete, high-volume use case, AI web-scraping/data extraction, abandoning AgentGPT, whose code was frozen at its November 2023 release until GitHub archival in January 2026.
13 Hugging Face SmolAgents Multi-agent framework Succeeded Hugging Face's minimal open-source Python library for building LLM agents whose CodeAgent writes actions as executable Python code instead of JSON tool calls. Repository created 2024-12-05; launched via Hugging Face blog 2024-12-31 (v1.0.0, December 2024); latest release v1.26.0 on 2026-05-29; no shutdown, acquisition, or pivot as of November 2026. It succeeded because of radical minimalism — a readable ~1,000-line core at launch — plus the code-as-action insight, which composes loops and conditionals in one step and measurably cut steps versus JSON tool calls, and Hugging Face distribution (Hub sharing, model-agnostic LiteLLM/Ollama/OpenAI support, MCP tools). Its limits are the flip side of that minimalism: no built-in durable state/checkpointing or enterprise orchestration, and code execution creates a serious sandboxing/security burden (a critical remote-executor RCE published Dec 2025 took weeks to fix), which likely contributes to the slower 2026 release cadence.
14 OpenAI Swarm / OpenAI Agents SDK Multi-agent framework In Progress OpenAI's lightweight open-source multi-agent orchestration framework built on Agents, handoffs, guardrails and tracing, originally released as the experimental Swarm and productionised as the Agents SDK. Swarm repo created 22 Feb 2024 and publicly released as an experimental framework in late 2024; OpenAI Agents SDK launched 11 Mar 2025 alongside the Responses API, replacing Swarm; Agents SDK v0.25.0 released 18 Nov 2026. Swarm was deliberately experimental and educational — stateless, client-side, with no official production support — so its 22k stars proved demand for the handoff pattern rather than for Swarm itself as a product. OpenAI therefore productionised the pattern instead of maintaining two frameworks, adding tracing, guardrails, sessions and Responses API integration in the Agents SDK. The lineage survives because the SDK stays minimal and OpenAI-native, but it remains pre-1.0 (0.x) and platform-centred, so its success is ongoing adoption rather than a finished, stable standard.
15 Google Agent Development Kit (ADK) Multi-agent framework In Progress Google's open-source, code-first framework for building, evaluating, and deploying AI agents and multi-agent systems across Python, TypeScript, Go, Java, and Kotlin. Repo created Apr 1, 2025; publicly launched Apr 9, 2025 at Google Cloud Next 2025; ADK 1.0 GA mid/late 2025; ADK 2.0 GA (Python) May 19, 2026; latest release v2.14.1 Nov 12, 2026. No shutdown, acquisition, or pivot. It succeeded in gaining traction because Google backs it as the same framework powering its own products (Agentspace, Customer Engagement Suite), it is Apache 2.0, model-agnostic via LiteLLM, and ships native A2A and MCP support with a roughly bi-weekly release cadence. Its main friction is churn: ADK 2.0 introduced breaking API/event/session changes and minor 2.x releases have carried further breaking changes, plus six CVEs in 2026, so adoption is strong but the framework is still stabilizing rather than fully mature.
16 Microsoft Magentic-One Multi-agent framework Succeeded Microsoft Research's open-source generalist multi-agent system in which a lead Orchestrator plans, tracks progress in ledgers and replans while directing specialist WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder and ComputerTerminal agents to complete complex multi-step web and file-based tasks. Launched November 2024 (Microsoft Research blog 4 Nov 2024; arXiv:2411.04468); ported to autogen-agentchat with original implementation deprecated; AutoGen maintenance mode ~Oct 2025; Magentic orchestration in Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA 2-3 Apr 2026 It succeeded technically because the orchestrator-plus-ledgers design with stall detection and outer-loop replanning was benchmark-validated and modular enough to be ported from AutoGen into the Microsoft Agent Framework, where it remains a supported pattern. Its limits were economic and safety-related rather than fatal: iterative manager-led runs are costly and unpredictable, and autonomous browser/file actions required Docker containment and human oversight, so it survived as a research-derived orchestration pattern inside a production framework rather than as a standalone consumer product.
17 Microsoft TaskWeaver Multi-agent framework Failed Microsoft Research's open-source code-first agent framework that plans data-analytics tasks and executes LLM-generated Python in a stateful Jupyter kernel with plugins. Paper arXiv:2311.17541 (Nov 2023); repo created 11 Sep 2023; released on GitHub 30 Nov 2023; last feature update 13 Mar 2025 (vision input for Planner); archived 23 Mar 2026. It remained a research project with explicitly limited maintainer resources and only two alpha releases, never reaching a stable release or a pip package. Microsoft consolidated its agent efforts into the newer Microsoft Agent Framework (successor to AutoGen and Semantic Kernel), superseding TaskWeaver. Its narrow data-analytics niche and code-execution security burden limited broader adoption, so it was archived in March 2026 rather than sustained.
18 Alibaba AgentScope Multi-agent framework In Progress Alibaba Tongyi Lab's open-source (Apache 2.0), production-ready multi-agent framework built around ReAct agents, with tool/MCP/skill management, multi-agent team orchestration, sandboxed execution, observability, and a multi-tenant FastAPI agent service. Repo created 2024-01-12; original paper submitted 2024-02-21 (arXiv:2402.14034); AgentScope 1.0 launched 2025-09-02 (paper arXiv:2508.16279, Aug 2025); AgentScope 2.0 released in 2026; v2.0.12 released 2026-11-16. No shutdown, acquisition, or pivot. It has sustained because of durable corporate backing from Alibaba Tongyi Lab, a permissive open-source license, and model-agnostic design spanning OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, DashScope/Qwen, DeepSeek and local models, plus production features (observability, sandboxing, multi-tenancy, A2A/MCP support) that kept it competitive through major rewrites. Adoption kept compounding into late 2026 — stars and PyPI downloads were still accelerating in November 2026 — rather than stalling after the 1.0 release. The main cost has been churn: the 1.x-to-2.0 refactor broke APIs and invalidated older tutorials, and late-2026 security advisories show the fast-moving surface still needs hardening.
19 Anthropic Model Context Protocol (MCP) Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded MCP is an open, JSON-RPC-based standard that lets AI applications connect to external tools, data sources and prompts through reusable MCP servers instead of bespoke per-system integrations. Launched/open-sourced 25 Nov 2024 by Anthropic; OpenAI added support Mar 2025, Google/Gemini Apr 2025, Microsoft Copilot Studio GA May 2025; official MCP Registry preview 8 Sep 2025; donated 9 Dec 2025 to the Agentic AI Foundation under the Linux Foundation; current specification 2026-07-28. It succeeded because it solved a concrete N×M integration problem with a simple, open standard at the moment agents needed tool access, then won cross-vendor adoption from OpenAI, Google and Microsoft within months. Neutral governance — donation to the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation in Dec 2025 — removed single-vendor lock-in risk and cemented it as shared infrastructure, while remaining risks are security/trust around arbitrary tool execution rather than adoption.
20 Microsoft Semantic Kernel multi-agent Multi-agent framework Succeeded Microsoft's open-source, MIT-licensed SDK for building AI agents and multi-agent systems with plugins, connectors, memory, and orchestration patterns in .NET, Python, and Java. Launched 17 March 2023; multi-agent Agent Framework introduced 1 August 2024, RC 28 February 2025, GA 4 April 2025; Microsoft Agent Framework public preview October 2025 and 1.0 GA 3 April 2026 as the successor unifying Semantic Kernel and AutoGen; Semantic Kernel v1.x supported for at least one year after Agent Framework GA. It succeeded through Microsoft/Azure enterprise distribution, strong .NET type safety, dependency injection, telemetry, and a broad connector/plugin ecosystem, making it a credible choice for embedding copilots and multi-agent workflows in existing enterprise apps. It was nevertheless superseded as a standalone strategic direction because it overlapped and fragmented the community with AutoGen, and its multi-agent orchestration was verbose and partly experimental, requiring developers to hand-roll turn-taking and termination. Microsoft therefore consolidated both lineages into Microsoft Agent Framework, carrying Semantic Kernel's enterprise plumbing forward rather than abandoning it.
21 IBM BeeAI / watsonx Orchestrate Orchestration/Enterprise In Progress IBM's paired agentic stack: BeeAI is the open-source Python/TypeScript framework for building production multi-agent systems, and watsonx Orchestrate is IBM's commercial enterprise platform for building, orchestrating, governing, and observing those agents at scale. BeeAI began internally at IBM Research in early 2024 (repo created 2024-08-23); donated to the Linux Foundation in March 2025; ACP merged into Google's A2A protocol in August 2025; watsonx Orchestrate agent-building/ADK tech preview 30 April 2025, agentic relaunch with 150+ agent catalog 6 May 2025, agentic workflows GA 7 October 2025; next-generation Orchestrate announced as private preview 5 May 2026. It survives on IBM's enterprise distribution, governance/observability (AgentOps), and open-protocol bets — donating BeeAI and ACP to the Linux Foundation and converging on A2A/MCP — rather than on developer mindshare. Adoption lags LangGraph (~30k stars) and CrewAI (~30k stars) in a crowded framework market, the framework was still 0.x in November 2026 with rapid, breaking iteration, and the next-generation Orchestrate was only in private preview in May 2026, so it is actively sustained but not yet widely adopted.
22 Salesforce Agentforce Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Salesforce's enterprise platform for building, deploying, and governing autonomous AI agents that act on CRM and business data across service, sales, marketing, and operations. Unveiled September 12, 2024 at Dreamforce; generally available October 2024; Agentforce 2.0 December 2024; 2dx March 2025; Agentforce 3 June 23, 2025; Agentforce 360 generally available October 13, 2025; new Core/Advanced/Max editions at Dreamforce September 2026. It succeeded primarily through distribution and grounding rather than novel agent research: agents were embedded in Salesforce's existing CRM, Data Cloud/Data 360, permissions, and workflows, so customers did not need a new system of record. Bounded autonomy with topics/subagents, guardrails, the Einstein Trust Layer, and later Command Center observability, testing, and MCP interoperability in Agentforce 3 made enterprise deployment governable, while iterative releases and consumption pricing ($2/conversation, later Flex Credits) lowered adoption friction. The main caveat is uneven depth of adoption — many deployments remain bounded pilots rather than end-to-end autonomous workflows.
23 UiPath Agentic Automation Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded UiPath's enterprise platform that unifies AI agents, RPA robots, APIs, and people under Maestro orchestration with governance, audit, and human-in-the-loop controls. UiPath founded 2005; agentic platform private preview Jan 2025; UiPath Platform for Agentic Automation launched Apr 30, 2025, with Maestro orchestration and Agents GA in May 2025; expanded offerings Sep 30, 2025; WorkFusion acquired Feb 2026; on-premises agentic AI on Automation Suite shipped Apr 15, 2026 / announced May 5, 2026; Maestro Flow GA Sep 2026. It succeeded commercially because it layered agents onto an existing large RPA installed base and distribution channel rather than starting from zero, and because it paired non-deterministic agent reasoning with deterministic robot/API execution and enterprise governance that regulated buyers require. Continued 2025-2026 releases — Maestro Case/Flow, ScreenPlay, on-premises agentic AI, and vertical solutions such as financial crime compliance — show adoption expanding from pilots into operating-model use inside existing customers.
24 Devin (Cognition AI) Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded An autonomous AI software engineer from Cognition that takes a ticket, plans, codes, tests and debugs in its own cloud sandbox, and returns a pull request. Cognition founded Aug 2023; Devin unveiled Mar 12, 2024; generally available Dec 10, 2024; Devin 2.0 Apr 2025; Windsurf acquired Jul 14, 2025, rebranded Devin Desktop Jun 2, 2026; Series D May 27, 2026 ($1B+ at $26B); Series E Sep 8, 2026 ($2B+ at $48B); SWE-2 Sep 10, 2026; SWE-2 Flash Nov 4, 2026 Devin succeeded by narrowing autonomy to scoped, verifiable engineering work — a sandboxed shell/editor/browser plus test suites and CI as the acceptance check — rather than open-ended autonomy, and by iterating after its disputed 2024 demo (merge rate 34% to 67%) while cutting entry pricing from $500 to $20/month. Its model-agnostic harness plus in-house SWE models, dogfooding (Cognition claims ~90% of its own code is written by Devin), enterprise go-to-market, and the Windsurf acquisition expanding it into an IDE/CLI/review suite turned a viral demo into a sustained commercial platform.
25 LangChain Agents ecosystem Multi-agent framework Succeeded An open-source Python/JavaScript framework for building LLM agents, now centred on LangGraph as its stateful multi-agent runtime and LangSmith as its commercial observability, deployment and governance platform. Founded as an open-source project Oct 2022 (repo created 17 Oct 2022, v0.0.1 on 25 Oct 2022); company co-founded early 2023; LangGraph first released 8 Jan 2024; Deep Agents launched 29 Jul 2025; LangChain 1.0 and LangGraph 1.0 GA 22 Oct 2025; $300M Series C announced 13 Nov 2026. It was first to market in Oct 2022, five weeks before ChatGPT, and won on breadth — 1,000+ model, tool and data integrations made it the default starting point. When early chains were criticised as opaque and over-abstracted, it pivoted the agent layer onto LangGraph's durable, checkpointed, human-in-the-loop graph runtime and stabilised APIs in 1.0 (Oct 2025), turning a demo toolkit into production infrastructure. Commercially it monetised that infrastructure via LangSmith (observability, evals, deployment, governance) rather than the open-source libraries, sustaining a unicorn-scale business while the core remained MIT-licensed and actively developed.
26 Mistral Mixtral 8x7B / 8x22B MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded Mistral AI's Apache 2.0 open-weight sparse mixture-of-experts LLMs — Mixtral 8x7B (46.7B total parameters, 12.9B active per token) and Mixtral 8x22B (141B total, 39B active) — routing each token to 2 of 8 experts. Mixtral 8x7B weights leaked via torrent 9 Dec 2023 and formally announced 11 Dec 2023; Mixtral 8x22B weights released 10 Apr 2024 and formally announced 17 Apr 2024; both deprecated 30 Nov 2024 and retired from Mistral's hosted API 30 Mar 2025, replaced by Mistral Small 4, while open weights remain available. It succeeded technically and commercially because sparse MoE delivered near-frontier quality at roughly one-third the active compute of dense models of similar total size, under a permissive Apache 2.0 licence that drove mass fine-tuning and deployment. Its hosted retirement in 2025 was planned lifecycle supersession, not failure: Mistral's own newer Small/Large models and the wider frontier moved past it within ~15 months, so Mistral retired the hosted endpoints while leaving the weights usable.
27 Google Switch Transformer MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded A Google Brain sparse Mixture-of-Experts Transformer that routes each token to exactly one expert, scaling to 1.6 trillion parameters at near-constant per-token compute. arXiv posted 2021-01-11 (arXiv:2101.03961); published in Journal of Machine Learning Research 23(120), April 2022; no shutdown — a research architecture, not a product, so no end date. It succeeded by radical simplification: replacing noisy top-k MoE gating with top-1 routing plus an auxiliary load-balancing loss, float32 router precision, and expert capacity limits, which eliminated the training instability that had blocked trillion-parameter sparse models. The timing was ideal — it arrived just as the field hit dense-scaling cost walls — and its design was simple enough to be reimplemented everywhere, so successors (GLaM, Mixtral, DeepSeek) refined rather than replaced it.
28 Google GLaM MoE/Modular LLM Failed Google's Generalist Language Model (GLaM) is a sparsely activated mixture-of-experts language-model family whose largest model has 1.2 trillion parameters but activates only about 97 billion per token. Paper submitted 13 Dec 2021; revised 1 Aug 2022; accepted to ICML 2022; superseded by PaLM (Apr 2022) and later Gemini (Dec 2023, MoE-based from Gemini 1.5 in Feb 2024); no GLaM follow-up or shutdown notice found. GLaM proved MoE scaling worked, but it was a research-only demonstration, never productized or open-released, so it gained no users or ecosystem. Google moved its frontier effort to dense PaLM and then to multimodal Gemini, which internalized MoE ideas, leaving GLaM with no maintenance, successor, or deployment path — a technical success superseded by market timing and product strategy rather than by a flaw in sparse routing itself.
29 DeepSeek-V3 / DeepSeekMoE MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded DeepSeek-V3 is a 671B-parameter open-weights Mixture-of-Experts LLM (37B active per token) built on the DeepSeekMoE architecture, released by DeepSeek in December 2024. DeepSeekMoE paper: 11 Jan 2024; DeepSeek-V3 released: 26 Dec 2024; V3-0324 refresh (MIT licence): 25 Mar 2025; V3.1: 21 Aug 2025; V3.2: 1 Dec 2025; superseded on DeepSeek's API by V4 (24 Apr 2026), legacy V3 API names discontinued 24 Jul 2026. It succeeded technically and economically by combining fine-grained expert segmentation and shared experts (DeepSeekMoE), Multi-head Latent Attention, auxiliary-loss-free load balancing and FP8 training to match GPT-4o/Claude 3.5 Sonnet at roughly one-tenth the reported training cost, proving sparse MoE scaling. Adoption was driven by open weights, a permissive licence from V3-0324 onward, and very low API pricing, making it a default self-hosted and third-party-hosted frontier-class model. The original V3 checkpoint itself is now superseded — removed from DeepSeek's own API in 2026 in favour of V3.2/V4 — but that is succession after success, not failure: the MoE architecture and lineage remain in active use.
30 Alibaba Qwen2.5-MoE / Qwen3-MoE MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded Alibaba's Qwen mixture-of-experts LLMs — hosted-only Qwen2.5-Max in the Qwen2.5 generation, then open-weight Qwen3-30B-A3B (30B total/3B active) and Qwen3-235B-A22B (235B total/22B active, 128 experts, 8 active per token). Qwen2.5 launched 19 Sep 2024 (dense open weights); Qwen2.5-Max MoE announced 28-29 Jan 2025 (hosted/API only, weights not released); Qwen3 launched 28-29 Apr 2025 with open MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B and Qwen3-235B-A22B under Apache 2.0; Qwen3-2507 refresh 21 Jul 2025; superseded as flagship by Qwen3-Max Sep 2025 and Qwen3.5 16 Feb 2026 MoE sparse activation delivered near-flagship reasoning at roughly 22B-active inference cost, while Apache 2.0 licensing, a 0.6B-to-235B size ladder, and broad vLLM/Ollama/Hugging Face support made Qwen the default fine-tuning base. Alibaba sustained it with a fast release cadence and cloud/API monetisation of the closed Max tier, even as individual checkpoints were quickly superseded by Qwen3.5/3.6/3.8 — the success is the lineage and ecosystem, not any single checkpoint.
31 JetMoE MoE/Modular LLM Stalled/Abandoned JetMoE-8B is an open-source sparsely-gated Mixture-of-Experts LLM from MyShell and MIT that applies sparse activation to both attention heads and feedforward layers, activating only 2.2B of its 8B parameters per token. GitHub repo created 2024-04-02; arXiv paper submitted 2024-04-11 (arXiv:2404.07413); last repo push 2024-04-04; no successor release, shutdown notice, or acquisition since. It was a one-shot academic demonstration rather than a product: it proved low-cost MoE training was possible, but there was no follow-up model, commercial deployment, or maintenance roadmap. It was quickly superseded by stronger open MoE models (notably DeepSeek-MoE and later Qwen/Llama MoE variants), and its sponsor MyShell pivoted toward its AI-agent platform and SHELL token, leaving the JetMoE repo untouched since April 2024.
32 OLMoE (Allen Institute for AI) MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded OLMoE is Ai2's fully open sparse mixture-of-experts language model (7B total / ~1B active parameters per token) released with its weights, training data, code, logs, and 244 checkpoints. Paper posted Sep 3, 2024; OLMoE-1B-7B-0924 released Sep 2024 (jointly with Contextual AI); ICLR 2025 (Oral); updated OLMoE-1B-7B-0125 released Jan 2025; on-device iOS app launched Feb 11, 2025; no new OLMoE generation since Jan 2025 It succeeded as a research artifact because it was the first fully open MoE — closed rivals released weights at most, while Ai2 published data, code, logs, and every checkpoint — and because its small active size made frontier-style MoE research cheap and runnable on-device. Its ceiling was architectural and market timing: a 1B-active model was quickly outclassed by larger dense models (Ai2's own OLMo 2/3) and frontier MoEs, so it endures as a widely used research baseline and backbone rather than as an actively extended product line.
33 Databricks DBRX MoE/Modular LLM Failed Databricks' open-weight, fine-grained mixture-of-experts LLM with 132B total / 36B active parameters, released in March 2024 as a general-purpose enterprise model. MosaicML acquired by Databricks June/July 2023 for ~$1.3B; DBRX launched March 27, 2024; retired from pay-per-token April 30, 2025; retired from provisioned throughput December 19, 2025 DBRX was rapidly superseded by newer open models such as Llama 3/4 and Qwen, eroding its benchmark lead within a year, while Databricks shifted strategy from building its own foundation model to serving third-party models and building its Mosaic AI agent/platform layer. Maintaining and serving a 132B model offered little durable advantage once frontier open weights improved faster and cheaper, so Databricks retired it in two stages in 2025 rather than shipping a DBRX 2.
34 xAI Grok-1 MoE MoE/Modular LLM Failed xAI's first large language model, a 314-billion-parameter Mixture-of-Experts model whose raw base weights and architecture were open-released under Apache 2.0. Launched 3 Nov 2023; open-sourced under Apache 2.0 on 17 Mar 2024; superseded by Grok-1.5 (announced 29 Mar 2024, released 15 May 2024); GitHub repo last pushed 30 Aug 2024 Grok-1 was rapidly superseded by Grok-1.5 in spring 2024 and then by closed Grok-2/3/4 models, with no active maintenance of the Grok-1 repo since August 2024. The release was a raw base checkpoint, not fine-tuned for dialogue, and its 314B size made practical self-hosting prohibitively expensive, so newer open-weight models surpassed it for real use. xAI's strategy also pivoted away from openness: no Grok generation from Grok-3 onward has been open-released.
35 Snowflake Arctic MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded Snowflake's open-weights, Apache 2.0 enterprise LLM family built on a dense-MoE hybrid architecture (480B total parameters, 17B active per token via 128 experts), optimized for SQL generation, coding, and instruction following. Launched April 24, 2024; Arctic Embed 2.0 released December 4, 2024; Arctic-TILT published July 2025; Arctic-Text2SQL-R1 released May 29, 2025; Arctic RL work ongoing into September 2026; no shutdown or abandonment. It succeeded commercially because Snowflake embedded it in Cortex, its governed data platform serving 9,400+ organizations, rather than selling a standalone frontier model, and its Apache 2.0 openness plus published training recipes built enterprise trust. The original 480B generalist itself lost frontier relevance — a 4K context window and 224GB+ VRAM requirement made it heavy and dated versus newer models — so Snowflake pivoted the family toward small, cheap specialists (Text2SQL, Embed, TILT) where narrow models beat general-purpose giants at a fraction of the size and cost.
36 Microsoft Phi-3.5-MoE MoE/Modular LLM Failed Microsoft's open-weight Mixture-of-Experts small language model with 16 experts, 42B total parameters but only 6.6B active per token, and a 128K-token context window. Launched August 2024 (Phi-3.5 family, 20-23 Aug 2024); succeeded by Phi-4 on 12 Dec 2024; deprecated in GitHub Models on 11 Sep 2025, with users directed to Phi-4-mini. It worked technically but was superseded within four months: the dense 14B Phi-4 offered stronger reasoning in a far smaller, simpler-to-serve footprint, undermining the MoE variant's small-model value proposition despite its sparse activation. Microsoft's rapid Phi cadence then consolidated development around Phi-4 and its reasoning/multimodal variants, leaving Phi-3.5-MoE with no new releases or hosted maintenance. MoE training and inference complexity, noted by Microsoft at launch, added cost without a durable quality-per-dollar advantage over the successor.
37 Meta Llama 4 Maverick (MoE) MoE/Modular LLM Failed Meta's open-weight, natively multimodal Mixture-of-Experts LLM with 17B active parameters out of 400B total across 128 experts and a 1M-token context window. Launched April 5, 2025; no successor or meaningful update since May 2025; superseded by closed-weight Muse Spark on April 8, 2026; Groq deprecated it March 9, 2026; Meta's Llama API retired July 6, 2026; AWS Bedrock deprecated it September 30, 2026. Maverick shipped as a credible frontier open-weight model but was rapidly overtaken by DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi and Gemma rivals while Meta issued no follow-up release, so its benchmarks and hosted availability decayed. Meta then pivoted its frontier effort to closed-weight Muse Spark and reorganised around Meta Superintelligence Labs, retiring the Llama API and letting major hosts deprecate Maverick. The root cause was therefore market timing and strategic pivot rather than a single technical failure: MoE efficiency delivered a good cost/performance ratio at launch, but without sustained iteration it could not hold the frontier.
38 Tencent Hunyuan-Large (MoE) MoE/Modular LLM Failed Tencent's open-weight 389B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts language model (52B parameters activated per token), released in November 2024 as the largest open Transformer-based MoE model at the time. Paper submitted to arXiv 4 Nov 2024 (arXiv:2411.02265); open-sourced 5 Nov 2024; superseded by Hy3 preview Apr 2026 / Hy3 Jul 2026 and Hy4 preview Aug 2026 / Hy4 Nov 2026; weights remain downloadable, no formal shutdown. It succeeded at launch as a scale and efficiency proof-point, but was overtaken within two years: DeepSeek-V3 (Dec 2024) reset the open-MoE frontier, and Tencent itself rebuilt its training stack and replaced Hunyuan-Large with the cheaper, agent-optimised Hy3 and then the 770B Hy4 line. The specific checkpoint received no further releases or maintenance, so it survives only as downloadable legacy weights rather than a living model line.
39 Google Gemini MoE architecture MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded Google Gemini is a natively multimodal, sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Transformer family in which a learned router activates only a small subset of expert sub-networks per token, decoupling total model capacity from per-token compute. Gemini 1.0 launched Dec 2023; Gemini 1.5 Pro, the first public sparse-MoE Gemini, announced 15 Feb 2024 with technical report arXiv:2403.05530 on 8 Mar 2024; Gemini 2.5 in Mar 2025; Gemini 3 Pro launched 18 Nov 2025; Gemini 1.5 Pro API alias retired 24 Sep 2025, while the MoE architecture continued into Gemini 2.5/3. It succeeded technically and economically because sparse routing let Google scale total capacity, multimodality and 1M-token context without a proportional rise in per-token inference cost, making flagship capability servable at scale on TPU infrastructure. Adoption was driven less by the architecture alone than by distribution: Gemini shipped day-one in Search AI Mode, the Gemini app, Android, AI Studio and Vertex AI. The main costs are MoE-specific engineering complexity — routing/load-balancing, all-expert memory footprint and training instability — which Google absorbed with its infrastructure rather than eliminating.
40 Baidu ERNIE MoE MoE/Modular LLM In Progress Baidu's ERNIE family of sparse Mixture-of-Experts foundation models, in which a router activates only a small subset of experts per token to deliver large-model capability at much lower inference cost. ERNIE family originated 2019; ERNIE 4.5 and reasoning model ERNIE X1 launched 16 Mar 2025; ERNIE 4.5 open-sourced 30 Jun 2025; ERNIE X1.1 Sept 2025; ERNIE 5.0 previewed 13 Nov 2025 (proprietary); ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base released 5 Nov 2026; ERNIE 5.5 Flash Preview 11 Nov 2026 MoE efficiency is the technical driver: 280B total parameters with only 14B active per token makes frontier-scale models cheap to serve, and Baidu reversed its closed strategy after DeepSeek-driven price competition, open-sourcing ERNIE 4.5 while keeping ERNIE 5.0/5.1 proprietary. Adoption remains strongest in China, constrained outside it by Chinese-language documentation, mainland verification for full Qianfan access, weaker English reasoning/coding versus Western frontier models, and restrictive licensing on the newest 5.5 weights.
41 LoRA / PEFT ecosystem (Hugging Face) Specialist/Adapter Succeeded LoRA freezes a pretrained model's weights and trains only small low-rank adapter matrices, and Hugging Face PEFT is the standard open-source library implementing LoRA, QLoRA, DoRA and many other parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods. LoRA paper by Hu et al. (Microsoft Research) June 2021, published ICLR 2022; Hugging Face PEFT repo created 2022-11-25; QLoRA May 2023; PEFT v0.19.0 2026-04-14, v0.20.0 2026-07-28, v0.21.2 2026-10-01 It succeeded technically and economically: the low-rank-update insight cut trainable parameters by orders of magnitude with near-full-fine-tuning quality and no added inference latency once merged, making fine-tuning feasible on consumer/single GPUs. Standardization through Hugging Face PEFT, plus integration with Transformers, Diffusers, Accelerate and TRL and serving stacks like vLLM, made adapters portable, shareable on the Hub, and swappable at runtime. Adoption compounded because tiny adapter files (MBs vs GBs) lowered storage, distribution and multi-task serving costs, turning LoRA into the default fine-tuning recipe.
42 AdapterHub / AdapterFusion Specialist/Adapter In Progress AdapterHub is a framework and shared hub for parameter-efficient adapters that plug small trainable modules into frozen Transformers, and AdapterFusion is its two-stage method for combining multiple task-specific adapters via a learned fusion layer. Repo created 2020-04-21; AdapterHub paper EMNLP 2020 and AdapterFusion arXiv May 2020 / EACL Apr 2021; successor Adapters library paper 2023; original adapter-transformers archived and Hub migrated to Hugging Face; v1.1.0/v1.2.0 in 2025; adapters 1.3.0 on PyPI Apr 26, 2026 It survived because it was a thin add-on to Hugging Face Transformers rather than a competing platform, with a unified interface, reusable pre-trained adapters, and academic maintenance from TU Darmstadt/UKP Lab. Its ceiling was set by economics and adoption: mainstream fine-tuning consolidated around LoRA and Hugging Face PEFT, while AdapterFusion's extra fusion-training step added cost and complexity for marginal gains over simpler merging or stacking. The result is a sustained research niche, not a failed project: still maintained in 2026, but no longer the default adapter stack.
43 Mixture-of-Agents (MoA, Together AI paper) Multi-agent framework Succeeded A layered inference-time method in which multiple LLM proposers independently answer a prompt and aggregator LLMs synthesize their outputs into a single improved response, with no fine-tuning. arXiv submitted 7 Jun 2024; GitHub repo created 4 Jun 2024; Together AI blog 11 Jun 2024; published at ICLR 2025; Together docs updated 2 Nov 2026; no shutdown or pivot reported. It succeeded academically because it exploited 'collaborativeness' — models improve when conditioned on other models' outputs — achieving state-of-the-art results with only open-source models and a simple, reusable reference implementation. It did not become a standalone product because it trades compute for quality: a dozen-plus model calls per prompt, high time-to-first-token, and roughly 2x the cost of a comparable single frontier call, while later stronger single models and Self-MoA (sampling one strong model) narrowed its advantage. Its durable outcome is therefore as a proven aggregation pattern, not as a maintained platform.
44 FrugalGPT / LLM routing (Stanford) Orchestration/Enterprise Stalled/Abandoned Stanford's FrugalGPT is a cost-aware LLM cascade that tries cheap models first and escalates to expensive ones only when a learned scorer judges the answer unreliable. arXiv submitted 9 May 2023; GitHub repo created 23 Jun 2023; published in Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR) 2024; last repo push 10 Feb 2025; no shutdown/acquisition announced It was a completed research artifact, not a funded product, so maintenance stopped once the paper and 2024-2025 model evaluations were done. The cascade also needs labeled in-distribution data to train its scorer and adds latency when it escalates, while falling frontier-model prices and newer one-shot routers (e.g. RouteLLM) and commercial routing platforms superseded the specific implementation even as they preserved its core idea.
45 RouteLLM Orchestration/Enterprise Stalled/Abandoned An open-source LMSYS/UC Berkeley framework that trains lightweight routers on Chatbot Arena preference data to send each query to a cheap weak model or an expensive strong model via an OpenAI-compatible API. Repo created 3 Jun 2024; paper submitted 26 Jun 2024 (arXiv:2406.18665, revised 23 Feb 2025); LMSYS blog 1 Jul 2024; presented at ICLR 2025; last commit 10 Aug 2024; no releases or commits since. It succeeded academically but was a research project with no commercial maintainer or funding to keep retraining it. Its pretrained routers were frozen against the mid-2024 GPT-4/Mixtral model pair, so rapid model releases silently invalidated their training data, and active gateways such as LiteLLM superseded it as the maintained routing layer.
46 Sakana AI evolutionary model merging Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress An evolutionary method that automatically searches parameter-space (weights) and data-flow-space (layers) combinations of existing open-source specialist models to create new merged foundation models without gradient-based training. Sakana AI founded July 2023; Evolutionary Model Merge launched 21 Mar 2024 (arXiv 19 Mar 2024); published in Nature Machine Intelligence 28 Jan 2025; M2N2 presented at GECCO'25, July-Aug 2025; extended in ICLR 2026 AC/DC paper; no shutdown, acquisition or pivot away announced. It succeeded technically because evolutionary search (notably CMA-ES) found cross-domain merges human experts missed, at far lower compute than training from scratch, and was validated by peer review and adoption in mergekit. It has not become a standalone commercial product because it only works on open-weight models, not closed frontier API models, and each candidate merge needs costly fitness evaluation, so gains diminish against frontier models. Sakana has therefore kept the technique as an ongoing research component and lifted the same evolutionary machinery to higher layers, including collective-intelligence orchestration in 2026, rather than scaling merging alone.
47 Meta Llemma / DeepSeek-Math (math specialist models) Specialist/Adapter Succeeded Open math-specialist language models created by continued pretraining on large math corpora — Llemma (EleutherAI/Princeton, built on Meta's Code Llama, not a Meta model) and DeepSeekMath (DeepSeek, 7B, later V2) — for competition math and formal theorem proving. Llemma launched 16 Oct 2023 (arXiv rev. 15 Mar 2024); DeepSeekMath paper 5 Feb 2024, v3 27 Apr 2024; DeepSeekMath-V2 launched 27 Nov 2025; no formal shutdown for either line. Specialization worked because math has verifiable answers and proofs, so curated corpora plus RL with outcome/verifier rewards (notably GRPO) produced large, measurable gains at small scale. The standalone specialists then faded as general reasoning models absorbed the same recipe: Llemma was left as a frozen 2023-2024 research artifact, while DeepSeek folded math into its larger R1/V3 reasoning line and revived the name only for the much larger self-verifying V2 in 2025.
48 DeepMind AlphaFold 2/3 Specialist/Adapter Succeeded Google DeepMind's AI system that predicts 3D protein structures from amino-acid sequence (AlphaFold 2) and, in AlphaFold 3, the joint structures of proteins with DNA, RNA, small-molecule ligands and ions. AlphaFold 1 won CASP13 Dec 2018; AlphaFold 2 won CASP14 30 Nov 2020 (median GDT 92.4), Nature paper and open code/database 15 Jul 2021, database expanded to >200 million structures Jul 2022; AlphaFold 3 published in Nature 8 May 2024, code released 11 Nov 2024; Nobel Prize in Chemistry 9 Oct 2024; dedicated AlphaFold team reported disbanded 30 Jul 2026; AlphaFold DB update Oct 2026. It succeeded technically by solving a narrowly scoped, 50-year grand challenge with a clear blind benchmark (CASP), using an end-to-end Evoformer (AF2) / Pairformer-plus-diffusion (AF3) architecture trained on the Protein Data Bank, and by shipping the result as a free, open database and (for AF2) open code, creating massive academic adoption. Commercialisation via Isomorphic Labs and Alphabet/EMBL-EBI sustainment funded it, but AlphaFold 3's initial code/weights restriction drew a reproducibility backlash and spawned fully open rivals, and in 2026 Google reprioritised Gemini over dedicated AlphaFold research, dissolving the original team even as the database and models remain widely used.
49 Google Med-PaLM / MedGemma Specialist/Adapter In Progress Google's medical specialist LLM lineage — Med-PaLM/Med-PaLM 2 as closed research models, succeeded by MedGemma, an open-weight Gemma 3-based family (4B multimodal and 27B text/multimodal) for medical text and image comprehension. Med-PaLM arXiv 26 Dec 2022, Nature 12 Jul 2023; Med-PaLM 2 announced Mar 2023, arXiv 16 May 2023; MedGemma launched 20 May 2025 (4B multimodal + 27B text-only), 27B multimodal 9 Jul 2025; MedLM deprecated 14 May 2025, access ended 29 Sep 2025; MedGemma 1.5 4B released 13 Jan 2026; MedGemma paper in Nature Medicine 6 Oct 2026 Technically it succeeded on benchmarks, reaching expert-level MedQA scores, but clinical adoption was blocked by safety, validation and regulatory barriers — Google explicitly says MedGemma is not clinical-grade. Economically, the closed, large PaLM 2-based MedLM API was superseded: frontier general models caught up or passed it on MedQA, so Google pivoted to smaller, cheaper, open-weight Gemma-based models that hospitals and developers can run and fine-tune locally. The specialist advantage therefore shifted from a proprietary API to an open, deployable adapter rather than disappearing.
50 StarCoder / specialist code models Specialist/Adapter Stalled/Abandoned StarCoder is BigCode's (Hugging Face + ServiceNow, later NVIDIA) family of open-weights specialist large language models for code generation and completion, trained on the permissively-licensed The Stack dataset. BigCode founded Sep 2022; StarCoder/StarCoderBase launched May 4 2023; StarCoder2 launched Feb 28 2024 (instruct variant Apr 2024); The Stack v3 released Jul 23 2026; no StarCoder3 or new StarCoder model release as of Nov 2026 The model line succeeded as a 2023-2024 open code model but froze after StarCoder2: its 16K context and base-model (non-agentic) design were outpaced by Qwen2.5-Coder and DeepSeek-Coder, which offered 128K context, much higher HumanEval scores and more permissive licensing than StarCoder's OpenRAIL-M. BigCode/Hugging Face effort shifted to the underlying data and evaluation assets — The Stack v3 in July 2026 and BigCodeBench — rather than training a StarCoder3, leaving the models usable for low-latency completion but with no meaningful model release in over 32 months and no formal shutdown.
51 Hugging Face model hub specialist-model ecosystem Specialist/Adapter Succeeded A git-based open hub hosting millions of task- and domain-specialized fine-tuned models and lightweight PEFT/LoRA adapters, plus datasets and demo Spaces, all loadable through a standard Transformers interface. Founded 2016 as a chatbot, pivoted to open NLP/Transformers; Hub passed 1M public models Sep 2024, 2M Aug 2025, 3M Aug 2026; Series D Aug 2023 ($235M at $4.5B valuation); NVIDIA agreed to acquire Hugging Face for $12.93B on Sep 3, 2026, expected to close H1 2027 pending regulatory approval. It succeeded through standardization and network effects: one-line loading, safetensors, and tiny swappable PEFT adapters collapsed the cost of publishing and reusing a specialist model, while git-based versioning, search, and an open, vendor-neutral registry attracted every major lab and independent builder. The weakness is discoverability and concentration — the top 200 models account for ~49.6% of downloads while ~85.6% of models have fewer than 200 lifetime downloads — and neutrality is now the open risk, with NVIDIA ownership pending and a reported DOJ antitrust inquiry in Oct 2026.
52 Modular Deep Learning / Mixture-of-Adapters (MoA) MoE/Modular LLM In Progress A research programme in which a frozen foundation model is adapted by composing small, swappable specialist adapter modules — Mixture-of-Adapters (MoA) routes tokens to heterogeneous LoRA, parallel-adapter and prompt-tuning experts instead of retraining the whole model. Feb 2023: 'Modular Deep Learning' survey by Pfeiffer, Ruder, Vulic and Ponti (arXiv:2302.11529); Dec 2023: Adapters unified library (EMNLP 2023); Mar 2024/2025: SEMA mixture-of-adapters for continual learning (CVPR 2025); Apr-May 2025: SMOA (NAACL 2025); 6 Jun 2025: heterogeneous MoA preprint (Zhejiang University/Tencent); 2-7 Jul 2026: MoA published at ACL 2026 in San Diego. It stays in progress rather than succeeding as a product because the core technical problem is real but unsolved in general form: homogeneous MoE-LoRA experts suffer representation collapse and load imbalance, and each fix (heterogeneous MoA, SEMA, SMOA, DM-Adapter, L-MoE) is a narrow, benchmark-specific variant with its own router and composition rules. Adoption is therefore academic and fragmented — many papers, no dominant standard, library or deployed system — so progress continues through successive papers rather than through a shipped, sustained implementation.
53 OpenCog Hyperon / SingularityNET Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress OpenCog Hyperon is SingularityNET's open-source neural-symbolic cognitive architecture for AGI, built around the AtomSpace knowledge metagraph and the MeTTa programming language, with SingularityNET as its decentralized AI marketplace and funding/coordination layer. Founded/launched December 2017 (SingularityNET, $36M ICO); OpenCog Hyperon Alpha April 2024; March-July 2024 ASI Alliance token merger with Fetch.ai and Ocean Protocol (AGIX/FET/OCEAN -> ASI); Hyperon Alpha 1 and ASI:Chain DevNet November 2025; roadmap targets human-level AGI by 2028 (unverified). It survives on sustained token-funded research rather than product revenue: the 2017 ICO, the 2024 ASI Alliance merger, and a $53M infrastructure commitment keep a small research community building, but progress is slow because the ambition is maximal (human-level AGI by 2028) while the stack is fragmented across parallel MeTTa runtimes and the AtomSpace-Scheme legacy, and the marketplace has not produced a breakout commercial application. The root cause is coordination overhead and market timing — a bespoke language and cognitive architecture must mature before adoption, while mainstream LLM agent frameworks ship usable products today.
54 SOAR cognitive architecture Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress A symbolic cognitive architecture for general intelligent agents, built around a repeating decision cycle that selects and applies operators using working, procedural, semantic and episodic memory, with chunking and reinforcement learning. Begun 1983 as John Laird's thesis at Carnegie Mellon University, created by Laird, Allen Newell and Paul Rosenbloom; moved to University of Michigan (Laird joined 1986); definitive book published 2012; v9.6.4 released 2025-07-22; v9.6.5 released 2026-05-06; no shutdown, acquisition or pivot found. It has survived 40+ years because its fixed decision cycle, impasse handling and distinct memory systems provide a principled, inspectable substrate for planning, learning and real-time control, sustained by academic and government-funded research at the University of Michigan. It never achieved mass commercial adoption because knowledge must largely be hand-authored as production rules, onboarding is steep and tooling such as VisualSoar is aging, while LLM-based agents lowered the entry cost for most applications. Its current adaptation is LLM-modulo integration — using LLMs for language and rule generation while Soar retains control, memory and verification — rather than competing with LLMs directly.
55 ACT-R (Adaptive Control of Thought—Rational) Cognitive architecture/AGI Succeeded A symbolic cognitive architecture from Carnegie Mellon that models human cognition as interacting modules (declarative and procedural memory, perception, motor) coordinated through buffers and production rules, implemented as a Common Lisp interpreter. Roots in Anderson's HAM model (1973) and ACT theory; renamed ACT-R with rational analysis in the early 1990s; ACT-R 4.0 released 1993 (with Christian Lebiere); ACT-R 7.0 from 2015; stable release 7.21.6 on 2020-12-21; annual workshops held continuously since 1995, including the 32nd (July 2025), a special ecosystem workshop (May 2026), and the 33rd workshop (July 2026). It succeeded academically because it is an empirically validated, quantitative theory of human cognition — its ~50ms production cycle and activation-based declarative memory make falsifiable predictions about reaction times, errors, and even fMRI BOLD patterns — which kept it funded and cited for three decades. Its ceiling is engineering adoption: the Lisp implementation, steep learning curve, and human-fidelity orientation make it hard to repurpose as a general AI substrate, which is exactly why the May 2026 workshop focused on ecosystem sustainability, integration, and reusability in the LLM era.
56 CLARION cognitive architecture Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress CLARION (Connectionist Learning with Adaptive Rule Induction On-line) is Ron Sun's hybrid cognitive architecture in which every subsystem — action-centered, non-action-centered, motivational, and metacognitive — has an explicit symbolic top level and an implicit connectionist bottom level that interact via bottom-up and top-down learning. Proposed by Ron Sun in the mid-1990s (Sun 1994/1995); book 'Duality of the Mind' 2002; CLARION 5.0 specification 2003; 'Anatomy of the Mind' (Oxford) 2016; LLM-integration papers 2024-2025; pyClarion / ICAART agent paper 2026 It survived as an academic theory because its implicit/explicit distinction maps well onto human psychology and it was validated against many lab tasks, but it never became a widely used engineering platform: the original Java/C++ implementation aged, the community stayed small, and its rule extraction relied on simple MLPs. The LLM era bypassed hand-built architectures, so recent work is grafting LLMs onto CLARION as the implicit layer rather than scaling CLARION itself.
57 LIDA (Learning Intelligent Decision Agent) Cognitive architecture/AGI Stalled/Abandoned LIDA is a University of Memphis cognitive architecture by Stan Franklin and colleagues that implements Bernard Baars' Global Workspace Theory as repeating ~10 Hz cognitive cycles of perception, competitive attention/broadcast, and action selection with learning across multiple memory systems. IDA developed for the US Navy in the late 1990s; LIDA formalised in 2006 (Franklin & Patterson) and in Baars & Franklin 2009; Franklin died 23 January 2023; last substantive spin-off activity was the neuro-symbolic LIDA paper first published online 6 August 2025 (AGI 2025). Its founder Stan Franklin died in January 2023 and the core Java framework was not actively maintained afterwards, leaving a research prototype that never shipped as a product. Its hand-coded codelets, non-standard non-commercial licence and contributor agreement deterred open-source adoption, while the LLM era superseded its labour-intensive, unified-theory-first approach with narrower, shippable agent systems.
58 Sigma cognitive architecture Cognitive architecture/AGI Stalled/Abandoned Sigma is Paul Rosenbloom's USC Institute for Creative Technologies cognitive architecture that unifies symbolic, probabilistic, and neural cognition — memory, reasoning, learning, perception, and action — in a single factor-graph substrate using summary-product message passing and gradient-descent learning. 2009: first Sigma papers; 2013: AISB Quarterly introduction; 2016: definitive JAGI paper (Rosenbloom, Demski & Ustun); 2019: last Sigma-specific papers listed by USC; 2020-04-26: PySigma created; 2024-02-02: last PySigma push; 2025: Rosenbloom's retrospective memoir 'In Search of Insight' and Professor Emeritus status (Sept 2025) — no new Sigma release. Its single-substrate elegance was also its bottleneck: everything had to go through the same message-passing machinery, making specialised fast paths and practical engineering hard, while the original Lisp system and its incomplete Python 3.7-era PyTorch port never became a usable product. A small academic team, aimed at virtual-human research rather than a commercial or open-source community, could not sustain it, and the LLM era then absorbed attention and funding that might have carried a graphical-architecture revival.
59 NARS (Non-Axiomatic Reasoning System) Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress A general-purpose AGI cognitive architecture by Pei Wang that reasons with Non-Axiomatic Logic (NAL) in the language Narsese, learning and acting under insufficient knowledge and resources. Invented mid-1980s by Pei Wang; PhD dissertation 1995 (Indiana University); NARS 4.1 in 2000; OpenNARS open-source implementations thereafter; ONA v0.9.3 on 22 April 2025; NARS Workshop at AGI-25 in August 2025; Non-Axiomatic Logic 2nd edition published 2025; no shutdown or acquisition. After 30+ years it remains an academically sustained research project rather than a product: its symbolic term-logic approach never achieved LLM-scale performance or a breakout benchmark, and funding has been academic/grants-level rather than commercial. Adoption is limited to a small research community and niche robotics demos, so progress is steady but slow — code releases paused after May 2025 while theory and papers continued into 2026.
60 LeCun JEPA / Meta V-JEPA world model Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress A self-supervised world-model architecture proposed by Yann LeCun in which an encoder and predictor forecast future observations in abstract latent representation space instead of generating pixels or tokens, embodied in Meta's open V-JEPA 2 video model and its action-conditioned robot-planning variant V-JEPA 2-AC. June 2022: LeCun proposes JEPA in 'A Path Towards Autonomous Machine Intelligence'; June 2023: I-JEPA; Feb 2024: V-JEPA; June 11, 2025: V-JEPA 2 released (arXiv:2506.09985); Nov 2025: LeCun leaves Meta; March 2026: V-JEPA 2.1 released and AMI Labs announces $1.03B seed round JEPA remains a research program, not a product: it is technically promising — a 1.2B-parameter model beats far larger pixel-generating models on motion understanding and robot planning with far less data — but physical reasoning is still near chance on Meta's own benchmarks, long-horizon planning degrades, and robot control needs a goal image rather than language instructions. Its survival is driven by open weights, strong academic adoption, and massive funding: LeCun's AMI Labs raised $1.03B in March 2026 specifically to continue JEPA, and Meta kept shipping follow-ups (V-JEPA 2.1) after his departure. The risk is market timing — LLMs dominate commercial AI while JEPA has no shipped product — so its payoff depends on sustained research funding rather than near-term revenue.
61 DeepMind AlphaZero / MuZero Cognitive architecture/AGI Succeeded A pair of DeepMind general reinforcement-learning systems that master games by self-play plus Monte Carlo tree search, with MuZero planning using a learned model of the environment instead of being given the rules. AlphaZero: arXiv preprint Dec 2017, peer-reviewed in Science Dec 2018; MuZero: arXiv preprint 19 Nov 2019, Nature paper Dec 2020; MuZero in production for YouTube VP9 compression Feb 2022; AlphaDev (AlphaZero descendant) faster sorting/hashing algorithms announced Jun 2023; no shutdown — lineage continues via variants (MuZero Unplugged, Sampled/Gumbel/Stochastic MuZero) and DeepMind's mctx library. It succeeded because a single general recipe — self-play reinforcement learning plus search — replaced game-specific engineering, so the same algorithm transferred across games and then to real optimization problems (video compression, chip design, algorithms). MuZero's learned latent model removed the last hand-coded dependency (the rules/simulator), making planning applicable to messy real-world settings where dynamics are unknown. The lineage stayed alive because DeepMind kept generalizing it (AlphaDev, MuZero variants) rather than shipping it as a fixed product, though it was never a standalone commercial product itself.
62 IBM Watson (Jeopardy / Watson Health) Orchestration/Enterprise Failed IBM Watson was a DeepQA natural-language question-answering system that beat Jeopardy! champions in 2011 and was then commercialised as Watson Health, an enterprise clinical-decision-support and health-data platform. 2004-2006 conceived/developed (DeepQA); Feb 14-16, 2011 Jeopardy win; Feb 2013 first commercial use (lung cancer, Memorial Sloan Kettering); Apr 2015 Watson Health launched; 2017 MD Anderson project cancelled; Jan 21, 2022 sale to Francisco Partners announced, closed Jun 30, 2022 and rebranded Merative; May 2023 watsonx successor launched Technically, DeepQA's retrieval-and-ranking approach excelled at well-formed quiz clues but did not generalise to messy, heterogeneous clinical records, and Watson for Oncology was trained largely on synthetic/hypothetical cases, producing unsafe or incorrect recommendations. Economically and organisationally, IBM oversold the Jeopardy demo as near-ready cancer care while spending over $4B on acquisitions it never integrated into a working product, and hospitals found the system mismatched to real workflows and EHRs. The result was cancelled flagship partnerships, loss of clinical trust, and a fire-sale exit at roughly a quarter of acquisition cost, with IBM pivoting to the watsonx foundation-model platform instead.
63 Cyc / Cycorp Cognitive architecture/AGI Stalled/Abandoned Cyc is a hand-built, logic-based commonsense ontology and reasoning engine developed by Cycorp to let AI reason like a human. Begun July 1984 at MCC; spun out as Cycorp, Inc. end of 1994; OpenCyc released 2002, withdrawn March 2017; last stable release 6.1, 27 November 2017; founder Douglas Lenat died 31 August 2023 Hand-encoding commonsense never scaled: estimates of 'enough' knowledge kept rising from 250,000 rules to 24.5 million assertions with no self-learning takeoff, at enormous labour cost. Statistical LLMs then answered commonsense questions far more cheaply, superseding Cyc as a general-intelligence path, so Cycorp withdrew its open version in 2017 and retreated to low-profile niche enterprise and hospital products, with its founder's death in 2023 removing its driving force.
64 Wolfram Alpha / Wolfram Language symbolic AI Specialist/Adapter Succeeded A computational knowledge engine and symbolic programming language that computes exact answers from curated data and formal rules instead of predicting text like an LLM. Mathematica launched 23 June 1988; Wolfram|Alpha launched 18 May 2009; Wolfram|Alpha Pro launched 8 February 2012; Wolfram Language named in 2013 and formally announced June 2014; ChatGPT plugin launched 23 March 2023, replaced by Wolfram GPT after plugins retired April 2024; Wolfram Language 14.3 released August 2025; Version 15 released June 2026 and 15.0.1 in July 2026. Technically it survived because deterministic symbolic computation plus curated data solves exactly what LLMs get wrong — arithmetic, symbolic math, and factual lookup — making it a trustworthy verification/tool layer rather than a competing chatbot. Economically it was sustained by diversified Mathematica licensing, Pro subscriptions, and API/enterprise revenue as a private, debt-free, VC-free company, insulating it from ad-market and investor-exit pressure. Its mass-consumer ceiling came from adoption limits: a rigid natural-language parser, a proprietary language rarely used by general developers, and a narrow answer-engine scope versus general search and conversational AI, so it never became a Google killer but endured as a specialist backend.
