65 OpenAI o1/o3 reasoning models Cognitive architecture/AGI Succeeded OpenAI's o-series of closed large language models trained with large-scale reinforcement learning to reason via extended internal chain-of-thought — and, in o3, to call tools — before answering. o1-preview/o1-mini launched 12 Sep 2024; full o1 and ChatGPT Pro launched 5 Dec 2024, o1 API 17 Dec 2024; o3 previewed 20 Dec 2024, o3-mini 31 Jan 2025, full o3 and o4-mini 16 Apr 2025, o3-pro 10 Jun 2025; GPT-5 unified GPT/o-series 7 Aug 2025; o1 API shut down 23 Oct 2026; o3 API shutdown scheduled 11 Dec 2026 It succeeded technically because scaling test-time compute via RL chain-of-thought produced large, verifiable jumps on math, coding and science benchmarks over GPT-4o, establishing reasoning models as a product category that rivals copied. It was nevertheless superseded as a standalone line because separate o-series models were slower, costlier and harder to route than general models, so OpenAI folded the capability into GPT-5's unified router/thinking system in Aug 2025 and retired the o1/o3 SKUs in 2026 — a consolidation, not a failure of the reasoning approach itself.
66 Google DeepMind AlphaEvolve Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Succeeded Google DeepMind's evolutionary coding agent that pairs Gemini Flash/Pro models with automated evaluators to iteratively discover and optimize algorithms expressed as code. Announced 14 May 2025; arXiv white paper 16 Jun 2025; Google Cloud private preview 9 Dec 2025; one-year impact update May 2026; general availability on Google Cloud 9 Jul 2026 It succeeded because every candidate is machine-verifiable, so the system can run thousands of generations without human judgement in the loop — the evaluator, not the LLM, is the source of truth. It also had a built-in first customer: Google itself, where verified wins (Borg scheduling, Gemini kernels, TPU circuits) were deployed in production before external launch, proving economic value. Its main limitation is scope, not failure: it only works on problems with a fast, reliable automatic score, and the core remains closed-source and paid, so outside reproduction is limited.
67 Intel Loihi 2 Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress Intel Loihi 2 is a second-generation digital neuromorphic research chip that runs asynchronous spiking neural networks with on-chip learning for ultra-low-power, event-driven edge workloads. Launched/announced 30 Sep 2021; Hala Point announced 17 Apr 2024; Lava framework archived 13 May 2026; commercialization phase announced 18 Sep 2026; no verified Loihi 3 launch date or specs from Intel as of Nov 2026. Loihi 2 delivers dramatic efficiency on sparse, event-driven workloads (orders of magnitude vs. conventional hardware on narrow benchmarks), but its bespoke spiking-neural-network programming model and Lava toolchain never reached mainstream ML developers, and the chip remained research-access-only with no commercial product. Intel is therefore pivoting the program from open research toward commercialization and a next-generation chip/SDK built on open-standard AI frameworks — the hardware line continues, but the open Loihi 2 research platform is effectively being wound down, as shown by Lava's May 2026 archiving.
68 IBM TrueNorth / NorthPole Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress IBM's brain-inspired chip lineage: TrueNorth (2014) was a spiking neuromorphic chip with 1M neurons, superseded by NorthPole (2023), a synchronous digital neural-inference chip that co-locates memory and compute on-die to eliminate off-chip memory access. 2008: DARPA SyNAPSE programme begins; Aug 2014: TrueNorth unveiled/published in Science; 2016: TrueNorth end-to-end ecosystem; 20 Oct 2023: NorthPole published in Science; Sept-Oct 2024: NorthPole 3B LLM inference results; Nov 2025: 288-card NorthPole LLM system (arXiv 2511.15950); Air Force contract to 2027 TrueNorth never commercialised: its event-driven spiking design and bespoke programming language/libraries had no backward compatibility with C++/standard deep-learning tooling, creating vendor lock-in and a weak software ecosystem. NorthPole pivoted to conventional DNN/LLM inference with a compiler-mapped, synchronous design, achieving large efficiency wins on narrow benchmarks, but it remains constrained by on-chip SRAM — it cannot hold frontier-scale models (Modha: 'We can't run GPT-4 on this') — so it wins only in low-latency, low-batch inference and has not yet become a general commercial product, surviving on research and defence funding.
69 SpiNNaker / SpiNNaker2 Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress A massively parallel, brain-inspired neuromorphic supercomputer architecture from the University of Manchester (SpiNNaker1, 1M ARM cores) and its commercial second generation SpiNNaker2 from TU Dresden spinout SpiNNcloud, using many small event-driven cores to simulate spiking neural networks. Project began 2005; first chips 2009; million-core milestone 14 Oct 2018; SpiNNaker released 2019; €8M grant for SpiNNaker2 announced 24 Sep 2019; SpiNNcloud Systems founded 2021 (TU Dresden spinout); SpiNNaker2 commercially available May 2024; Dresden system and Sandia NERL Braunfels operational/delivered spring 2025; UTSA THOR opened Feb 2026; Jülich ESAB launched 2026; SpiNNext EIC project Dec 2025-Nov 2027 It survived where many neuromorphic efforts stalled because it stayed anchored to funded research use — the EU Human Brain Project, then national labs (Sandia, Jülich) and universities — rather than chasing mainstream AI workloads it cannot yet win. Its limitation is economics and adoption: it has no independently audited benchmark against data-centre GPUs, no commercial generative-AI customer, and its customers are almost entirely publicly funded research institutions, so commercial scale remains unproven. Continued EU/NSF funding (notably the €10M EIC award for SpiNNext) keeps development active in 2025-2026, but the technology is still a research platform transitioning to product, not a widely adopted computing paradigm.
70 BrainScaleS-2 (Heidelberg) Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress An analog mixed-signal neuromorphic research platform from Heidelberg University's Electronic Vision(s) group that emulates spiking neural networks in physical circuits 1,000x faster than biology, freely accessible to researchers via the EBRAINS infrastructure. Prototypes publicly available 2017 (Human Brain Project); single-chip access via EBRAINS Collab June 2020, PyNN access March 2021; core system paper Frontiers in Neuroscience 2022; public research platform since 2023; multi-chip system paper Dec 2025; three NICE 2026 papers Mar 2026; EBRAINS tutorials Oct 2026 It survives as a publicly funded research platform, not a product: analog hardware is hard to scale (only 512 neurons per chip, with multi-chip scaling only arriving in 2025-2026) and analog neuron calibration is notoriously fiddly, so adoption stays confined to neuroscience and neuromorphic-ML researchers rather than mainstream AI. Its continuity depends on EU public funding — the Human Brain Project ended in 2023 and EBRAINS 2.0 now carries it — so its economics are grant cycles, not customers. The lesson is that exotic hardware wins only when wrapped in boring, standard software interfaces (PyTorch/PyNN) and shared access, which is exactly what kept it alive.
71 DARPA SyNAPSE (Systems of Neuromorphic Adaptive Plastic Scalable Electronics) Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm Failed A DARPA programme (2008-c.2016) to build brain-inspired, low-power neuromorphic computers scaling toward biological levels, whose flagship output was IBM's TrueNorth neurosynaptic chip. Launched 2008 (initial $4.9M IBM / $5.9M HRL awards, Nov 2008); TrueNorth unveiled 7 Aug 2014; LLNL 16-chip system announced 29 Mar 2016; programme scheduled to run to c.2015-2016 and then ended, with TrueNorth now End of Life. SyNAPSE was a laboratory success but a platform failure: TrueNorth proved million-neuron spiking hardware at milliwatts, yet it had no on-chip learning, required a wholly new spiking-neural-network programming model, and arrived just as GPUs and conventional deep learning won on software, benchmarks and economics. With no general-purpose workload, no commercial product and no successor chip, it was superseded and left with no active maintenance, despite seeding later neuromorphic work such as Intel Loihi.
72 Harvard Kilobot swarm robotics Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm Succeeded A Harvard (Radhika Nagpal lab) swarm of 1,024 tiny, ~$15, 3.3 cm robots that self-assemble into shapes using only simple local rules and infrared neighbor communication, with no central control. Development began Nov 2010; first introduced 2011 (25 units); 100 units by 2013; 1,024-robot demonstration and Science paper 14-15 Aug 2014; licensed to K-Team; still in active research use in 2025-2026, no shutdown It succeeded as a research platform because radical cost reduction (~$15/unit vs. expensive individual robots) plus open-source hardware/software made thousand-robot experiments affordable and reproducible for labs worldwide, and simple decentralized rules with built-in error correction overcame each unit's unreliability. Its limit was commercial: individual Kilobots are slow, myopic, and imprecise, so it remained an academic testbed rather than a deployed product — but that was its intended role, and it still defines the field's standard platform.
73 Numenta HTM / Thousand Brains Theory Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress A neuroscience-first AI research program from Numenta, now the Thousand Brains Project nonprofit, building Monty — an open-source sensorimotor learning system in which many cortical-column-like modules build reference-frame models of objects through movement and vote to reach consensus. Numenta founded 2005; NuPIC/HTM open-sourced 2013; Jeff Hawkins published 'A Thousand Brains' in 2021; Thousand Brains Project announced June 5, 2024 and incorporated as a nonprofit October 23, 2024; Monty v0.47.0 released August 2026; Two-Year Report published September 24, 2026. It remains research-stage rather than commercially adopted because it deliberately rejects the transformer/scale paradigm, so it lacks the ecosystem, benchmarks, and tooling that drive mainstream adoption, and its validation is still largely simulated 3D object recognition rather than broad real-world tasks. Progress is sustained by nonprofit and foundation funding (including Gates) rather than product revenue, which keeps development active but slow and narrowly focused. The original HTM/NuPIC stack is in maintenance mode, with momentum concentrated in Monty and a small community.
74 Cortical Labs DishBrain Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm In Progress DishBrain is Cortical Labs' 2022 system of ~800,000 living human and mouse cortical neurons grown on a microelectrode array and taught to play Pong via closed-loop electrical feedback, later commercialised as the CL1 biological computer. Founded 2019; DishBrain Neuron paper Oct/Dec 2022; $10M raise April 2023; CL1 announced March 2025, shipped summer 2025; Doom demo Feb 2026; 120-unit Melbourne facility March 2026; 20-unit Singapore prototype unveiled Aug 6/announced Aug 17, 2026 It remains in progress rather than succeeded because, despite shipping a real product and expanding to prototype data-centre racks in 2026, it has not demonstrated wide adoption or a commercial workload advantage over silicon. The core constraints are biological: cultures live only months, need life support, and are hard to scale, standardise and replicate, while the original learning claims remain contested and unreplicated outside the company and its collaborators. Continued venture funding, government grants and high-profile demos have sustained development, but economics and verifiable performance — not just novelty — will decide whether it becomes a sustained computing platform or remains a research instrument.
75 SynSense Speck Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm Succeeded SynSense Speck is an event-driven neuromorphic vision SoC integrating a 128x128 dynamic vision sensor with a ~330,000-neuron spiking CNN processor for sub-milliwatt, always-on edge vision. SynSense founded 2017; Speck/Xylo dev kits launched Dec 2022; ~$10M pre-B+ round Mar 2023 for Speck mass production; SynSense-iniVation merger Feb 2024; Nature Communications paper 2024; Sinabs 3.1.0 Oct 2025; Series B2 $14.8M 30 Jun 2026 It succeeded technically by fusing sensing and spiking compute on one asynchronous chip, eliminating frame-based power waste and achieving verified sub-milliwatt operation. Commercially it survived by staying narrowly scoped to always-on gesture, tracking, and eye-tracking niches and raising sustained venture funding ($101M total) rather than chasing general-purpose AI. Its ceiling is adoption, not physics: low 128x128 resolution, inference-only operation, and a vendor-specific Sinabs toolchain keep it a specialist edge part rather than a broad platform.
76 MemGPT / Letta Memory/Verification/Self-improvement In Progress An open-source platform (Apache 2.0) for stateful AI agents whose LLM manages its own tiered, persistent memory, now shipped as the Letta Code agent harness with git-backed MemFS memory. MemGPT paper posted 12 Oct 2023; repo created 11 Oct 2023; rebranded MemGPT to Letta and $10M seed announced 23-24 Sep 2024; Letta Code introduced 16 Dec 2025 and made flagship 16 Mar 2026; Letta V1 Python server archived/retired 16 Aug 2026. The original virtual-context-management idea proved influential (1,000+ paper citations in under a year) but memory management adds extra LLM calls and cost, and the first Python API server was superseded rather than sustained. Letta pivoted in 2026 to a crowded coding-agent harness (Letta Code), where it competes with better-funded memory rivals such as mem0, and it remained at seed funding into mid-2026, slowing commercial scale despite sustained open-source activity.
77 Reflexion (Shinn et al.) Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Succeeded A framework in which a language agent improves across trials without weight updates by verbally reflecting on task feedback and storing those reflections in an episodic memory buffer that conditions its next attempt. Submitted 20 Mar 2023 (arXiv v1); last revised 10 Oct 2023 (v4); published NeurIPS 2023; GitHub repo created 22 Mar 2023; last repo push 14 Jan 2025; no shutdown or pivot announced. It succeeded academically because verbal reinforcement was a cheap, training-free way to convert sparse pass/fail signals into actionable lessons, and its Actor-Evaluator-Self-Reflection pattern was simple enough to be absorbed into LangGraph, LangChain and other frameworks. The original repo itself is now dormant because it was a paper artifact, not a maintained product — no team or funding sustained it, and its gains depend on a reliable evaluator (unit tests/environment reward) and costly multi-trial GPT-4 calls, failing outright on WebShop and underperforming on MBPP where self-written tests were unreliable. Its influence therefore outlived its codebase, which is the expected lifecycle for a research pattern rather than a failure of the idea.
78 Self-Refine Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Succeeded A training-free method in which a single LLM generates a draft, critiques its own output, and iteratively refines it using that feedback. arXiv submitted 30 Mar 2023, revised 25 May 2023; GitHub repo created 28 Mar 2023; published at NeurIPS 2023, 10-16 Dec 2023; last repo README update Nov 2023; no shutdown or pivot. It succeeded academically because it was simple, required no training data, RL, or second model, and delivered large measured gains, making it easy to adopt as a baseline pattern. Its ceiling was the reliability of self-feedback: later work showed LLMs often cannot reliably self-correct reasoning or factual errors and can regress correct answers, so successors such as CRITIC grounded critique in external tools. The original code repo froze after the paper, but the idea survived and was absorbed into mainstream evaluator-optimizer loops rather than failing.
79 Voyager Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Stalled/Abandoned Voyager is the first LLM-powered embodied lifelong-learning agent in Minecraft that autonomously explores, writes and self-verifies executable JavaScript skills via GPT-4, and stores them in a growing reusable skill library without fine-tuning. arXiv posted 25 May 2023, revised 19 Oct 2023; presented NeurIPS 2023 and published in TMLR March 2024; GitHub repo created 25 May 2023, last commits July 2023; no releases, no formal shutdown Voyager was a research prototype, not a product: it was tightly coupled to GPT-4 API cost and latency, Minecraft 1.19/Mineflayer, and a fixed 2023 stack, with no commercial path. Its authors moved on to successor embodied-AI work (Eureka, then GR00T), so the original codebase stopped being maintained even though the skill-library idea remained highly influential academically.
80 Generative Agents / Stanford Smallville Multi-agent framework Succeeded A 2023 Stanford/Google research system in which 25 LLM-driven agents live in a Sims-like town called Smallville, remembering, reflecting on, and planning their days, producing emergent social behaviour such as organising a Valentine's Day party from a single seed instruction. Submitted 7 Apr 2023; revised 6 Aug 2023; presented UIST '23, 29 Oct-1 Nov 2023; code released on GitHub 23 Jul 2023, last commits Aug 2023; follow-up 'Generative Agent Simulations of 1,000 People' Nov 2024; Simile Series A 12 Feb 2026, Series B 30 Jul 2026. It succeeded because its simple, legible architecture — a natural-language memory stream scored by recency, importance and relevance, plus periodic reflection and top-down planning — made believable multi-agent behaviour reproducible and became the canonical reference for agent memory and social simulation. The original Smallville code itself was never productised: a 25-agent, two-day run cost thousands of dollars in 2023 tokens and took days, with fragile retrieval and spatial errors. Its value therefore migrated to the grounded-person follow-up research and to Simile, the authors' enterprise simulation company, rather than to continued maintenance of the demo repo.
81 STaR (Self-Taught Reasoner) Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Succeeded A 2022 Stanford method that bootstraps LLM reasoning by iteratively generating chain-of-thought rationales, rationalizing wrong answers using the known correct answer, and fine-tuning only on rationales that yield correct answers. arXiv 28 Mar 2022 (v2 20 May 2022); NeurIPS 2022 (Dec 2022); GitHub repo created 3 Oct 2022; successors Quiet-STaR Mar 2024 and V-STaR Feb 2024; no further repo activity after 2022. Technically it worked and was simple: it needed only a few seed rationales plus questions with known answers, and it demonstrably closed most of the gap to a 30x larger model. Its limits — dependence on ground-truth labels, need for initial above-chance reasoning ability, and rationalization that can reverse-engineer answers without true reasoning — meant the standalone JAX/TPU codebase froze after 2022 while the idea was absorbed and superseded by verifier-augmented variants (V-STaR, Quiet-STaR) and large-scale RLVR/GRPO training. It succeeded as a foundational technique rather than as a maintained product.
82 AlphaCode / AlphaCode 2 (DeepMind) Specialist/Adapter Succeeded DeepMind's competitive-programming code-generation system that samples many candidate programs from a fine-tuned transformer (Gemini Pro in AlphaCode 2) and filters, clusters, and reranks them by executing tests to submit only the best few. Announced 2 Feb 2022; published in Science 8 Dec 2022; AlphaCode 2 technical report released 6 Dec 2023 alongside Gemini; no standalone product launch or shutdown notice since. It succeeded as a research milestone because massive sampling plus execution-based filtering works on well-specified problems with a fast, trustworthy test oracle. Its own technical report conceded it required extensive trial and error, relied heavily on filtering out bad samples, and was too costly to operate at scale, so it never shipped as a product and its approach was absorbed into general Gemini coding agents and AlphaEvolve rather than continued as a standalone system.
83 Google AutoML / Neural Architecture Search Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Google's AutoML is a cloud suite that uses Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and transfer learning to automatically discover model architectures and hyperparameters for custom vision, tabular, text, and video models, now delivered inside Vertex AI. NAS paper Nov 2016 / ICLR 2017; NASNet 2017; Cloud AutoML announced 17 Jan 2018 (AutoML Vision first); AutoML Tables Apr 2019; consolidated into Vertex AI May 2021; AutoML Text training ended 15 Sep 2024, shutdown 15 Jun 2025; AutoML Video deprecated 31 Jul 2024, shutdown 31 Jul 2025 It succeeded technically because NAS produced genuinely better, more efficient architectures (NASNet, EfficientNet, MnasNet) and Google productised that research as a no-code enterprise service, but the original RL-based search was enormously expensive (about 800 GPUs for weeks) and later work showed simpler methods could match it. Market timing then shifted the value from bespoke small-model search to foundation-model prompting and fine-tuning, so Google kept the still-valuable vision/tabular AutoML while retiring text and video AutoML in 2024-2025 in favour of Gemini tuning.
84 SWE-agent / OpenAI Codex agentic coding Specialist/Adapter Succeeded An agentic coding system — SWE-agent as the Princeton academic agent that autonomously fixes GitHub issues via a purpose-built agent-computer interface, and OpenAI Codex as its commercial successor: a sandboxed CLI/cloud/IDE agent that writes, tests and proposes code changes in real repositories. SWE-agent repo created 2024-04-02; paper May 2024, NeurIPS 2024; SWE-agent 1.0 Feb 2025; mini-SWE-agent superseded SWE-agent (Jul 2025, 65% SWE-bench Verified); OpenAI Codex CLI repo created 2025-04-13, cloud research preview launched 2025-05-16 (codex-1), Plus rollout 2025-06-03, GPT-5-Codex Sep 2025, generally available 2025-10-06, GPT-5.3-Codex Feb 2026. Technical fit: a narrow, verifiable task — fix a real issue and pass its tests — plus a purpose-built tool interface and sandboxed execute-test-revise loop made autonomy tractable where generic agents failed. Adoption/economics: SWE-agent was open, hackable research infrastructure that defined the benchmark, while Codex paired the same loop with frontier models and ChatGPT/CLI/IDE distribution, so improvements in the underlying model directly raised agent performance. The academic original was superseded by the simpler mini-SWE-agent, showing the winning pattern was minimal scaffolding around a strong model, not a heavier framework.
85 Ray / Anyscale Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Ray is the open-source Python framework for distributed AI compute (training, data, serving, RL), and Anyscale is its managed commercial platform founded by Ray's Berkeley creators. Ray begun 2016-2017 at UC Berkeley RISELab, released 2017; Anyscale founded 2019; Series C at $1B valuation 2021-2022; Ray contributed to PyTorch Foundation Oct 22, 2025; Nscale definitive agreement to acquire Anyscale July 30, 2026 (closing expected H2 2026, not yet verified closed); Ray 2.59.0 Oct 2, 2026. Ray succeeded because its @ray.remote-style API made scaling Python across GPUs nearly frictionless, so it became the de facto standard just as LLM training/serving exploded, with OpenAI-scale adoption creating a durable ecosystem. Anyscale monetized that adoption via managed clusters and its RayTurbo runtime, while moving Ray to the PyTorch Foundation in 2025 de-risked vendor lock-in and made the ~$1.65B Nscale acquisition possible. The main friction was always commercial: many enterprises self-host free Ray, so Anyscale's standalone value was clearer as part of a full-stack GPU cloud than alone.
86 Temporal Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Temporal is an open-source durable-execution platform that lets developers write long-running workflows as ordinary code while it persists state and automatically retries and resumes work after failures, now widely used to orchestrate AI agents. Founded 2019 by Samar Abbas and Maxim Fateev (ex-Uber Cadence); first production release Oct 2020; Series C Mar 2025 ($146M, $1.72B valuation); Series D Feb 2026 ($300M, $5B); OpenAI Agents SDK integration GA Mar 23, 2026; Series E Sep 14, 2026 ($550M, $12.55B); Server v1.32.2 Nov 2, 2026. It succeeded by solving a pre-AI distributed-systems problem — failure handling for long-running work — that became critical as agents began running for days or months, rather than pivoting opportunistically to AI. Its code-first model, MIT-licensed open source, managed Temporal Cloud, and proven enterprise adoption created a durable moat, and the AI boom amplified demand instead of requiring a new product thesis.
87 Flower (Flower Labs) Orchestration/Enterprise In Progress Flower (flwr) is an open-source, framework-agnostic Python framework for federated and collaborative AI, letting organizations train and run models on data that stays distributed across devices and organizational silos. Framework created as a research project in 2020 (Cambridge/Oxford); Flower Labs founded March 2023; $3.6M pre-seed 2023; $20M Series A 15 Feb 2024; Flower 1.26 released Feb 2026; v1.43.0 released 4 Nov 2026 — no shutdown, pivot, or acquisition. It succeeded because it solved a real, growing constraint — privacy and compliance blocking centralized data pooling — with a genuinely framework-agnostic, Apache-2.0 open-source design that researchers could adopt for free, while Flower Labs monetized via an enterprise platform and forward-deployed engineering. Sustained academic credibility (Cambridge/Oxford origins, MLSys-published federated LLM pre-training) and marquee enterprise adopters created a self-reinforcing community, keeping releases frequent through 2026 rather than fading after the initial funding.
88 NVIDIA Triton / NIM multi-model inference Orchestration/Enterprise In Progress NVIDIA's open-source, backend-agnostic inference server (Triton, now Dynamo-Triton) that serves many models from different frameworks concurrently behind one HTTP/gRPC API, packaged per-model as NIM containerized microservices. Triton repo created 2018-10-04 (as TensorRT Inference Server, renamed Triton in 2020); NVIDIA NIM launched 18 Mar 2024 at GTC; Triton folded into NVIDIA Dynamo as Dynamo-Triton in Mar 2025; latest verified release v2.75.0 / container 26.11, Nov 2026. It succeeded as infrastructure rather than as an agent framework: it is hardware-coupled to NVIDIA's dominant GPU installed base, backend-agnostic (TensorRT, PyTorch, ONNX, vLLM and others), and maintained on a monthly release cadence inside NVIDIA AI Enterprise. Rather than being abandoned, it was absorbed upward into Dynamo in 2025 for large-scale LLM serving, while Triton remains the general multi-model layer — a sign of sustained adoption, though single-LLM workloads increasingly prefer dedicated engines such as vLLM or SGLang.
89 vLLM multi-model serving Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded vLLM is an open-source, high-throughput LLM inference and serving engine built on PagedAttention and continuous batching, used to serve many models in production via separate per-model server instances behind a router or production stack. Repository created 2023-02-09; PagedAttention paper at SOSP 2023; joined the PyTorch Foundation as a hosted project on 7 May 2025; v0.31.0 released 5 October 2026. It succeeded technically by solving KV-cache memory waste with PagedAttention and continuous batching, delivering high throughput for many concurrent requests, and by covering 200+ model architectures across NVIDIA, AMD, TPU and other hardware. Adoption compounded through open-source neutrality under the PyTorch Foundation, an OpenAI-compatible API, and production validation at hyperscale users, which made it the default serving layer rather than a niche engine.
90 Palantir AIP (Artificial Intelligence Platform) Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Palantir's enterprise generative-AI platform that connects LLMs and AI agents to an organization's live data, workflows and operations through its Ontology, with governance, evals and human oversight. Palantir founded 2003; AIP launched April 2023 (began deploying in 2023); still active through 2025-2026, no shutdown or pivot. It succeeded commercially because it solved the enterprise adoption bottleneck — grounding LLMs in governed operational data and actions rather than offering a standalone chatbot — and paired that with intensive five-day AIP Bootcamps that produced working prototypes on customers' own data. Timing also favoured it: launched in April 2023 at the peak of enterprise LLM demand, it rode Palantir's existing Foundry/Gotham footprint and government credibility into rapid commercial expansion. The trade-off is cost and complexity: it is a heavyweight, expensive platform requiring deep deployment engagement, so it wins large enterprises and government, not small teams.
91 ServiceNow Now Assist / agent mesh (AI Agent Orchestrator + AI Agent Fabric, now ServiceNow Otto) Orchestration/Enterprise In Progress ServiceNow's generative-AI layer on the Now Platform — Now Assist skills plus multi-agent AI Agents coordinated by AI Agent Orchestrator and connected to external agents via AI Agent Fabric (MCP/A2A) — now unified under the ServiceNow Otto experience. Now Assist launched Sept 2023 (Vancouver release, GA Sept 29, 2023); AI Agents and AI Agent Orchestrator announced Jan 29, 2025, available Mar 2025; Moveworks acquired for $2.85B, closed Dec 15, 2025; AI Agent Fabric external-agent (A2A) support GA Dec 2025; EmployeeWorks launched Feb 26, 2026; Otto announced May 5, 2026 at Knowledge 2026, folding Now Assist, Moveworks and AI Experience into one experience; legacy Pro Plus/Enterprise Plus tiers ended sale July 1, 2026, replaced by Foundation/Advanced/Prime. It succeeded commercially because it rides an entrenched workflow platform and CMDB — agents act on real enterprise data and existing ITSM/HR/SecOps workflows rather than starting from scratch — but adoption is slower than the vendor narrative because the value is bundled into expensive tier upgrades (Pro Plus/Enterprise Plus, now Prime), consumption is metered in 'assists' (agents cost 25–150 assists per run vs 1 for a skill), and ROI proof outside large reference customers remains thin. The May 2026 Otto rebrand also signals the standalone Now Assist brand was not enough on its own; ServiceNow had to buy Moveworks ($2.85B) and merge three AI surfaces to deliver a unified front door.
92 Adept AI (ACT-1 / Fuyu) Orchestration/Enterprise Stalled/Abandoned Adept AI built ACT-1, an Action Transformer that observes screens and operates software via clicks and keystrokes, and the Fuyu family of multimodal models optimized for UI understanding. Founded/emerged from stealth Apr 26, 2022; ACT-1 demo Sep 14, 2022; Series B Mar 14, 2023; Fuyu-8B Oct 17, 2023; Fuyu-Heavy Jan 24, 2024; Amazon reverse acqui-hire/licensing deal Jun 28, 2024; last public blog post Aug 23, 2024; no formal shutdown announced Adept tried to train frontier foundation models and build an enterprise agent product simultaneously, but ACT-1 never reached product-market fit and stayed in beta for two years while OpenAI, Anthropic and Google shipped competing computer-use capabilities. The economics were unsustainable — Luan himself said frontier agent work needed multi-billion-dollar compute clusters — so in June 2024 the company effectively surrendered its mission in a reverse acqui-hire, with Amazon licensing the tech and hiring the founders, leaving a ~20-person rump with no meaningful public release or news since August 2024.
93 Rabbit R1 / Large Action Model Cognitive architecture/AGI In Progress Rabbit R1 is a $199 handheld AI device running rabbitOS (based on Android) built around Rabbit's Large Action Model (LAM), pitched as an AI that operates apps and websites on the user's behalf instead of just answering questions. Founded as Rabbit Inc. (formerly Cyber Manufacturing Co.) by Jesse Lyu; R1 announced 9 Jan 2024 at CES 2024, shipped spring 2024; LAM Playground Oct 2024, teach mode Nov 2024; rabbitOS 2 on 8 Sept 2025; OS3 general availability 22 Sept 2026; R1 manufacturing stopped Sept 2026, no R2 planned. The CES demo outran the shipping product: at launch it had only a handful of integrations, reviewers gave it 3/10, and investigations showed the 'LAM' largely relied on cloud-operated Android VMs and scripts rather than a learned general action model, so retention collapsed to ~5% of buyers. Economics and market timing compounded this — a single-purpose $199 gadget could not justify itself against smartphones that gained the same AI features for free — and after reported financial distress in 2025 Rabbit pivoted from R1 hardware to OS3, cross-device agent software running on computers users already own.
94 Humane AI Pin Orchestration/Enterprise Failed A screenless, voice-controlled wearable AI pin intended as a smartphone replacement, powered by Humane's cloud-based CosmOS platform. Founded 2018; AI Pin unveiled 9 Nov 2023; shipped Apr 2024; HP asset deal announced 18 Feb 2025; AI Pin discontinued and servers shut down 28 Feb 2025 Reviews found it slow, inaccurate, hot, short on battery, and with a laser display unreadable in sunlight, while its $699 price plus $24/month subscription offered no compelling advantage over a phone. Poor adoption and returns, compounded by a charging-case fire-risk warning, made the economics unsustainable, and because its intelligence lived in the cloud, the Feb 2025 shutdown left sold devices effectively bricked — HP bought the platform, patents and team, not the consumer product.
95 Microsoft AutoGen Studio / Magentic-UI Multi-agent framework Failed Microsoft's open-source multi-agent framework with a no-code Studio for building agent teams, and its human-centred web-agent prototype Magentic-UI (later MagenticLite) built on AutoGen/Magentic-One. AutoGen launched Sep 2023; AutoGen Studio introduced 17 Jun 2024; AutoGen v0.4 released 19 Feb 2025; Magentic-UI launched 19 May 2025 (repo created 5 May 2025); AutoGen v0.7.5, last release, 30 Sep 2025; maintenance mode Oct 2025; Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA 2 Apr 2026; Magentic-UI 0.2/MagenticLite May 2026, last main commit 23 Jul 2026; AutoGen archived Nov 2026, with the AI Frontiers group behind it dissolved/reorganised in Sep-Nov 2026. AutoGen was a research prototype that never reached 1.0 and was explicitly not production-ready, so Microsoft consolidated it with Semantic Kernel into the enterprise-supported Microsoft Agent Framework instead of sustaining two overlapping stacks. Magentic-UI compounded this by pivoting from frontier-model 0.1 to small-model MagenticLite 0.2 with no database migration, while its parent research group was dissolved and its underlying framework was archived, leaving a well-starred demo with no durable product or maintenance path.
96 Google A2A Protocol (Agent2Agent) + ADK ecosystem Multi-agent framework In Progress Google's open, vendor-neutral Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol for agents built on different frameworks to discover each other and delegate tasks, plus the Agent Development Kit (ADK), Google's open-source multi-agent SDK in Python, TypeScript, Go, Java and Kotlin. 9 Apr 2025: Google announces A2A and ADK at Cloud Next, with 50+ A2A partners; May 2025: ADK Python 1.0; 23 Jun 2025: A2A donated to Linux Foundation; Aug 2025: IBM Agent Communication Protocol merges into A2A; 12 Mar 2026: A2A v1.0.0; 9 Apr 2026: 150+ organisations reported; 19 May 2026: ADK Python 2.0 GA; 27 Aug 2026: A2A joins Agentic AI Foundation as Growth Stage project; 12 Nov 2026: ADK Python 2.14.1 It gained traction because Google donated A2A to the Linux Foundation (then the Agentic AI Foundation) within months, removing single-vendor lock-in fears, and because it uses boring proven plumbing (HTTP, JSON-RPC, gRPC) and complements rather than competes with Anthropic's MCP. It remains In Progress rather than fully Succeeded because published evidence is mostly supporter and platform-integration counts, not verified deployment or task volume, and ADK's A2A support only graduated from experimental to stable in November 2026, trailing MCP's maturity by roughly 12-18 months.
97 OpenAI ChatGPT / GPT-4o monolith baseline MoE/Modular LLM Succeeded A general-purpose, single-model conversational AI product — ChatGPT powered by the natively multimodal GPT-4o flagship model — used as the monolithic control against multi-agent and modular systems. ChatGPT launched 30 Nov 2022; GPT-4o launched 13 May 2024; GPT-5 replaced GPT-4o as default 7 Aug 2025 (restored for paid users after backlash); GPT-4o retired from ChatGPT 13 Feb 2026 (announced 29 Jan 2026); first GPT-4o API snapshot shut down 23 Oct 2026. It succeeded through a frictionless chat interface, native text/vision/audio multimodality in one model, and massive distribution, with OpenAI continuously swapping newer models behind the same product so adoption compounded rather than resetting. The monolith's weakness was version churn: GPT-4o's warm, sycophantic style created user attachment, so its abrupt replacement by GPT-5 and final retirement in Feb 2026 provoked backlash and lawsuits despite the platform's overall growth.
98 Anthropic Claude / Constitutional AI Memory/Verification/Self-improvement Succeeded Claude is Anthropic's single-model AI assistant family, trained with Constitutional AI, in which the model critiques and revises its own behaviour against an explicit written constitution instead of relying only on human preference labels. Founded 26 Jan 2021; Constitutional AI paper published 15 Dec 2022; Claude launched 14 Mar 2023; Claude Opus 4.5 released 24 Nov 2025; new Claude constitution published 22 Jan 2026; Series H announced 28 May 2026; public Form S-1 filed 26 Oct 2026. The explicit constitution made alignment scalable: self-critique reduced the human-labelling bottleneck while producing auditable, updatable rules, and Constitutional Classifiers extended the same idea to runtime safeguards. Anthropic then converted that trusted single model into a commercial wedge through tool use, MCP, and Claude Code, especially in coding and enterprise workflows. Favourable market timing and massive Amazon/Google-backed compute and funding sustained rapid iteration from Claude 4 through Opus 4.5 and beyond.
99 Amazon Bedrock Agents / multi-agent collaboration Orchestration/Enterprise Failed Amazon Bedrock Agents' multi-agent collaboration is a managed AWS service in which a supervisor agent decomposes complex tasks and delegates them to up to 10 specialized collaborator agents with their own tools, knowledge bases, and guardrails. Bedrock Agents launched November 2023; multi-agent collaboration preview December 3, 2024; GA March 10, 2025; AgentCore GA July 23, 2026; Bedrock Agents Classic closed to new customers and placed in maintenance mode July 30, 2026 It shipped and reached GA, but AWS superseded its console-first, fixed-orchestration model with AgentCore, a framework-agnostic runtime where developers bring their own agent code (Strands, LangGraph, CrewAI) and AWS supplies runtime, memory, gateway, identity, and observability. The Classic service's frozen model catalog after July 30, 2026 made staying on it progressively untenable for new models, so the root cause was architectural rigidity and platform economics: a managed loop could not keep pace with open frameworks and new foundation models. Existing Classic workloads were left running in maintenance mode rather than being shut down outright.
100 Databricks Mosaic AI Agent Framework Orchestration/Enterprise Succeeded Databricks' enterprise, framework-agnostic platform for building, evaluating, deploying and monitoring production AI agents and RAG applications on governed enterprise data. MosaicML acquired by Databricks June-July 2023 for ~$1.3B; Mosaic AI Agent Framework public preview launched 12 June 2024; generally available March 2025; extended 2025-2026 with Agent Bricks (Data + AI Summit 2025) and Agent Bricks Supervisor Agent GA in 2026. It succeeded commercially because it did not try to win as a standalone library: it piggybacked on Databricks' existing data, Unity Catalog governance and MLflow/Model Serving moat, solving the enterprise pilot-to-production blockers of quality measurement, safety and auditability. Being framework-agnostic — wrapping LangGraph, LangChain, CrewAI or plain Python — removed adoption friction, while consumption-based serving/evaluation pricing aligned its economics with Databricks' platform revenue rather than requiring a separate framework sale.

Detail per project — evidence and HIVE relevance

1. Microsoft AutoGen — Failed

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Microsoft Research's open-source framework for building multi-agent AI applications in which LLM agents collaborate through structured conversation.
  • Key dates: GitHub repo created 2023-08-18; first released fall/October 2023; v0.4 ground-up async/event-driven rewrite announced 2025-01-14; last feature release python-v0.7.5 on 2025-09-30; maintenance mode from 2025-10-02; successor Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA 2026-04-03; AutoGen README changed to 'archived and no longer maintained' on 2026-10-16 per secondary reports, confirmed in the live README fetched 2026-11-19.
  • Evidence: 61,928 GitHub stars, 9,412 forks, 3,785 commits and 98 releases as of the 2026-11-19 GitHub crawl; Magentic-One, built on AutoGen, scored 38% on GAIA, 27.7% on AssistantBench and 32.8% on WebArena at launch; no feature release since v0.7.5 (2025-09-30), and the current README states Microsoft provides no bug fixes, security fixes, new features or support and advises against production use, directing users to Microsoft Agent Framework.
  • Why: AutoGen succeeded as research and popularised conversational multi-agent orchestration, but its v0.2-to-v0.4 breaking rewrite fragmented users and its original creators left to continue the old design as the AG2 fork. Microsoft then consolidated AutoGen with Semantic Kernel into a single enterprise-supported Microsoft Agent Framework with stable APIs and long-term support, so the standalone project was superseded by design rather than collapsing from a single technical failure.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy AutoGen's conversation/group-chat orchestration and layered Core/AgentChat/Extensions separation, but avoid a breaking rewrite, single-vendor consolidation risk, and dependence on a framework without a stable API and migration path.
  • Sources: https://github.com/microsoft/autogen; https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/autogen-v0-4-reimagining-the-foundation-of-agentic-ai-for-scale-extensibility-and-robustness/; https://atlan.com/know/ai-agent/what-is-autogen/; https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/autogen-enabling-next-gen-llm-applications-via-multi-agent-conversation-framework/

2. CrewAI — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: CrewAI is an MIT-licensed open-source Python framework for orchestrating role-based teams of autonomous AI agents (Crews) within deterministic, event-driven workflows (Flows), plus the commercial CrewAI AMP platform for deploying, observing, and governing them in production.
  • Key dates: Repo created 27 Oct 2023; first PyPI release 4 Dec 2023; company launched Jan 2024; $18M seed+Series A announced 22 Oct 2024; CrewAI Enterprise launched late 2024; 1.0 GA and CrewAI AMP (formerly AOP) launched Oct 2025; v1.15.22 stable release 16 Sep 2026; active releases/docs through Nov 2026.
  • Evidence: $18M raised across seed (boldstart-led) and Series A (Insight Partners-led) in Oct 2024, at ~$100M valuation per TechCrunch, with angels Andrew Ng and Dharmesh Shah; ~58,800-59,700 GitHub stars and 112.6M all-time PyPI downloads as of Sep-Oct 2026; v1.15.22 released 16 Sep 2026, with releases continuing into Nov 2026 (v1.15.41 on 12 Nov 2026 per secondary checks) and docs updated 7 Nov 2026; 150 enterprise customers in its first year; company claims 65% of Fortune 500 use and 2B+ agentic executions, which are unverified vendor claims. Four critical CVEs in Mar 2026 were fixed in v1.14.0 by removing the Code Interpreter tool.
  • Why: It succeeded by lowering the barrier to multi-agent prototyping: describing agents by role, goal, and backstory is an intuitive mental model, the MIT-licensed open source seeded wide community and enterprise adoption, and Flows added the deterministic control production use required. Monetization followed that adoption via an enterprise control plane rather than paywalling the framework. Remaining risk is coordination overhead and reliability of autonomous crews, illustrated by the March 2026 security flaws and the removal of its $25/month mid-tier, suggesting enterprise pricing and governance are still being tuned.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy CrewAI's role-goal-backstory crews for fast prototyping plus deterministic Flows as the production backbone, and avoid pure autonomous multi-agent collaboration without guardrails, observability, and a deterministic control layer.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CrewAI; https://docs.crewai.com/v1.15.37/en/introduction; https://techcrunch.com/2024/10/22/crewai-uses-third-party-models-to-automate-business-tasks/; https://javisreview.com/review/crewai-review-2026/

3. LangGraph (LangChain) — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: LangGraph is LangChain's open-source, MIT-licensed low-level graph/state-machine framework and runtime for building long-running, stateful single- and multi-agent applications with checkpointing, branching/loops, and human-in-the-loop control.
  • Key dates: Introduced Jan 2024; v0.1 stable release 27 Jun 2024; LangGraph 1.0 GA 17-22 Oct 2025 (tagged 17 Oct, announced 22 Oct); LangGraph Platform renamed LangSmith Deployment Oct 2025; v1.2.21 released 12 Nov 2026
  • Evidence: LangGraph 1.0 reached GA in Oct 2025 with a no-breaking-changes commitment until 2.0; v1.2.21 Python was released 12 Nov 2026. As of 13 Nov 2026 it had 44,888 GitHub stars and 58.7M PyPI downloads in 30 days (including installs pulled in via LangChain, which depends on it). LangChain Inc. raised a $125M Series B on 20 Oct 2025 at a $1.25B valuation (IVP-led); production users named include Klarna, Uber, LinkedIn, Replit and Elastic, and LangChain reports 35% of the Fortune 500 use its products.
  • Why: It succeeded because it solved the reliability problem of opaque early agent abstractions (notably LangChain's AgentExecutor) by giving developers explicit control over state, control flow, persistence, and approvals, with durable checkpointing and time-travel/resume. It became the execution layer under LangChain's own create_agent in the 1.0 release, locking in adoption, while the free MIT core drove developer uptake and paid LangSmith observability/deployment monetized production use. Its timing matched enterprise demand for controllable, auditable agents in 2024-2025, backed by $125M Series B funding at a $1.25B valuation.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy LangGraph's explicit graph/state model, checkpoint-after-every-step durability, and human-in-the-loop interrupts, but avoid its steep learning curve and verbosity by providing higher-level prebuilt multi-agent patterns on top.
  • Sources: https://www.langchain.com/blog/langgraph-cloud; https://www.cognyx.ai/blog/langgraph-vs-crewai-vs-openai-agents-sdk-2026; https://javisreview.com/review/langgraph-review-2026/

4. MetaGPT — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source Python multi-agent framework from DeepWisdom/FoundationAgents that simulates a software company — Product Manager, Architect, Project Manager, Engineer and QA agents collaborating via codified SOPs — to turn a one-line requirement into documents and code.
  • Key dates: Repo created 2023-06-30; arXiv paper posted 2023-08-01; public release late Aug 2023; ICLR 2024 oral (top 1.2%); MGX launched 2025-02-19; latest release v0.8.2 on 2025-03-09; last commit 2026-01-21; MGX rebranded Atoms 2026-01-13; no shutdown/archival as of 2026-11-18.
  • Evidence: GitHub (checked 2026-11-19): 71,348 stars, 9,114 forks, 6,367 commits, 22 releases, MIT licence, not archived; latest release v0.8.2 on 2025-03-09 (~20 months ago) and last push 2026-01-21 (~10 months ago), with README news stopping Mar 2025. DeepWisdom raised ~US$31M / RMB 220M in 2025 (Series A/A+); its commercial product MGX reached 500,000 users in its first month and >US$1M ARR, later rebranded Atoms, while the same team's OpenManus remained active — indicating energy moved off the MetaGPT repo itself.
  • Why: Its rigid SOP/company-role structure produced impressive demos but was brittle, hard to customise and debug, and expensive in coordination and LLM calls per run, limiting production adoption despite ~71k stars. The team shifted focus and funding to the commercial MGX/Atoms product and to OpenManus, leaving the framework with no release since Mar 2025 and no commit since Jan 2026. Star count therefore reflects historical influence, not current maintenance.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy MetaGPT's SOP-driven role handoffs and structured artifacts (PRD/design/tasks/code), but avoid rigid fixed software-company roles and a demo-first framework with no maintenance owner — keep roles flexible, verifiable, and actively maintained.
  • Sources: https://github.com/FoundationAgents/MetaGPT; https://www.mindworks.vc/logos/deepwisdom; https://aiwiki.ai/wiki/metagpt; https://ww.tomrochette.com/agents/orchestration/metagpt/

5. AutoGPT — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source project, now the AutoGPT Platform, in which a coordinating agent ('Otto') directs a team of specialist AI 'Experts' and visual-block workflows to carry out recurring multi-step work.
  • Key dates: Repo created 16 Mar 2023; released 30 Mar 2023; $12M funding Oct 2023; AutoGPT Platform launched 24 Sep 2024; Classic marked unsupported Nov 2024; latest platform beta v0.11.2, 14 Nov 2026
  • Evidence: GitHub (crawled 2026-11-19): Significant-Gravitas/AutoGPT, not archived, 188,093 stars, 45,864 forks, 10,640 commits, 131 releases; ~5,272 commits in the last 52 weeks and platform-beta-v0.11.2 released 14 Nov 2026. Significant Gravitas raised $12M in Oct 2023 led by Redpoint Ventures with GitHub Ventures. The original standalone agent is now AutoGPT Classic and explicitly unsupported, while the AutoGPT Platform remains in beta; no verified active-user or revenue figures were found, so stars should not be read as current adoption.
  • Why: The original 2023 autonomous GPT-4 loop went viral but was unreliable in practice: it hallucinated, got stuck in loops, accumulated errors, and incurred high API costs with no measurable success criterion. The team therefore rebuilt it in 2024 as a structured, human-authored platform of typed workflow blocks, scheduled routines, and supervised experts, which is more reliable but still in beta more than two years later — so its 188k stars largely reflect 2023 hype for a product that no longer exists in that form, not proven commercial adoption of the current platform.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy AutoGPT's pivot from unbounded autonomy to bounded, human-authored, verifiable workflows with cost controls — and avoid launching an open-ended agent loop with no success criteria, verification, or spend caps.
  • Sources: https://github.com/Significant-Gravitas/AutoGPT; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AutoGPT; https://chatforest.com/reviews/autogpt-autonomous-agent-platform/; https://latent.space/wiki/autogpt-bff166c7/

6. BabyAGI — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source experimental autonomous-agent framework by Yohei Nakajima, originally a ~140-line Python script looping task creation, execution, and reprioritization with GPT-4 and a vector store, later rebuilt as a 'self-building' function framework.
  • Key dates: 28 Mar 2023: first posted by Yohei Nakajima; 3 Apr 2023: GitHub repo created; Sep 2024: original task-loop archived to babyagi_archive; 2024: repo rebuilt as 'functionz' self-building agent framework; 11 Oct 2024: last code change; 31 Jan 2026: last commit (docs-only); early 2026: 'BabyAGI 3' mentioned in secondary sources but not evidenced by code activity in the main repo.
  • Evidence: GitHub (verified 2026-11-18): 22,361 stars, 2,830 forks, 303 watchers, 0 releases, only 15 commits in the current (rebuilt) repo; last code change 11 Oct 2024, last commit overall 31 Jan 2026 (documentation file only). README states the original March 2023 task-loop was archived and moved to babyagi_archive in September 2024, and warns the current code is 'not meant for production use.' No funding, company, hosted API, or commercial product ever existed; MIT-licensed, solo-maintained by Yohei Nakajima.
  • Why: It was a viral demo, not a product: the unbounded task loop racked up API costs, hallucinated tasks, and looped without verification, guardrails, or a stopping condition. It stayed a solo, nights-and-weekends experiment with no team, funding, or commercial model, while the field moved to structured frameworks with tools, evals, and human-in-the-loop control. The original loop was archived in September 2024 and the rebuild has had no code change since October 2024, leaving it an explicitly non-production idea repository rather than a sustained system.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy BabyAGI's canonical create-prioritize-execute loop as the minimal agent primitive, but avoid its fatal flaw: unbounded self-generated task loops with no verification, cost budget, or termination condition.
  • Sources: https://github.com/yoheinakajima/babyagi; https://www.promptquorum.com/power-local-llm/babyagi-review-autonomous-agent; https://www.agentichistory.org/faq.html; https://github.com/fyodoriv/minsky/blob/HEAD/competitors/babyagi.md

7. SuperAGI — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: A dev-first open-source autonomous AI agent framework (Python/FastAPI + GUI, tool marketplace, memory, workflows) whose company pivoted to a commercial AI sales/GTM platform and was acquired by QuestionPro in November 2026.
  • Key dates: Company founded 2021 as Contlo Inc. (Palo Alto); open-source framework repo created May 13, 2023; last release v0.0.14 Jan 2024; last framework commit Jan 22, 2025; $10M Series A Mar 2024; acquired by QuestionPro Nov 4, 2026.
  • Evidence: Open-source repo TransformerOptimus/SuperAGI: 17,747 stars, 2,221 forks, 2,342 commits, MIT license; last release v0.0.14 (Jan 2024) and last commit Jan 22, 2025 — no framework commits in ~22 months. Company (formerly Contlo Inc.) raised ~$14.3M total, including a $10M Series A led by Newlands Ventures (Mar 2024); G2 rating 4.5/5 across 445 reviews; estimated ~$20.2M annual revenue. Acquired by QuestionPro, announced Nov 4, 2026, terms undisclosed; co-founders Ishaan Bhola and Mukunda NS joined QuestionPro as AI advisors and QuestionPro said it will deploy SuperAGI's agentic AI platform across its product suite.
  • Why: The open-source framework succeeded as a 2023 hype-era hit (~17.7k stars) but stalled: no release since Jan 2024 and no commits since Jan 2025, with publicly disclosed unpatched vulnerabilities, because the company redirected all effort to its paid SaaS GTM product. That pivot was the economically rational move — the framework alone had no revenue model — and it ended in an acquisition, with QuestionPro buying the company (Contlo/SuperAGI) for its agentic platform to power agentic commerce. So the framework is effectively abandoned while the company succeeded via pivot and exit.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the pivot discipline — monetize a narrow, verifiable business workflow (sales/GTM agents) rather than the open framework — but avoid letting the open-source core rot: unpatched security flaws and a stalled repo destroyed developer trust even as the company itself exited successfully.
  • Sources: https://github.com/TransformerOptimus/SuperAGI; https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/11/04/3395622/0/en/questionpro-acquires-contlo-to-advance-agentic-commerce.html?print=1; https://www.datacamp.com/blog/superagi; https://www.cbinsights.com/company/superagi

8. CAMEL (Communicative Agents for Mind Exploration) — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source Python multi-agent framework from KAUST that lets LLM agents autonomously cooperate via role-playing with inception prompting.
  • Key dates: Repo created 2023-03-17; arXiv paper submitted 2023-03-31 (v2 Nov 2023); accepted at NeurIPS 2023; OWL spin-off gained prominence in 2025; still actively released in 2026 (camel-ai 0.2.90, Oct 2026).
  • Evidence: GitHub (accessed 2026-11-19): 18,048 stars, 2,148 forks, 2,316 commits, 213 releases, Apache 2.0, not archived; repo created 2023-03-17. PyPI camel-ai at version 0.2.90 (Oct 2026), with a reported push on 2026-11-16. NeurIPS 2023 paper; community claims 100+ researchers and OASIS simulation of up to 1M agents; its sibling OWL framework was named among GitHub's top open-source AI projects of 2025.
  • Why: It succeeded because its core idea was simple, general, and immediately usable — two agents with assigned roles collaborating with minimal human input — and because it grew beyond a single paper demo into a sustained open-source community (Apache 2.0, 100+ researchers) that kept adding Workforce, RAG, data generation, and large-scale simulation (OASIS, OWL). Academic adoption and continuous releases, rather than venture funding or a commercial product, sustained it while many 2023-era agent frameworks froze or went dormant.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy CAMEL's role-playing inception prompting — explicit user/assistant roles with task-consistent instructions — and its scalable agent-society abstractions, but avoid its research-first sprawl by keeping HIVE's orchestration production-hardened and narrowly scoped.
  • Sources: https://github.com/camel-ai/camel; https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.17760; https://pypi.org/project/camel-ai/

9. ChatDev — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source multi-agent framework from OpenBMB/Tsinghua that originally simulated a virtual software company (CEO, CTO, programmer, tester) collaborating through chat chains, now pivoted to ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll), a zero-code YAML/visual multi-agent orchestration platform.
  • Key dates: 2023-06-30 initial repo; 2023-07-16 arXiv preprint (2307.07924); 2023-08-17 v1.0.0; 2023-08-28 public release; 2023-11-15 SaaS launch; 2024 ACL 2024 publication; 2026-01-07 ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll) release, v1.x moved to chatdev1.0 maintenance branch; 2026-08 ChatDev 2.0 accepted to EMNLP 2026 Demo Track
  • Evidence: GitHub (OpenBMB/ChatDev, crawled 2026-11-19): 34,805 stars, 4,354 forks, Apache-2.0, not archived, 12 releases/tags; ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll) released 2026-01-07, with commits into Oct 2026 (main pushed 2026-10-30; latest tag v2.2.0) and ChatDev 2.0 paper accepted to EMNLP 2026 Demo Track. Original paper (ACL 2024): on its 1,200-prompt SRDD set it reported quality 0.3953 vs MetaGPT 0.1523, ~88% executability, and a full small build in under 7 minutes for under $1. No verified commercial revenue or production-user count found; stars are the main adoption signal.
  • Why: The original rigid waterfall role-play was a strong research demo — fast and cheap on small toy apps — but fixed roles and chat-only coordination limited real-world code quality and adaptability as single-agent coding tools improved. Rather than dying, the team pivoted in January 2026 to a general-purpose zero-code platform (DevAll), keeping the v1 software-company pipeline only as a maintenance-branch demo. Sustained academic output (ACL 2024, NeurIPS 2025 puppeteer work, EMNLP 2026 Demo) and 34.8k stars kept it alive, though stars have not translated into verified commercial adoption.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its instructor/assistant chat-chain and communicative dehallucination for role-specialised agents, but avoid its rigid waterfall/company role-play — make orchestration a configurable workflow graph with human-in-the-loop, as ChatDev itself had to pivot to in 2.0.
  • Sources: https://github.com/OpenBMB/ChatDev; https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.07924; https://blog.tomrochette.com/agents/orchestration/chatdev/

10. AgentVerse — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: AgentVerse is an open-source multi-agent framework that dynamically recruits LLM expert agents and loops through collaborative decision-making, action execution, and evaluation to solve tasks or run simulations.
  • Key dates: Launched 1 May 2023; repo created 6 May 2023; arXiv paper 21 Aug 2023 (revised 23 Oct 2023); ICLR 2024 acceptance announced 17 Jan 2024; last commit 9 Sep 2024; no shutdown or pivot announced
  • Evidence: OpenBMB/AgentVerse on GitHub: 5,164 stars, 523 forks, 361 commits, Apache-2.0, not archived, but last commit 9 Sep 2024 (fix to OpenAI async call) and no release since v0.1.8.1 (27 Oct 2023) — over 26 months inactive as of Nov 2026. Paper-reported results: HumanEval Pass@1 for GPT-4 rose 83.5 to 89.0 and it completed 9/10 intricate tool-use tasks vs 3/10 for a standalone ReAct agent. Correction to the brief: it is Tsinghua/OpenBMB-led, not a Microsoft product — Microsoft researchers were co-authors — and it was never commercialised; no funding, users, or shutdown notice was verifiable.
  • Why: It succeeded as research — ICLR 2024 and 5.1k GitHub stars — but had no product owner, commercial model, or maintainer to carry it past the paper, so development stopped in September 2024. It was superseded by actively maintained frameworks such as AutoGen/LangGraph/CrewAI and Microsoft's Magentic-One/Agent Framework, while its flat dynamic-recruitment design carried high coordination and token costs and documented emergent harms such as conformity and destructive criticism that are hard to make reliable in production.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its dynamic expert-recruitment plus evaluation/feedback loop, but avoid flat free-form multi-agent discussion as the core — use bounded hierarchical orchestration with explicit evaluation gates and cost caps instead.
  • Sources: https://github.com/OpenBMB/AgentVerse; https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.10848; https://iclr.cc/virtual/2024/poster/19109; https://github.com/OpenBMB/AgentVerse/commits/main

11. OpenAgents (XLang Lab; listed in task as Tsinghua / OpenBMB) — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source platform for using and hosting language agents in everyday life, with three demo agents: a Data Agent, a Plugins Agent with 200+ tools, and a Web Agent for autonomous browsing.
  • Key dates: Created/demos launched 8 Aug 2023; paper submitted 16 Oct 2023 and published at COLM 2024; reached 3,000 users 26 Oct 2023; last code push 18 Nov 2024; no releases or meaningful activity since.
  • Evidence: GitHub xlang-ai/OpenAgents: 4,868 stars, 525 forks, 61 commits, 0 releases, not archived; GitHub API records pushed_at 2024-11-18T09:39:29Z; an Oct 2026 review classifies it Dormant with last push 18 Nov 2024. A critical path-traversal CVE-2025-6282 affects code up to the final commit ff2e464, with the GitHub issue reportedly closed as 'not planned', indicating no patch. Note on attribution: the arXiv paper and repo show XLang Lab (HKU-led) authors, not Tsinghua/OpenBMB; OpenBMB's separate multi-agent work is AgentVerse/ChatDev/XAgent.
  • Why: It was an academic proof-of-concept and demo platform, not a sustained product: it had no releases, commercial model, or ongoing maintainer commitment, and its hosted demo and 200+ third-party plugins were brittle and costly to maintain. The same lab moved on to successor research, notably OSWorld (NeurIPS 2024) and OpenCUA (2025-2026), while LangGraph, CrewAI, and built-in ChatGPT-style agents superseded its approach. With no code push in 24 months and a critical vulnerability left unpatched, it was effectively abandoned without a formal shutdown notice.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its application-level lesson — ship usable Data/Plugins/Web agents behind a simple chat UI for non-experts — but avoid treating an academic demo as a platform: HIVE needs named ownership, releases, patching, and durable multi-agent coordination from day one.
  • Sources: https://github.com/xlang-ai/OpenAgents; https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.10634; https://kanerika.com/blogs/open-source-ai-agents/

12. AgentGPT (Reworkd) — Failed

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: AgentGPT was Reworkd's open-source, browser-based platform that let users name an autonomous AI agent, give it a goal, and watch it decompose the goal into subtasks and execute them in a loop using GPT-3.5/GPT-4.
  • Key dates: Launched April 2023; v1.0.0 November 2023 (development effectively stopped); Reworkd pivoted to web-scraping agents July 2024; GitHub repository archived 28 January 2026
  • Evidence: Viral launch: 100,000+ daily users in first week, $2,000/day in OpenAI API costs, ~500,000 users and ~25,000 GitHub stars within weeks, peaking at ~36,000 stars and 9,400 forks; Reworkd raised $4M total ($1.25M pre-seed + $2.75M seed, Y Combinator S23). Last release v1.0.0 in November 2023, last commit 29 April 2025, 130+ unanswered GitHub issues, and the repo was archived read-only on 28 January 2026; the hosted site still loads with a limited free tier of 5 GPT-3.5 runs/day and no patches or new-model support.
  • Why: The general-purpose autonomous loop was too broad and unreliable for production use — agents went off-track, hallucinated completion, and had no evaluation or observability — while free viral usage cost ~$2,000/day in API calls without converting to paying customers. Reworkd therefore pivoted in July 2024 to the one concrete, high-volume use case, AI web-scraping/data extraction, abandoning AgentGPT, whose code was frozen at its November 2023 release until GitHub archival in January 2026.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy AgentGPT's frictionless browser demo of goal decomposition, but avoid unbounded general-purpose agent loops — HIVE should ship narrow, evaluable, cost-capped tasks with observability instead of open-ended autonomy.
  • Sources: https://techcrunch.com/2024/07/24/reworkd-paul-graham-nat-friedman-daniel-gross-scrape-ai-agents/; https://www.datacamp.com/tutorial/agentgpt; https://www.voiceflow.com/blog/agentgpt; https://www.reworkd.ai/blog/reworkd-raises-1-25m

13. Hugging Face SmolAgents — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Hugging Face's minimal open-source Python library for building LLM agents whose CodeAgent writes actions as executable Python code instead of JSON tool calls.
  • Key dates: Repository created 2024-12-05; launched via Hugging Face blog 2024-12-31 (v1.0.0, December 2024); latest release v1.26.0 on 2026-05-29; no shutdown, acquisition, or pivot as of November 2026.
  • Evidence: GitHub (accessed 2026-11-19): 30,434 stars, 3,224 forks, 36 releases, Apache-2.0, not archived. PyPI downloads ~171,000/week as of November 2026. HF reports CodeAgent uses ~30% fewer steps/LLM calls than JSON tool-calling; an open smolagents-based Deep Research reproduction scored 55.15% on the GAIA validation set in February 2025. Caveat: no release since v1.26.0 (29 May 2026) — about 5.5 months by 12 Nov 2026 — with only ~7 commits in the preceding three months, mostly CI/docs/security-policy, and last main commit 6 Oct 2026, so momentum has cooled even though the project remains live and widely used.
  • Why: It succeeded because of radical minimalism — a readable ~1,000-line core at launch — plus the code-as-action insight, which composes loops and conditionals in one step and measurably cut steps versus JSON tool calls, and Hugging Face distribution (Hub sharing, model-agnostic LiteLLM/Ollama/OpenAI support, MCP tools). Its limits are the flip side of that minimalism: no built-in durable state/checkpointing or enterprise orchestration, and code execution creates a serious sandboxing/security burden (a critical remote-executor RCE published Dec 2025 took weeks to fix), which likely contributes to the slower 2026 release cadence.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the code-as-action agent loop and tiny, readable core with sandboxed execution, but do not copy its minimalism wholesale — HIVE needs durable state, checkpointing, and verification/evals that smolagents deliberately omits.
  • Sources: https://github.com/huggingface/smolagents; https://huggingface.co/blog/smolagents; https://gravity.fast/blog/open-source-ai-agent-frameworks/; https://www.aiagentsinfo.com/reports/ai-agent-framework-github-stats-november-2026

14. OpenAI Swarm / OpenAI Agents SDK — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: OpenAI's lightweight open-source multi-agent orchestration framework built on Agents, handoffs, guardrails and tracing, originally released as the experimental Swarm and productionised as the Agents SDK.
  • Key dates: Swarm repo created 22 Feb 2024 and publicly released as an experimental framework in late 2024; OpenAI Agents SDK launched 11 Mar 2025 alongside the Responses API, replacing Swarm; Agents SDK v0.25.0 released 18 Nov 2026.
  • Evidence: Swarm repo: 22,147 GitHub stars, 2,357 forks, 29 commits, MIT licence; its README states 'Swarm is now replaced by the OpenAI Agents SDK' and recommends migration for production. Agents SDK: first released 11 Mar 2025, Python repo ~30,700 GitHub stars, v0.25.0 released 18 Nov 2026, with a TypeScript port and integration into OpenAI's AgentKit; OpenAI's June 2026 AgentKit update recommends the Agents SDK for code-based workflows after winding down Agent Builder.
  • Why: Swarm was deliberately experimental and educational — stateless, client-side, with no official production support — so its 22k stars proved demand for the handoff pattern rather than for Swarm itself as a product. OpenAI therefore productionised the pattern instead of maintaining two frameworks, adding tracing, guardrails, sessions and Responses API integration in the Agents SDK. The lineage survives because the SDK stays minimal and OpenAI-native, but it remains pre-1.0 (0.x) and platform-centred, so its success is ongoing adoption rather than a finished, stable standard.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Swarm's thin Agents-plus-handoffs primitives with built-in tracing and guardrails, and avoid maintaining a separate experimental fork once a production SDK exists.
  • Sources: https://github.com/openai/swarm; https://community.openai.com/t/new-tools-for-building-agents-responses-api-web-search-file-search-computer-use-and-agents-sdk/1140896; https://agentgid.com/agents/openai-agents-sdk/

15. Google Agent Development Kit (ADK) — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Google's open-source, code-first framework for building, evaluating, and deploying AI agents and multi-agent systems across Python, TypeScript, Go, Java, and Kotlin.
  • Key dates: Repo created Apr 1, 2025; publicly launched Apr 9, 2025 at Google Cloud Next 2025; ADK 1.0 GA mid/late 2025; ADK 2.0 GA (Python) May 19, 2026; latest release v2.14.1 Nov 12, 2026. No shutdown, acquisition, or pivot.
  • Evidence: Google's own repo google/adk-python has 22,265 stars, 4,385 forks, 5,323 commits and 93 releases as of Nov 2026; latest stable google-adk 2.14.1 released Nov 12, 2026, with 3 stable releases in the prior 30 days and ~3.1M npm+PyPI downloads in the week ending Nov 16, 2026; ADK 2.0 reached GA in Python May 19, 2026, Go June 30, 2026, and TypeScript Aug 21, 2026, with Java/Kotlin SDKs also shipping; it powers agents inside Google's Agentspace and Customer Engagement Suite.
  • Why: It succeeded in gaining traction because Google backs it as the same framework powering its own products (Agentspace, Customer Engagement Suite), it is Apache 2.0, model-agnostic via LiteLLM, and ships native A2A and MCP support with a roughly bi-weekly release cadence. Its main friction is churn: ADK 2.0 introduced breaking API/event/session changes and minor 2.x releases have carried further breaking changes, plus six CVEs in 2026, so adoption is strong but the framework is still stabilizing rather than fully mature.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy ADK's code-first multi-agent composition with graph workflows, built-in evaluation, and model-agnostic tooling, but avoid its rapid breaking changes in minor releases — HIVE should version its agent APIs more conservatively.
  • Sources: https://developers.googleblog.com/en/agent-development-kit-easy-to-build-multi-agent-applications/; https://github.com/google/adk-python; https://agentgid.com/agents/google-adk/; https://www.motiontic.com/glossary/google-adk

16. Microsoft Magentic-One — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Microsoft Research's open-source generalist multi-agent system in which a lead Orchestrator plans, tracks progress in ledgers and replans while directing specialist WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder and ComputerTerminal agents to complete complex multi-step web and file-based tasks.
  • Key dates: Launched November 2024 (Microsoft Research blog 4 Nov 2024; arXiv:2411.04468); ported to autogen-agentchat with original implementation deprecated; AutoGen maintenance mode ~Oct 2025; Magentic orchestration in Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA 2-3 Apr 2026
  • Evidence: Open-source (MIT) via Microsoft AutoGen; at launch scored 38% on GAIA, 32.8% on WebArena and 27.7% on AssistantBench, statistically competitive with state of the art at the time, with ablations showing removing the ledgers cost ~31%. The original autogen-core implementation was deprecated after porting to autogen-agentchat as MagenticOneGroupChat, and AutoGen entered maintenance mode around Oct 2025, but the Magentic pattern survived as a supported orchestration in Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 GA (2-3 Apr 2026), whose repo drew 14,000+ GitHub stars in ~18 months; successors Magentic-UI (Jul 2025) and experimental MagenticLite (May 2026) continued the line.
  • Why: It succeeded technically because the orchestrator-plus-ledgers design with stall detection and outer-loop replanning was benchmark-validated and modular enough to be ported from AutoGen into the Microsoft Agent Framework, where it remains a supported pattern. Its limits were economic and safety-related rather than fatal: iterative manager-led runs are costly and unpredictable, and autonomous browser/file actions required Docker containment and human oversight, so it survived as a research-derived orchestration pattern inside a production framework rather than as a standalone consumer product.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the Orchestrator plus Task/Progress ledgers, stall detection and replanning with hard round/stall caps and human-in-the-loop plan review, and avoid unbounded autonomous web/file actions without Docker sandboxing and oversight.
  • Sources: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/magentic-one-a-generalist-multi-agent-system-for-solving-complex-tasks/; https://github.com/microsoft/autogen/blob/HEAD/python/packages/autogen-magentic-one/README.md; https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.04468; https://resources.rework.com/tools/ai-agents/best-multi-agent-frameworks-2026

17. Microsoft TaskWeaver — Failed

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Microsoft Research's open-source code-first agent framework that plans data-analytics tasks and executes LLM-generated Python in a stateful Jupyter kernel with plugins.
  • Key dates: Paper arXiv:2311.17541 (Nov 2023); repo created 11 Sep 2023; released on GitHub 30 Nov 2023; last feature update 13 Mar 2025 (vision input for Planner); archived 23 Mar 2026.
  • Evidence: GitHub (verified 2026-11-19): repo is a 'Public archive' with 'Archived: Yes', 6,156 stars, 777 forks, 657 commits, only 2 releases — the latest a 0.0.2-alpha from 13 May 2024; last README news item is vision-input support on 13 Mar 2025, and directory checks put the last commit on 23 Mar 2026, the archive date. Microsoft's newer Agent Framework is described as the successor consolidating AutoGen and Semantic Kernel, effectively superseding TaskWeaver.
  • Why: It remained a research project with explicitly limited maintainer resources and only two alpha releases, never reaching a stable release or a pip package. Microsoft consolidated its agent efforts into the newer Microsoft Agent Framework (successor to AutoGen and Semantic Kernel), superseding TaskWeaver. Its narrow data-analytics niche and code-execution security burden limited broader adoption, so it was archived in March 2026 rather than sustained.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its code-first, stateful execution model that preserves both chat and code/data history, but avoid building on a single-vendor research framework that can be archived when the vendor consolidates around a successor.
  • Sources: https://github.com/microsoft/TaskWeaver; https://microsoft.github.io/TaskWeaver/docs/overview/?ref=thestack.technology; https://www.thestack.technology/microsoft-taskweaver-ai-framework/; https://geekflare.com/open-source/ai-agent-frameworks/

18. Alibaba AgentScope — In Progress

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Alibaba Tongyi Lab's open-source (Apache 2.0), production-ready multi-agent framework built around ReAct agents, with tool/MCP/skill management, multi-agent team orchestration, sandboxed execution, observability, and a multi-tenant FastAPI agent service.
  • Key dates: Repo created 2024-01-12; original paper submitted 2024-02-21 (arXiv:2402.14034); AgentScope 1.0 launched 2025-09-02 (paper arXiv:2508.16279, Aug 2025); AgentScope 2.0 released in 2026; v2.0.12 released 2026-11-16. No shutdown, acquisition, or pivot.
  • Evidence: GitHub (crawled 2026-11-19): 36,246 stars, 3,886 forks, 52 releases, 1,013 commits, Apache 2.0, not archived; 896 commits in the last 52 weeks per RepoRadar. Latest release v2.0.12 on 2026-11-16 (v2.0.11 on 2026-10-27, v2.0.10 on 2026-10-17); 171,000 PyPI downloads in the week ending 2026-11-16, up 223.4% over the prior 30 days, and +2,802 stars in the last 30 days. Also expanded to a Java version (AgentScope Java 2.0 GA July 2026, ~6,237 stars) and a QwenPaw personal-assistant app built on it.
  • Why: It has sustained because of durable corporate backing from Alibaba Tongyi Lab, a permissive open-source license, and model-agnostic design spanning OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, DashScope/Qwen, DeepSeek and local models, plus production features (observability, sandboxing, multi-tenancy, A2A/MCP support) that kept it competitive through major rewrites. Adoption kept compounding into late 2026 — stars and PyPI downloads were still accelerating in November 2026 — rather than stalling after the 1.0 release. The main cost has been churn: the 1.x-to-2.0 refactor broke APIs and invalidated older tutorials, and late-2026 security advisories show the fast-moving surface still needs hardening.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its trust/visibility design — ReAct core, unified event bus streaming reasoning and tool calls, fine-grained permissions and human-in-the-loop, sandboxed workspaces, and multi-tenant agent service — but avoid its 1.x-to-2.0 breaking API refactor without a smoother migration path.
