  • Sources: https://github.com/agentscope-ai/agentscope; https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14034; https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16279; https://agentgid.com/agents/agentscope/

19. Anthropic Model Context Protocol (MCP) — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: MCP is an open, JSON-RPC-based standard that lets AI applications connect to external tools, data sources and prompts through reusable MCP servers instead of bespoke per-system integrations.
  • Key dates: Launched/open-sourced 25 Nov 2024 by Anthropic; OpenAI added support Mar 2025, Google/Gemini Apr 2025, Microsoft Copilot Studio GA May 2025; official MCP Registry preview 8 Sep 2025; donated 9 Dec 2025 to the Agentic AI Foundation under the Linux Foundation; current specification 2026-07-28.
  • Evidence: At donation in Dec 2025 Anthropic reported 10,000+ active public MCP servers and 97M+ monthly SDK downloads, with adoption in ChatGPT, Gemini, Microsoft Copilot, Cursor, VS Code and Claude; by July 2026 Tier 1 SDK downloads were ~500M/month, with TypeScript and Python SDKs each past 1B total downloads. The current specification is dated 2026-07-28 and the official registry counted ~59,506 latest-version records in a Nov 8, 2026 snapshot (preview catalog, so a snapshot not a stable total).
  • Why: It succeeded because it solved a concrete N×M integration problem with a simple, open standard at the moment agents needed tool access, then won cross-vendor adoption from OpenAI, Google and Microsoft within months. Neutral governance — donation to the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation in Dec 2025 — removed single-vendor lock-in risk and cemented it as shared infrastructure, while remaining risks are security/trust around arbitrary tool execution rather than adoption.
  • Relevance to HIVE: HIVE should copy MCP's approach: adopt a single open, vendor-neutral tool/data protocol with a registry and SDKs instead of building bespoke N×M integrations for every agent and tool.
  • Sources: https://www.anthropic.com/news/model-context-protocol?ref=dataphoenix.info; https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28; https://slash-digital.io/en/insights/mcp-explained/; https://agnost.ai/blog/mcp-agents-md-join-agentic-ai-foundation/

20. Microsoft Semantic Kernel multi-agent — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: Microsoft's open-source, MIT-licensed SDK for building AI agents and multi-agent systems with plugins, connectors, memory, and orchestration patterns in .NET, Python, and Java.
  • Key dates: Launched 17 March 2023; multi-agent Agent Framework introduced 1 August 2024, RC 28 February 2025, GA 4 April 2025; Microsoft Agent Framework public preview October 2025 and 1.0 GA 3 April 2026 as the successor unifying Semantic Kernel and AutoGen; Semantic Kernel v1.x supported for at least one year after Agent Framework GA.
  • Evidence: GitHub microsoft/semantic-kernel: 28,786 stars, 4,865 forks, 283 releases, 5,195 commits, not archived, as crawled 19 Nov 2026; recent releases Python 1.47.0 on 5 Nov 2026 and .NET 1.81.0 on 6 Oct 2026; Microsoft's own README calls Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0 the enterprise-ready successor with stable APIs, long-term support, multi-agent orchestration, and A2A/MCP interoperability, while Semantic Kernel v1.x continues to receive bug and security fixes.
  • Why: It succeeded through Microsoft/Azure enterprise distribution, strong .NET type safety, dependency injection, telemetry, and a broad connector/plugin ecosystem, making it a credible choice for embedding copilots and multi-agent workflows in existing enterprise apps. It was nevertheless superseded as a standalone strategic direction because it overlapped and fragmented the community with AutoGen, and its multi-agent orchestration was verbose and partly experimental, requiring developers to hand-roll turn-taking and termination. Microsoft therefore consolidated both lineages into Microsoft Agent Framework, carrying Semantic Kernel's enterprise plumbing forward rather than abandoning it.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Semantic Kernel's enterprise plumbing and ready-made orchestration patterns (sequential, concurrent, group chat, handoff) plus MCP/A2A interop, and avoid maintaining two overlapping frameworks or forcing developers to hand-roll agent turn-taking and termination.
  • Sources: https://github.com/microsoft/semantic-kernel; https://atlan.com/know/ai-agent/microsoft/semantic-kernel/; https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/discussions/azure/building-multi-agent-orchestration-using-microsoft-semantic-kernel-a-complete-st/4507660; https://alexbevi.com/blog/2026/06/18/two-lineages-one-framework-how-autogen-and-semantic-kernel-became-the-microsoft-agent-framework/

21. IBM BeeAI / watsonx Orchestrate — In Progress

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: IBM's paired agentic stack: BeeAI is the open-source Python/TypeScript framework for building production multi-agent systems, and watsonx Orchestrate is IBM's commercial enterprise platform for building, orchestrating, governing, and observing those agents at scale.
  • Key dates: BeeAI began internally at IBM Research in early 2024 (repo created 2024-08-23); donated to the Linux Foundation in March 2025; ACP merged into Google's A2A protocol in August 2025; watsonx Orchestrate agent-building/ADK tech preview 30 April 2025, agentic relaunch with 150+ agent catalog 6 May 2025, agentic workflows GA 7 October 2025; next-generation Orchestrate announced as private preview 5 May 2026.
  • Evidence: BeeAI Framework: ~3,436 GitHub stars, 171 releases, v0.1.86 released 2026-11-02, ~3,226 stars/425 forks and 12,647 npm downloads in the month to 26 April 2026; watsonx Orchestrate: 150+ pre-built agents (May 2025) and 500+ tools (Oct 2025), with new releases Dec 2025 and Feb 2026 (Software Hub 5.3.0/5.3.1). IBM vendor claims of 40% accuracy improvement over RAG and up to 67% time savings are unverified, and independent reviews note few documented production deployments outside IBM.
  • Why: It survives on IBM's enterprise distribution, governance/observability (AgentOps), and open-protocol bets — donating BeeAI and ACP to the Linux Foundation and converging on A2A/MCP — rather than on developer mindshare. Adoption lags LangGraph (~30k stars) and CrewAI (~30k stars) in a crowded framework market, the framework was still 0.x in November 2026 with rapid, breaking iteration, and the next-generation Orchestrate was only in private preview in May 2026, so it is actively sustained but not yet widely adopted.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the open-protocol interop (A2A/MCP) plus a governance/observability control plane over heterogeneous agents, but avoid betting HIVE on a single vendor's 0.x framework — abstract agents behind standard protocols and pin versions.
  • Sources: https://www.ibm.com/think/news/year-agentic-ai-center-stage-2025?lnk=thinkhpaic4us; https://newsroom.ibm.com/2026-05-05-think-2026-ibm-delivers-the-blueprint-for-the-ai-operating-model-as-the-ai-divide-widens; https://github.com/i-am-bee/beeai-framework; https://newsroom.ibm.com/2025-10-07-ibm-unveils-new-software-product-and-intelligent-infrastructure-capabilities-to-help-enterprises-operationalize-ai?asPDF=1

22. Salesforce Agentforce — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Salesforce's enterprise platform for building, deploying, and governing autonomous AI agents that act on CRM and business data across service, sales, marketing, and operations.
  • Key dates: Unveiled September 12, 2024 at Dreamforce; generally available October 2024; Agentforce 2.0 December 2024; 2dx March 2025; Agentforce 3 June 23, 2025; Agentforce 360 generally available October 13, 2025; new Core/Advanced/Max editions at Dreamforce September 2026.
  • Evidence: Salesforce reported Agentforce ARR of ~$800 million in Q4 FY26 (quarter ended Jan 2026), up 169% year over year, with 29,000+ cumulative deals and 2.4 billion agentic work units; combined Agentforce + Data 360 ARR was $2.9 billion. By Q2 FY27 (quarter ended July 31, 2026) Agentforce annualized revenue exceeded $1.5 billion, up over 240% year over year. At the Agentforce 360 launch Salesforce cited 12,000 customers; Reddit reported 46% advertiser-support case deflection and resolution time cut from 8.9 to 1.4 minutes. Figures are vendor-reported, not independently audited.
  • Why: It succeeded primarily through distribution and grounding rather than novel agent research: agents were embedded in Salesforce's existing CRM, Data Cloud/Data 360, permissions, and workflows, so customers did not need a new system of record. Bounded autonomy with topics/subagents, guardrails, the Einstein Trust Layer, and later Command Center observability, testing, and MCP interoperability in Agentforce 3 made enterprise deployment governable, while iterative releases and consumption pricing ($2/conversation, later Flex Credits) lowered adoption friction. The main caveat is uneven depth of adoption — many deployments remain bounded pilots rather than end-to-end autonomous workflows.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Agentforce's formula of grounding agents in a trusted data layer, bounding autonomy with explicit permissions and guardrails, and shipping observability/testing from the start; avoid selling unbounded autonomy before production retention and deep adoption are proven.
  • Sources: https://www.salesforce.com/news/press-releases/2025/10/13/agentic-enterprise-announcement/; https://cyntexa.com/blog/agentforce-statistics-and-trends/; https://valueaddvc.com/blog/salesforce-rebrand-reversal-why-agentforce-branding-got-walked-back; https://www.grazitti.com/blog/salesforce-launches-agentforce-3-0-heres-everything-you-need-to-know-about-the-upgrade/

23. UiPath Agentic Automation — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: UiPath's enterprise platform that unifies AI agents, RPA robots, APIs, and people under Maestro orchestration with governance, audit, and human-in-the-loop controls.
  • Key dates: UiPath founded 2005; agentic platform private preview Jan 2025; UiPath Platform for Agentic Automation launched Apr 30, 2025, with Maestro orchestration and Agents GA in May 2025; expanded offerings Sep 30, 2025; WorkFusion acquired Feb 2026; on-premises agentic AI on Automation Suite shipped Apr 15, 2026 / announced May 5, 2026; Maestro Flow GA Sep 2026.
  • Evidence: Publicly traded UiPath (NYSE: PATH) reported FY2026 revenue of $1.611 billion (+13% YoY) and ARR of $1.853 billion (+11% YoY), with its first full year of GAAP profitability; secondary reporting of FY2026 results cites AI product ARR of nearly $200 million, 90% of $1M+ ARR customers using AI products, and 16 of the top 20 Q4 deals including AI products. The platform was named one of TIME Magazine's Best Inventions of 2025.
  • Why: It succeeded commercially because it layered agents onto an existing large RPA installed base and distribution channel rather than starting from zero, and because it paired non-deterministic agent reasoning with deterministic robot/API execution and enterprise governance that regulated buyers require. Continued 2025-2026 releases — Maestro Case/Flow, ScreenPlay, on-premises agentic AI, and vertical solutions such as financial crime compliance — show adoption expanding from pilots into operating-model use inside existing customers.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy UiPath's orchestration-first model — agents for judgment, deterministic robots/APIs for execution, humans in the loop, and built-in governance/audit — and avoid selling standalone agents without that execution and trust layer.
  • Sources: https://ir.uipath.com/news/detail/388/uipath-launches-the-first-enterprise-grade-platform-for-agentic-automation; https://www.uipath.com/newsroom/uipath-accelerates-ai-transformation-with-agentic-automation-and-orchestration; https://ir.uipath.com/news/detail/446/uipath-automation-suite-delivers-on-premises-agentic-ai-for-the-public-sector; https://idp-software.com/vendors/uipath/

24. Devin (Cognition AI) — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: An autonomous AI software engineer from Cognition that takes a ticket, plans, codes, tests and debugs in its own cloud sandbox, and returns a pull request.
  • Key dates: Cognition founded Aug 2023; Devin unveiled Mar 12, 2024; generally available Dec 10, 2024; Devin 2.0 Apr 2025; Windsurf acquired Jul 14, 2025, rebranded Devin Desktop Jun 2, 2026; Series D May 27, 2026 ($1B+ at $26B); Series E Sep 8, 2026 ($2B+ at $48B); SWE-2 Sep 10, 2026; SWE-2 Flash Nov 4, 2026
  • Evidence: Launched with 13.86% on SWE-bench (Mar 2024, ~2x prior best); Cognition reports PR merge rate rose from 34% to 67%, with ~326,500 public PRs opened by Devin's GitHub bot and ~206,800 merged (~63% overall, ~82% of closed PRs). Commercially sustained: $492M annualized run-rate revenue in May 2026, claimed to have crossed $1B on Sep 25, 2026; raised $1B+ at a $26B valuation May 27, 2026 and $2B+ at a $48B valuation Sep 8, 2026; enterprise customers include Goldman Sachs, Mercedes-Benz, NASA, Itaú and Cognizant; 2.9% of Stack Overflow 2026 respondents reported using it. Still actively developed in 2026: Devin Review (Jan 2026), Devin 2.2 (Feb 2026), Devin Desktop/Windsurf rebrand (Jun 2, 2026), SWE-2 model (Sep 10, 2026, 50.0% FrontierCode 1.1 Main, vendor-reported) and SWE-2 Flash (Nov 4, 2026). Note: revenue and benchmark figures are company-reported, not independently audited.
  • Why: Devin succeeded by narrowing autonomy to scoped, verifiable engineering work — a sandboxed shell/editor/browser plus test suites and CI as the acceptance check — rather than open-ended autonomy, and by iterating after its disputed 2024 demo (merge rate 34% to 67%) while cutting entry pricing from $500 to $20/month. Its model-agnostic harness plus in-house SWE models, dogfooding (Cognition claims ~90% of its own code is written by Devin), enterprise go-to-market, and the Windsurf acquisition expanding it into an IDE/CLI/review suite turned a viral demo into a sustained commercial platform.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Devin's delegation-plus-verification pattern — scoped tasks, sandboxed execution, tests/CI as the acceptance gate, and human-reviewed PRs — and avoid launching on a hyped demo before the verification loop and pricing make real adoption possible.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition_AI; https://old.cognition.ai/blog/series-d; https://www.software-lifecycle.com/software/devin; https://www.testmuai.com/blog/devin-review/

25. LangChain Agents ecosystem — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: An open-source Python/JavaScript framework for building LLM agents, now centred on LangGraph as its stateful multi-agent runtime and LangSmith as its commercial observability, deployment and governance platform.
  • Key dates: Founded as an open-source project Oct 2022 (repo created 17 Oct 2022, v0.0.1 on 25 Oct 2022); company co-founded early 2023; LangGraph first released 8 Jan 2024; Deep Agents launched 29 Jul 2025; LangChain 1.0 and LangGraph 1.0 GA 22 Oct 2025; $300M Series C announced 13 Nov 2026.
  • Evidence: About $460M total funding: $10M seed Apr 2023 (Benchmark), $25M Series A Feb 2024 (Sequoia), $125M Series B 20 Oct 2025 at $1.25B (IVP), $300M Series C 13 Nov 2026 at $3B (CapitalG). ~147,000 GitHub stars for langchain and ~42,000 for langgraph as of Sep 2026; ~90M combined monthly PyPI downloads reported Oct 2025; company claims 1,000+ integrations, 35% of Fortune 500 as users, 7,000+ LangSmith organisations and 1B+ events/day; production use cited at Uber, LinkedIn, Klarna, Cisco and Replit; LangChain's State of Agent Engineering survey (1,340 respondents, Nov-Dec 2025) found 57% had agents in production.
  • Why: It was first to market in Oct 2022, five weeks before ChatGPT, and won on breadth — 1,000+ model, tool and data integrations made it the default starting point. When early chains were criticised as opaque and over-abstracted, it pivoted the agent layer onto LangGraph's durable, checkpointed, human-in-the-loop graph runtime and stabilised APIs in 1.0 (Oct 2025), turning a demo toolkit into production infrastructure. Commercially it monetised that infrastructure via LangSmith (observability, evals, deployment, governance) rather than the open-source libraries, sustaining a unicorn-scale business while the core remained MIT-licensed and actively developed.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy LangGraph's durable stateful graph runtime with checkpointing, human-in-the-loop and full trace observability/evals, but avoid LangChain's early over-abstraction and API churn — keep the agent API thin and stable from v1.
  • Sources: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/13/ai-agent-startup-langchain-hits-3b-valuation/; https://www.taskade.com/blog/langchain-history; https://atlan.com/know/ai-agent/ai-agent-memory/what-is-langchain/; https://javisreview.com/review/langgraph-review-2026/

26. Mistral Mixtral 8x7B / 8x22B — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Mistral AI's Apache 2.0 open-weight sparse mixture-of-experts LLMs — Mixtral 8x7B (46.7B total parameters, 12.9B active per token) and Mixtral 8x22B (141B total, 39B active) — routing each token to 2 of 8 experts.
  • Key dates: Mixtral 8x7B weights leaked via torrent 9 Dec 2023 and formally announced 11 Dec 2023; Mixtral 8x22B weights released 10 Apr 2024 and formally announced 17 Apr 2024; both deprecated 30 Nov 2024 and retired from Mistral's hosted API 30 Mar 2025, replaced by Mistral Small 4, while open weights remain available.
  • Evidence: At launch Mistral reported 8x7B outperformed Llama 2 70B on most benchmarks, matched or exceeded GPT-3.5, and scored 8.30 on MT-Bench (Instruct); 8x22B added a 64k context and native function calling. Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct-v0.1 had 24.1M all-time Hugging Face downloads and ~302,669 downloads in the 30 days to 9 Nov 2026, showing continued self-hosted use after hosted retirement.
  • Why: It succeeded technically and commercially because sparse MoE delivered near-frontier quality at roughly one-third the active compute of dense models of similar total size, under a permissive Apache 2.0 licence that drove mass fine-tuning and deployment. Its hosted retirement in 2025 was planned lifecycle supersession, not failure: Mistral's own newer Small/Large models and the wider frontier moved past it within ~15 months, so Mistral retired the hosted endpoints while leaving the weights usable.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy sparse expert routing — small active cost per task with specialist experts — but design experts as replaceable, versioned modules so HIVE is not locked to a single frozen checkpoint when a better model supersedes it.
  • Sources: https://docs.mistral.ai/models/mixtral-8x7b-0-1; https://docs.mistral.ai/models/mixtral-8x22b-0-1-0-3; https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/v5.1.0/model_doc/mixtral; https://tools8020.com/blog/mistral-ai-statistics/

27. Google Switch Transformer — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: A Google Brain sparse Mixture-of-Experts Transformer that routes each token to exactly one expert, scaling to 1.6 trillion parameters at near-constant per-token compute.
  • Key dates: arXiv posted 2021-01-11 (arXiv:2101.03961); published in Journal of Machine Learning Research 23(120), April 2022; no shutdown — a research architecture, not a product, so no end date.
  • Evidence: Switch-C reached 1.6 trillion parameters with 2,048 experts, trained on 32 TPU cores on the C4 corpus, with up to 7x pre-training speedup over dense T5 baselines at the same compute and a 4x speedup over T5-XXL; code and checkpoints released via google-research/t5x and Mesh TensorFlow. Its top-1 routing, capacity factor, and load-balancing design became the baseline for the whole modern MoE lineage: Google's GLaM (1.2T params, 2021) and ST-MoE (2022), then Mixtral 8x7B (2024), DeepSeek-V2/V3 (671B total, 37B active), and Gemini's MoE designs.
  • Why: It succeeded by radical simplification: replacing noisy top-k MoE gating with top-1 routing plus an auxiliary load-balancing loss, float32 router precision, and expert capacity limits, which eliminated the training instability that had blocked trillion-parameter sparse models. The timing was ideal — it arrived just as the field hit dense-scaling cost walls — and its design was simple enough to be reimplemented everywhere, so successors (GLaM, Mixtral, DeepSeek) refined rather than replaced it.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its lesson that the simplest possible routing (top-1, one expert per token) beats cleverer multi-expert schemes on stability, communication cost, and scaling — but avoid assuming experts specialize semantically, since later analyses show routing correlates more with syntax/position than topic.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2101.03961; https://www.jmlr.org/papers/v23/21-0998.html; https://www.hpcwire.com/bigdatawire/2021/02/08/googles-new-switch-transformer-model-achieves-1-6-trillion-parameters-efficiency-gains/

28. Google GLaM — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Google's Generalist Language Model (GLaM) is a sparsely activated mixture-of-experts language-model family whose largest model has 1.2 trillion parameters but activates only about 97 billion per token.
  • Key dates: Paper submitted 13 Dec 2021; revised 1 Aug 2022; accepted to ICML 2022; superseded by PaLM (Apr 2022) and later Gemini (Dec 2023, MoE-based from Gemini 1.5 in Feb 2024); no GLaM follow-up or shutdown notice found.
  • Evidence: Largest GLaM: 1.2T total parameters, 64 experts, only 96.6B (~8%) active per token via top-2 gating; trained on 1.6T tokens, using 456 MWh vs 1,287 MWh for GPT-3 (about one-third the training energy) and about half the inference FLOPs, while outperforming GPT-3 across 29 NLP tasks in zero/one-shot settings. It was a proprietary research model — never sold or released as weights/API/product — with no follow-up GLaM release after the paper's v2 revision on 1 Aug 2022.
  • Why: GLaM proved MoE scaling worked, but it was a research-only demonstration, never productized or open-released, so it gained no users or ecosystem. Google moved its frontier effort to dense PaLM and then to multimodal Gemini, which internalized MoE ideas, leaving GLaM with no maintenance, successor, or deployment path — a technical success superseded by market timing and product strategy rather than by a flaw in sparse routing itself.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy GLaM's sparse top-2 expert routing — activate a small subset per token to scale capacity without proportional compute — but avoid building a named model that is research-only with no product, API, or maintenance path.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.06905v2; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_large_language_models; https://theaidatabase.com/m/glam-google.html

29. DeepSeek-V3 / DeepSeekMoE — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: DeepSeek-V3 is a 671B-parameter open-weights Mixture-of-Experts LLM (37B active per token) built on the DeepSeekMoE architecture, released by DeepSeek in December 2024.
  • Key dates: DeepSeekMoE paper: 11 Jan 2024; DeepSeek-V3 released: 26 Dec 2024; V3-0324 refresh (MIT licence): 25 Mar 2025; V3.1: 21 Aug 2025; V3.2: 1 Dec 2025; superseded on DeepSeek's API by V4 (24 Apr 2026), legacy V3 API names discontinued 24 Jul 2026.
  • Evidence: Trained on 14.8T tokens using 2.788M H800 GPU-hours for a reported $5.576M; vendor-reported MMLU 88.5, MATH-500 90.2%, AIME 2024 39.2%, SWE-bench Verified 42-64% depending on version/evaluation; checkpoint still pulled >1.1M times/month from Hugging Face with ~104,000 GitHub stars for the V3 repo, and DeepSeek repos overall exceeded 170,000 stars in 2025; its successor R1, built on V3, triggered a ~$589B single-day Nvidia market-cap loss on 27 Jan 2025.
  • Why: It succeeded technically and economically by combining fine-grained expert segmentation and shared experts (DeepSeekMoE), Multi-head Latent Attention, auxiliary-loss-free load balancing and FP8 training to match GPT-4o/Claude 3.5 Sonnet at roughly one-tenth the reported training cost, proving sparse MoE scaling. Adoption was driven by open weights, a permissive licence from V3-0324 onward, and very low API pricing, making it a default self-hosted and third-party-hosted frontier-class model. The original V3 checkpoint itself is now superseded — removed from DeepSeek's own API in 2026 in favour of V3.2/V4 — but that is succession after success, not failure: the MoE architecture and lineage remain in active use.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy DeepSeekMoE's lesson: use fine-grained, sparsely-activated specialists with a shared expert and auxiliary-loss-free routing so HIVE can scale capability without activating (or paying for) the whole system per task.
  • Sources: https://www.tellworld.net/models/deepseek-v3; https://deepseekai.guide/reviews/deepseek-v3-review/; https://www.beri.net/tools/deepseek-v3; https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437

30. Alibaba Qwen2.5-MoE / Qwen3-MoE — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Alibaba's Qwen mixture-of-experts LLMs — hosted-only Qwen2.5-Max in the Qwen2.5 generation, then open-weight Qwen3-30B-A3B (30B total/3B active) and Qwen3-235B-A22B (235B total/22B active, 128 experts, 8 active per token).
  • Key dates: Qwen2.5 launched 19 Sep 2024 (dense open weights); Qwen2.5-Max MoE announced 28-29 Jan 2025 (hosted/API only, weights not released); Qwen3 launched 28-29 Apr 2025 with open MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B and Qwen3-235B-A22B under Apache 2.0; Qwen3-2507 refresh 21 Jul 2025; superseded as flagship by Qwen3-Max Sep 2025 and Qwen3.5 16 Feb 2026
  • Evidence: Qwen family reached 942.1M cumulative Hugging Face downloads by March 2026, passing Meta's Llama in Sept 2025 (325.4M vs 323.7M), with 200,000+ derivative models and ~69% of new HF derivatives Qwen-based by Feb 2026. Qwen3-235B-A22B (thinking mode, vendor-reported) scored 85.7 on AIME'24, 81.5 on AIME'25 and 70.7 on LiveCodeBench v5, beating DeepSeek-R1 on 17/23 benchmarks in Alibaba's technical report; Qwen2.5-Max was claimed to beat DeepSeek-V3 and GPT-4o on Alibaba's reported benchmarks. Note: Qwen2.5's open weights were dense (0.5B-72B); its MoE was the hosted-only Qwen2.5-Max (size undisclosed, >20T tokens).
  • Why: MoE sparse activation delivered near-flagship reasoning at roughly 22B-active inference cost, while Apache 2.0 licensing, a 0.6B-to-235B size ladder, and broad vLLM/Ollama/Hugging Face support made Qwen the default fine-tuning base. Alibaba sustained it with a fast release cadence and cloud/API monetisation of the closed Max tier, even as individual checkpoints were quickly superseded by Qwen3.5/3.6/3.8 — the success is the lineage and ecosystem, not any single checkpoint.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy sparse MoE routing (large total capacity, small active cost per token) plus a permissive license and a full size ladder to become the default base others build on; avoid treating any single checkpoint as permanent — plan for rapid supersession within the same lineage.
  • Sources: https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen2.5/; https://www.tellworld.net/models/qwen3; https://siliconangle.com/2025/01/29/alibaba-unveils-qwen-2-5-max-ai-model-saying-outperforms-deepseek-v3/; https://metaprompt.ai/posts/chinese-open-weight-models-by-the-numbers

31. JetMoE — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: JetMoE-8B is an open-source sparsely-gated Mixture-of-Experts LLM from MyShell and MIT that applies sparse activation to both attention heads and feedforward layers, activating only 2.2B of its 8B parameters per token.
  • Key dates: GitHub repo created 2024-04-02; arXiv paper submitted 2024-04-11 (arXiv:2404.07413); last repo push 2024-04-04; no successor release, shutdown notice, or acquisition since.
  • Evidence: Trained for under $0.1M (~$0.08M, 30,000 H100 hours) on 1.25T tokens; JetMoE-8B has 8B parameters with only 2.2B active per token, scored 53.0 average on the Open LLM Leaderboard (beating Llama2-7B at 51.0 and DeepSeekMoE-16B at 51.1), and JetMoE-8B-Chat scored 6.681 on MT-Bench vs 6.650 for Llama-2-13B-Chat. GitHub repo has 987 stars, 77 forks, only 13 commits and 0 releases, with last push 2024-04-04; the model remains supported in Hugging Face Transformers and is still cited in 2025-2026 MoE routing research, but there has been no new release or follow-up model.
  • Why: It was a one-shot academic demonstration rather than a product: it proved low-cost MoE training was possible, but there was no follow-up model, commercial deployment, or maintenance roadmap. It was quickly superseded by stronger open MoE models (notably DeepSeek-MoE and later Qwen/Llama MoE variants), and its sponsor MyShell pivoted toward its AI-agent platform and SHELL token, leaving the JetMoE repo untouched since April 2024.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy JetMoE's sparse activation of both attention and feedforward experts to cut inference cost ~70%, but avoid depending on a one-off paper model as a foundation — pin or fork it, since upstream maintenance has stopped.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2404.07413v1/; https://github.com/myshell-ai/JetMoE; https://huggingface.co/jetmoe/jetmoe-8b-chat; https://awesome.ecosyste.ms/projects/github.com%2Fmyshell-ai%2FJetMoE

32. OLMoE (Allen Institute for AI) — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: OLMoE is Ai2's fully open sparse mixture-of-experts language model (7B total / ~1B active parameters per token) released with its weights, training data, code, logs, and 244 checkpoints.
  • Key dates: Paper posted Sep 3, 2024; OLMoE-1B-7B-0924 released Sep 2024 (jointly with Contextual AI); ICLR 2025 (Oral); updated OLMoE-1B-7B-0125 released Jan 2025; on-device iOS app launched Feb 11, 2025; no new OLMoE generation since Jan 2025
  • Evidence: OLMoE-1B-7B: 6.9B total parameters, only ~1.3B active per token (64 experts per layer, top-8 routed), pretrained on 5.1T tokens with 244 intermediate checkpoints released. Paper reports MMLU 54.1, beating Llama2-7B (46.2) and surpassing Llama2-13B-Chat and DeepSeekMoE-16B; accepted as an ICLR 2025 Oral (top 1.8% of submissions). January 2025 OLMoE-1B-7B-0125 re-post-training with the Tulu 3 recipe raised average eval score from 38.44 to 45.62 with the same base model; the 0125-Instruct checkpoint has tens of thousands of Hugging Face downloads. Ai2 also shipped an on-device iOS app (Feb 11, 2025, macOS Mar 7, 2025) running OLMoE offline, and OLMoE serves as the language backbone of MolmoE-1B. It remains cited in 2026 research, though Ai2's newer flagship work moved to dense OLMo 2 (Nov 2024) and OLMo 3 (Nov 2025), with no new OLMoE generation after Jan 2025.
  • Why: It succeeded as a research artifact because it was the first fully open MoE — closed rivals released weights at most, while Ai2 published data, code, logs, and every checkpoint — and because its small active size made frontier-style MoE research cheap and runnable on-device. Its ceiling was architectural and market timing: a 1B-active model was quickly outclassed by larger dense models (Ai2's own OLMo 2/3) and frontier MoEs, so it endures as a widely used research baseline and backbone rather than as an actively extended product line.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy OLMoE's sparse top-k expert routing plus full openness (weights, data, code, logs, checkpoints) for reproducible, cheap-to-run specialists — but avoid treating one small MoE as a durable product, since Ai2 itself moved on to newer dense backbones.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2409.02060v1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Institute_for_AI; https://huggingface.co/collections/allenai/olmoe-january-2025; https://www.llmobile.news/ticker/olmoe/

33. Databricks DBRX — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Databricks' open-weight, fine-grained mixture-of-experts LLM with 132B total / 36B active parameters, released in March 2024 as a general-purpose enterprise model.
  • Key dates: MosaicML acquired by Databricks June/July 2023 for ~$1.3B; DBRX launched March 27, 2024; retired from pay-per-token April 30, 2025; retired from provisioned throughput December 19, 2025
  • Evidence: 132B total / 36B active parameters, 16 experts with 4 active per token, pre-trained on 12T tokens over 2.5 months on 3,072 NVIDIA H100s for ~$10M; at launch beat Llama 2 70B, Mixtral-8x7B and Grok-1, with MMLU 73.7% vs GPT-3.5 70.0% and HumanEval 70.1% vs 48.1%. Databricks retired it from Foundation Model APIs pay-per-token on April 30, 2025 and provisioned throughput on December 19, 2025, recommending Llama 4 Maverick / comparable Llama models; no DBRX successor was announced, and a secondary source reports its weights were no longer retrievable from Databricks' Hugging Face org as of July 8, 2026.
  • Why: DBRX was rapidly superseded by newer open models such as Llama 3/4 and Qwen, eroding its benchmark lead within a year, while Databricks shifted strategy from building its own foundation model to serving third-party models and building its Mosaic AI agent/platform layer. Maintaining and serving a 132B model offered little durable advantage once frontier open weights improved faster and cheaper, so Databricks retired it in two stages in 2025 rather than shipping a DBRX 2.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy DBRX's fine-grained MoE efficiency lesson (many small experts, few active) for cost-effective routing, but avoid betting HIVE on a single monolithic foundation model that can be superseded within a year — build the orchestration, evaluation and migration layer instead.
  • Sources: https://www.databricks.com/company/newsroom/press-releases/databricks-launches-dbrx-new-standard-efficient-open-source-models; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DBRX; https://docs.databricks.com/aws/en/release-notes/product/2025/april; https://docs.databricks.com/gcp/en/machine-learning/retired-models-policy#retired

34. xAI Grok-1 MoE — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: xAI's first large language model, a 314-billion-parameter Mixture-of-Experts model whose raw base weights and architecture were open-released under Apache 2.0.
  • Key dates: Launched 3 Nov 2023; open-sourced under Apache 2.0 on 17 Mar 2024; superseded by Grok-1.5 (announced 29 Mar 2024, released 15 May 2024); GitHub repo last pushed 30 Aug 2024
  • Evidence: 314B total parameters (8 MoE experts, 2 active per token, ~25% active), 64 layers, 8,192-token context; open weights are ~318 GB and need a multi-GPU server (~628 GB at BF16). GitHub xai-org/grok-1 has 52,241 stars and 8,531 forks but only 9 commits and its last push was 2024-08-30; Wikipedia lists Grok-1 as Discontinued, succeeded by Grok-1.5, with Grok-2/3/4/4.7 following as closed proprietary models.
  • Why: Grok-1 was rapidly superseded by Grok-1.5 in spring 2024 and then by closed Grok-2/3/4 models, with no active maintenance of the Grok-1 repo since August 2024. The release was a raw base checkpoint, not fine-tuned for dialogue, and its 314B size made practical self-hosting prohibitively expensive, so newer open-weight models surpassed it for real use. xAI's strategy also pivoted away from openness: no Grok generation from Grok-3 onward has been open-released.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy sparse MoE routing to activate only a fraction of experts per token, but avoid shipping a frozen, untuned base checkpoint with no serving stack or maintenance plan.
  • Sources: https://github.com/xai-org/grok-1; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grok_AI; http://x.ai/blog/grok-os?ref=tinyalternatives; https://techcrunch.com/2024/03/17/xai-open-sources-base-model-of-grok-but-without-any-training-code/

35. Snowflake Arctic — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Snowflake's open-weights, Apache 2.0 enterprise LLM family built on a dense-MoE hybrid architecture (480B total parameters, 17B active per token via 128 experts), optimized for SQL generation, coding, and instruction following.
  • Key dates: Launched April 24, 2024; Arctic Embed 2.0 released December 4, 2024; Arctic-TILT published July 2025; Arctic-Text2SQL-R1 released May 29, 2025; Arctic RL work ongoing into September 2026; no shutdown or abandonment.
  • Evidence: Shipped on launch day via Snowflake Cortex, Hugging Face, AWS, Azure, NVIDIA API catalog, Perplexity and Together AI; trained in under 3 months for roughly under $2M compute (about one-eighth the cost of comparable models, per Snowflake); original model scored 79% on Spider SQL benchmark, beating DBRX (76.3%); successor Arctic-Text2SQL-R1-32B reached 71.83% execution accuracy on BIRD (May 2025), the top score of any open or proprietary model at the time, with the 7B variant open-weighted; Arctic Embed 2.0 covers 74 languages with 8K context.
  • Why: It succeeded commercially because Snowflake embedded it in Cortex, its governed data platform serving 9,400+ organizations, rather than selling a standalone frontier model, and its Apache 2.0 openness plus published training recipes built enterprise trust. The original 480B generalist itself lost frontier relevance — a 4K context window and 224GB+ VRAM requirement made it heavy and dated versus newer models — so Snowflake pivoted the family toward small, cheap specialists (Text2SQL, Embed, TILT) where narrow models beat general-purpose giants at a fraction of the size and cost.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Arctic's playbook: ship a narrow specialist that beats generalists on one verifiable benchmark (e.g., SQL/BIRD) with open weights and recipes, and embed it inside a governed platform rather than chasing a frontier generalist.
  • Sources: https://www.snowflake.com/en/news/press-releases/snowflake-launches-arctic-the-most-open-enterprise-grade-large-language-model-2/; https://www.snowflake.com/en/blog/engineering/arctic-text2sql-r1-sql-generation-benchmark/; https://www.snowflake.com/en/blog/engineering/snowflake-arctic-embed-2-multilingual/

36. Microsoft Phi-3.5-MoE — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Microsoft's open-weight Mixture-of-Experts small language model with 16 experts, 42B total parameters but only 6.6B active per token, and a 128K-token context window.
  • Key dates: Launched August 2024 (Phi-3.5 family, 20-23 Aug 2024); succeeded by Phi-4 on 12 Dec 2024; deprecated in GitHub Models on 11 Sep 2025, with users directed to Phi-4-mini.
  • Evidence: Trained on 4.9T tokens on 512 H100 GPUs over 23 days; Microsoft benchmarks: MMLU 78.9, GSM8K 88.7, average 69.2 across representative benchmarks, ahead of Llama-3.1-8B (61.0) and near Gemini-1.5-Flash (68.5); MIT-licensed open weights, Azure serverless API Sep 2024 and LoRA fine-tuning from 1 Nov 2024; by Sep 2026 trackers listed it as deprecated/retired from Azure AI Foundry/GitHub Models with no current hosted price, though the weights remain downloadable and supported in Transformers/SGLang.
  • Why: It worked technically but was superseded within four months: the dense 14B Phi-4 offered stronger reasoning in a far smaller, simpler-to-serve footprint, undermining the MoE variant's small-model value proposition despite its sparse activation. Microsoft's rapid Phi cadence then consolidated development around Phi-4 and its reasoning/multimodal variants, leaving Phi-3.5-MoE with no new releases or hosted maintenance. MoE training and inference complexity, noted by Microsoft at launch, added cost without a durable quality-per-dollar advantage over the successor.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy sparse expert activation for cost-efficient specialist routing, but avoid depending on a single vendor model generation — design experts as swappable modules with a planned migration path so a deprecated generation does not strand the system.
  • Sources: https://huggingface.co/microsoft/Phi-3.5-MoE-instruct; https://github.blog/changelog/2025-09-11-deprecated-microsoft-models-in-github-models; https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/announcing-the-availability-of-phi-3-5-moe-in-azure-ai-studio-and-github/4256278; https://seminal.ai/open-source-large-language-models/microsoft-phi-3-5-moe-instruct/

37. Meta Llama 4 Maverick (MoE) — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Meta's open-weight, natively multimodal Mixture-of-Experts LLM with 17B active parameters out of 400B total across 128 experts and a 1M-token context window.
  • Key dates: Launched April 5, 2025; no successor or meaningful update since May 2025; superseded by closed-weight Muse Spark on April 8, 2026; Groq deprecated it March 9, 2026; Meta's Llama API retired July 6, 2026; AWS Bedrock deprecated it September 30, 2026.
  • Evidence: Meta-reported benchmarks: MMLU-Pro 80.5, GPQA Diamond 69.8, LiveCodeBench 43.4; launch LMArena Elo of 1417 was for an experimental chat-tuned variant, not the public weights (~1370, ~32nd place). Hugging Face downloads total about 760,466 all-time with about 13,000 in the last 30 days as of November 2026, and Artificial Analysis scored it 9.99 on Intelligence Index v4.3.2 in October 2026, near the bottom of the open-weight field. Weights remain downloadable under the Llama 4 Community License and were not formally deleted, but Meta has not shipped a Llama 4.5 or Llama 5, Behemoth never shipped, and Muse Glimmer (August 10, 2026) became Meta's new open model.
  • Why: Maverick shipped as a credible frontier open-weight model but was rapidly overtaken by DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi and Gemma rivals while Meta issued no follow-up release, so its benchmarks and hosted availability decayed. Meta then pivoted its frontier effort to closed-weight Muse Spark and reorganised around Meta Superintelligence Labs, retiring the Llama API and letting major hosts deprecate Maverick. The root cause was therefore market timing and strategic pivot rather than a single technical failure: MoE efficiency delivered a good cost/performance ratio at launch, but without sustained iteration it could not hold the frontier.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Maverick's MoE efficiency idea — activating only a small expert subset per token — but avoid shipping a flagship once and letting it stagnate; HIVE needs continuous iteration and a hosted path, or it will be superseded the same way.
  • Sources: https://ai.meta.com/blog/llama-4-multimodal-intelligence/; https://www.frankx.ai/blog/llama-4-analysis-2026; https://www.spheron.network/blog/meta-shut-down-the-llama-api-self-hosting-llama-is-now-offic/; https://codersera.com/blog/llama-4-complete-guide-2026/

38. Tencent Hunyuan-Large (MoE) — Failed

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Tencent's open-weight 389B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts language model (52B parameters activated per token), released in November 2024 as the largest open Transformer-based MoE model at the time.
  • Key dates: Paper submitted to arXiv 4 Nov 2024 (arXiv:2411.02265); open-sourced 5 Nov 2024; superseded by Hy3 preview Apr 2026 / Hy3 Jul 2026 and Hy4 preview Aug 2026 / Hy4 Nov 2026; weights remain downloadable, no formal shutdown.
  • Evidence: 389B total / 52B activated parameters; 256K-token context (128K instruct); pretrained on 7T tokens incl. ~1.5T synthetic. Tencent-reported benchmarks: MMLU 88.4% (pretrain) / 89.9% (instruct) vs Llama 3.1 405B's 85.2%/87.3%; MATH 69.8%/77.4%. Open weights (Pretrain, Instruct, Instruct-FP8) on Hugging Face and code on GitHub since 5 Nov 2024. Model catalogs now list it as 'Superseded, still available'; Tencent's successor open MoEs Hy3 (295B/21B, Jul 2026) and Hy4 preview (770B/49B, Aug 2026, Apache 2.0) have replaced it as the flagship.
  • Why: It succeeded at launch as a scale and efficiency proof-point, but was overtaken within two years: DeepSeek-V3 (Dec 2024) reset the open-MoE frontier, and Tencent itself rebuilt its training stack and replaced Hunyuan-Large with the cheaper, agent-optimised Hy3 and then the 770B Hy4 line. The specific checkpoint received no further releases or maintenance, so it survives only as downloadable legacy weights rather than a living model line.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its sparse-MoE economics (huge total capacity, only ~13% of parameters active per token) and open-weights distribution, but avoid betting HIVE's core on a single giant checkpoint — plan for generational supersession and keep routing/abstraction layers model-swappable.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02265; https://huggingface.co/tencent/Tencent-Hunyuan-Large; https://www.avocolab.com/ai-models/tencent; https://www.tellworld.net/news/hunyuan-large-2024

39. Google Gemini MoE architecture — Succeeded

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Google Gemini is a natively multimodal, sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Transformer family in which a learned router activates only a small subset of expert sub-networks per token, decoupling total model capacity from per-token compute.
  • Key dates: Gemini 1.0 launched Dec 2023; Gemini 1.5 Pro, the first public sparse-MoE Gemini, announced 15 Feb 2024 with technical report arXiv:2403.05530 on 8 Mar 2024; Gemini 2.5 in Mar 2025; Gemini 3 Pro launched 18 Nov 2025; Gemini 1.5 Pro API alias retired 24 Sep 2025, while the MoE architecture continued into Gemini 2.5/3.
  • Evidence: Gemini 1.5 reported >99% needle-in-a-haystack retrieval up to 1M tokens and research tests to 10M tokens, with 1M-token production context; Gemini 3 Pro topped LMArena at 1501 Elo at launch; Gemini app grew from 400M MAU in May 2025 to 650M in Oct 2025 and crossed 1B MAU on 11 Aug 2026, Google's fastest-growing product. Exact expert counts, top-k values and total parameter counts for recent versions are not publicly disclosed by Google.
  • Why: It succeeded technically and economically because sparse routing let Google scale total capacity, multimodality and 1M-token context without a proportional rise in per-token inference cost, making flagship capability servable at scale on TPU infrastructure. Adoption was driven less by the architecture alone than by distribution: Gemini shipped day-one in Search AI Mode, the Gemini app, Android, AI Studio and Vertex AI. The main costs are MoE-specific engineering complexity — routing/load-balancing, all-expert memory footprint and training instability — which Google absorbed with its infrastructure rather than eliminating.
  • Relevance to HIVE: HIVE should copy sparse specialist routing with explicit load-balancing and a shared multimodal backbone so capacity can grow without per-task compute exploding, but avoid treating experts as independently deployable agents without solving routing, memory and fallback when an expert is unavailable.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05530; https://blog.google/products-and-platforms/products/gemini/gemini-3/; https://blog.bytebytego.com/p/chatgpt-vs-gemini-vs-claude-how-they; https://thegenevatimes.ch/googles-gemini-app-surpasses-1-billion-monthly-users-becoming-fastest-growing-product-ever/

40. Baidu ERNIE MoE — In Progress

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: Baidu's ERNIE family of sparse Mixture-of-Experts foundation models, in which a router activates only a small subset of experts per token to deliver large-model capability at much lower inference cost.
  • Key dates: ERNIE family originated 2019; ERNIE 4.5 and reasoning model ERNIE X1 launched 16 Mar 2025; ERNIE 4.5 open-sourced 30 Jun 2025; ERNIE X1.1 Sept 2025; ERNIE 5.0 previewed 13 Nov 2025 (proprietary); ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base released 5 Nov 2026; ERNIE 5.5 Flash Preview 11 Nov 2026
  • Evidence: ERNIE 4.5 open-sourced 30 June 2025 as 10 Apache 2.0 models, largest 424B total / 47B active MoE; ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base released 5 Nov 2026: 280B total / 14B active, 512 routed experts (top-10) + 1 shared expert, pre-trained on 33.5T tokens, 128K training / 1M inference context, with Baidu-reported MMLU 93.3 and MMLU-Pro 78.4 vs DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base; ERNIE 5.5 Flash Preview followed 11 Nov 2026. PaddlePaddle/ERNIE ecosystem reported at 23.33M developers and 760,000 enterprises in Sept 2025. Benchmarks are vendor-reported; 5.5 Base uses a custom licence excluding EU/UK/South Korea.
  • Why: MoE efficiency is the technical driver: 280B total parameters with only 14B active per token makes frontier-scale models cheap to serve, and Baidu reversed its closed strategy after DeepSeek-driven price competition, open-sourcing ERNIE 4.5 while keeping ERNIE 5.0/5.1 proprietary. Adoption remains strongest in China, constrained outside it by Chinese-language documentation, mainland verification for full Qianfan access, weaker English reasoning/coding versus Western frontier models, and restrictive licensing on the newest 5.5 weights.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy sparse MoE routing with a small active-parameter budget and a base-checkpoint-first release for domain adaptation, but avoid Baidu's split open/closed tiering and region-restricted licensing that fragment adoption.
  • Sources: https://huggingface.co/baidu/ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Base; https://www.tellworld.net/news/ernie-4-5-open-source-2025; https://nextomoro.com/ernie/

41. LoRA / PEFT ecosystem (Hugging Face) — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: LoRA freezes a pretrained model's weights and trains only small low-rank adapter matrices, and Hugging Face PEFT is the standard open-source library implementing LoRA, QLoRA, DoRA and many other parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods.
  • Key dates: LoRA paper by Hu et al. (Microsoft Research) June 2021, published ICLR 2022; Hugging Face PEFT repo created 2022-11-25; QLoRA May 2023; PEFT v0.19.0 2026-04-14, v0.20.0 2026-07-28, v0.21.2 2026-10-01
  • Evidence: PEFT on GitHub (crawled 2026-11-19): 21,908 stars, 2,674 forks, 2,096 commits, 37 releases, Apache 2.0, not archived; v0.21.2 released 2026-10-01, v0.20.0 on 2026-07-28 added nine new adapter methods and v0.19.0 on 2026-04-14 added eight more. LoRA paper reported 10,000x fewer trainable parameters and ~3x less GPU memory vs full fine-tuning of GPT-3 175B, with comparable performance; PEFT README example trains only 0.1193% of Qwen2.5-3B parameters (3.69M of 3.09B) and a T0-3B LoRA checkpoint is 19MB vs 11GB full model.
  • Why: It succeeded technically and economically: the low-rank-update insight cut trainable parameters by orders of magnitude with near-full-fine-tuning quality and no added inference latency once merged, making fine-tuning feasible on consumer/single GPUs. Standardization through Hugging Face PEFT, plus integration with Transformers, Diffusers, Accelerate and TRL and serving stacks like vLLM, made adapters portable, shareable on the Hub, and swappable at runtime. Adoption compounded because tiny adapter files (MBs vs GBs) lowered storage, distribution and multi-task serving costs, turning LoRA into the default fine-tuning recipe.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the adapter model: keep one shared base and swap tiny, versioned specialist adapters per agent/task instead of full-model copies, with hot-swapping and merging at deploy time.
  • Sources: https://github.com/huggingface/peft; https://huggingface.co/docs/peft/en/index; https://research.modelcitizendeveloper.com/survey/1-208/; https://temperature2.com/p/2026-10-03-did-you-know-lora-qlora-fine-tuning/

42. AdapterHub / AdapterFusion — In Progress

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: AdapterHub is a framework and shared hub for parameter-efficient adapters that plug small trainable modules into frozen Transformers, and AdapterFusion is its two-stage method for combining multiple task-specific adapters via a learned fusion layer.
  • Key dates: Repo created 2020-04-21; AdapterHub paper EMNLP 2020 and AdapterFusion arXiv May 2020 / EACL Apr 2021; successor Adapters library paper 2023; original adapter-transformers archived and Hub migrated to Hugging Face; v1.1.0/v1.2.0 in 2025; adapters 1.3.0 on PyPI Apr 26, 2026
  • Evidence: GitHub adapter-hub/adapters: 2,833 stars, 373 forks, 26 releases, not archived; integrates 10+ adapter methods into 20+ Transformer models. PyPI lists adapters 1.3.0 (Apr 26, 2026); v1.1.0 added AdapterPlus and one-line save/load/push of full adapter compositions including AdapterFusion, and the latest GitHub release targets Transformers v4.48.x. Original adapter-transformers package was archived/migrated, with its Hub repository moved to the Hugging Face Model Hub.
  • Why: It survived because it was a thin add-on to Hugging Face Transformers rather than a competing platform, with a unified interface, reusable pre-trained adapters, and academic maintenance from TU Darmstadt/UKP Lab. Its ceiling was set by economics and adoption: mainstream fine-tuning consolidated around LoRA and Hugging Face PEFT, while AdapterFusion's extra fusion-training step added cost and complexity for marginal gains over simpler merging or stacking. The result is a sustained research niche, not a failed project: still maintained in 2026, but no longer the default adapter stack.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy frozen, swappable specialist modules plus an explicit, saveable composition/fusion layer, but avoid AdapterFusion's per-combination fusion-training cost and tight coupling to one upstream framework version.
  • Sources: https://github.com/adapter-hub/adapters; https://pypi.org/project/adapters/1.3.0; https://adapterhub.ml/blog/2020/11/adapting-transformers-with-adapterhub/; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2005.00247

43. Mixture-of-Agents (MoA, Together AI paper) — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: A layered inference-time method in which multiple LLM proposers independently answer a prompt and aggregator LLMs synthesize their outputs into a single improved response, with no fine-tuning.
  • Key dates: arXiv submitted 7 Jun 2024; GitHub repo created 4 Jun 2024; Together AI blog 11 Jun 2024; published at ICLR 2025; Together docs updated 2 Nov 2026; no shutdown or pivot reported.
  • Evidence: Open-source-only MoA scored 65.1% on AlpacaEval 2.0 vs 57.5% for GPT-4 Omni (+7.6pp), MT-Bench 9.65 vs 9.32, and also led on Arena-Hard and FLASK; reference repo togethercomputer/MoA has ~2,996 stars and 384 forks under Apache 2.0, and Together AI's MoA documentation was updated 2026-11-02 with current models (e.g. GLM-5.3, Kimi-K3), showing the pattern remains in active use as a documented API recipe.
  • Why: It succeeded academically because it exploited 'collaborativeness' — models improve when conditioned on other models' outputs — achieving state-of-the-art results with only open-source models and a simple, reusable reference implementation. It did not become a standalone product because it trades compute for quality: a dozen-plus model calls per prompt, high time-to-first-token, and roughly 2x the cost of a comparable single frontier call, while later stronger single models and Self-MoA (sampling one strong model) narrowed its advantage. Its durable outcome is therefore as a proven aggregation pattern, not as a maintained platform.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy MoA's layered proposer-aggregator synthesis for high-value, checkable outputs where quality justifies extra calls, but avoid it as a default interactive path because of its latency and token cost.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04692; https://github.com/togethercomputer/MoA; https://docs.together.ai/docs/mixture-of-agents; https://www.together.ai/blog/together-moa?format=webp

44. FrugalGPT / LLM routing (Stanford) — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Stanford's FrugalGPT is a cost-aware LLM cascade that tries cheap models first and escalates to expensive ones only when a learned scorer judges the answer unreliable.
  • Key dates: arXiv submitted 9 May 2023; GitHub repo created 23 Jun 2023; published in Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR) 2024; last repo push 10 Feb 2025; no shutdown/acquisition announced
  • Evidence: Paper reports matching GPT-4 with up to 98% cost reduction on HEADLINES (50-98% across tasks) or +4% accuracy at equal cost, evaluated across 12 commercial LLM APIs; repo is Apache 2.0 with 293 stars, 39 forks and 55 commits, but last push was 2025-02-10 and last changelog entry 2025-02-09 (support for Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Gemini 1.5 Pro, GPT-4o, Llama 3.1), with no formal shutdown and no release/commit in the ~21 months to Nov 2026. The idea remains cited in 2026 routing surveys, while the code itself is dormant.
  • Why: It was a completed research artifact, not a funded product, so maintenance stopped once the paper and 2024-2025 model evaluations were done. The cascade also needs labeled in-distribution data to train its scorer and adds latency when it escalates, while falling frontier-model prices and newer one-shot routers (e.g. RouteLLM) and commercial routing platforms superseded the specific implementation even as they preserved its core idea.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the cheap-first, quality-gated cascade with a hard per-task budget, but avoid maintaining a bespoke scorer — use a modern learned router or commercial routing and validate savings in shadow mode on HIVE's own traffic.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.05176; https://github.com/stanford-futuredata/FrugalGPT; https://zylos.ai/research/2026-05-06-ai-agent-multi-model-orchestration-runtime-selection/

45. RouteLLM — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: An open-source LMSYS/UC Berkeley framework that trains lightweight routers on Chatbot Arena preference data to send each query to a cheap weak model or an expensive strong model via an OpenAI-compatible API.
  • Key dates: Repo created 3 Jun 2024; paper submitted 26 Jun 2024 (arXiv:2406.18665, revised 23 Feb 2025); LMSYS blog 1 Jul 2024; presented at ICLR 2025; last commit 10 Aug 2024; no releases or commits since.
  • Evidence: GitHub lm-sys/RouteLLM: 5,717 stars, 455 forks, 175 commits, Apache-2.0, 0 releases, not archived; last commit 10 Aug 2024 — over 2 years stale as of Nov 2026. Paper benchmarks: >85% cost reduction on MT Bench, 45% on MMLU, 35% on GSM8K vs GPT-4-only at 95% of GPT-4 quality, and >40% cheaper than commercial routers (Martian, Unify) at equal performance.
  • Why: It succeeded academically but was a research project with no commercial maintainer or funding to keep retraining it. Its pretrained routers were frozen against the mid-2024 GPT-4/Mixtral model pair, so rapid model releases silently invalidated their training data, and active gateways such as LiteLLM superseded it as the maintained routing layer.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its preference-trained, threshold-calibrated strong/weak routing for cost savings, but avoid shipping a frozen learned router — retrain continuously or use explicit tiers with fallbacks so new models do not silently invalidate routing.
  • Sources: https://github.com/lm-sys/RouteLLM; https://www.lmsys.org/blog/2024-07-01-routellm/; https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18665; https://www.routeplane.app/blog/2026-07-model-router-graveyard/

46. Sakana AI evolutionary model merging — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: An evolutionary method that automatically searches parameter-space (weights) and data-flow-space (layers) combinations of existing open-source specialist models to create new merged foundation models without gradient-based training.
  • Key dates: Sakana AI founded July 2023; Evolutionary Model Merge launched 21 Mar 2024 (arXiv 19 Mar 2024); published in Nature Machine Intelligence 28 Jan 2025; M2N2 presented at GECCO'25, July-Aug 2025; extended in ICLR 2026 AC/DC paper; no shutdown, acquisition or pivot away announced.
  • Evidence: EvoLLM-JP, a 7B merged Japanese/math model, surpassed some previous 70B Japanese LLMs on Japanese benchmarks; the method was published in Nature Machine Intelligence on 28 Jan 2025 and implemented in mergekit and Optuna Hub. It was extended by M2N2 at GECCO'25 (runner-up best paper, Aug 2025) and by the ICLR 2026 AC/DC paper, which coevolves LLMs via model merging. Sakana AI itself raised a Series B reported at $135M in Nov 2025 at a $2.65B valuation, later updated by the company in Apr 2026 to about $200M for the round and about $412M cumulative funding at a $2.7B valuation, with about 157 staff in June 2026.
  • Why: It succeeded technically because evolutionary search (notably CMA-ES) found cross-domain merges human experts missed, at far lower compute than training from scratch, and was validated by peer review and adoption in mergekit. It has not become a standalone commercial product because it only works on open-weight models, not closed frontier API models, and each candidate merge needs costly fitness evaluation, so gains diminish against frontier models. Sakana has therefore kept the technique as an ongoing research component and lifted the same evolutionary machinery to higher layers, including collective-intelligence orchestration in 2026, rather than scaling merging alone.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy fitness-driven merging of specialist models/adapters with explicit evaluation as a cheap capability-composition layer, but avoid making merging the product itself — it only works on open-weight models and Sakana has moved the same evolutionary idea up to inference-time orchestration.
  • Sources: https://sakana.ai/evolutionary-model-merge/; https://sakana.ai/m2n2/; https://arxiv.org/html/2403.13187v1; https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/file/449a016a6ce6fba3fe50d05482abf836-Paper-Conference.pdf

47. Meta Llemma / DeepSeek-Math (math specialist models) — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: Open math-specialist language models created by continued pretraining on large math corpora — Llemma (EleutherAI/Princeton, built on Meta's Code Llama, not a Meta model) and DeepSeekMath (DeepSeek, 7B, later V2) — for competition math and formal theorem proving.
  • Key dates: Llemma launched 16 Oct 2023 (arXiv rev. 15 Mar 2024); DeepSeekMath paper 5 Feb 2024, v3 27 Apr 2024; DeepSeekMath-V2 launched 27 Nov 2025; no formal shutdown for either line.
  • Evidence: Llemma (7B/34B, Proof-Pile-2, 55B tokens) outperformed open models and Minerva on an equi-parameter basis on MATH; DeepSeekMath-7B scored 51.7% on MATH without tools (60.9% with 64-sample self-consistency) and 88.2% on GSM8K, from a 120B-token math corpus; its GRPO algorithm was later used to train DeepSeek-R1; DeepSeekMath-V2 (27 Nov 2025, ~685B, Apache 2.0) reached gold-level IMO 2025 and CMO 2024 and 118/120 on Putnam 2024. Llemma itself has had no new model since its Hugging Face updates in Feb-Apr 2024, and the original 7B DeepSeekMath is superseded by V2 and by general reasoning models.
  • Why: Specialization worked because math has verifiable answers and proofs, so curated corpora plus RL with outcome/verifier rewards (notably GRPO) produced large, measurable gains at small scale. The standalone specialists then faded as general reasoning models absorbed the same recipe: Llemma was left as a frozen 2023-2024 research artifact, while DeepSeek folded math into its larger R1/V3 reasoning line and revived the name only for the much larger self-verifying V2 in 2025.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy domain continued-pretraining plus a verifiable reward/self-verification loop, but avoid shipping a narrow standalone specialist that a general reasoning model will absorb — build the verifier as a reusable HIVE capability instead.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.10631; https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03300; https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.22570; https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-Math

48. DeepMind AlphaFold 2/3 — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: Google DeepMind's AI system that predicts 3D protein structures from amino-acid sequence (AlphaFold 2) and, in AlphaFold 3, the joint structures of proteins with DNA, RNA, small-molecule ligands and ions.
  • Key dates: AlphaFold 1 won CASP13 Dec 2018; AlphaFold 2 won CASP14 30 Nov 2020 (median GDT 92.4), Nature paper and open code/database 15 Jul 2021, database expanded to >200 million structures Jul 2022; AlphaFold 3 published in Nature 8 May 2024, code released 11 Nov 2024; Nobel Prize in Chemistry 9 Oct 2024; dedicated AlphaFold team reported disbanded 30 Jul 2026; AlphaFold DB update Oct 2026.
  • Evidence: CASP14 (Nov 2020): median GDT 92.4, best prediction for 88 of 97 targets, comparable to experimental methods. AlphaFold 2 Nature paper (15 Jul 2021) cited nearly 43,000 times as of Nov 2025; AlphaFold 3 paper cited >9,000 times as of Nov 2025, with >=50% improvement on protein-ligand accuracy vs existing methods. AlphaFold DB (Google DeepMind/EMBL-EBI) holds >260 million predictions (>200 million entries, 214,684,311 in Jul 2022 expansion), used by >3 million researchers in 190+ countries by Nov 2025, with an Oct 2026 database update. Hassabis and Jumper shared half the 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry for protein structure prediction. Caveat: the dedicated AlphaFold team was reported disbanded on 30 Jul 2026, with most reassigned to Gemini/Isomorphic Labs and ~25% leaving, including Jumper to Anthropic — the artifact/database remain in use, but active dedicated development is in question.
  • Why: It succeeded technically by solving a narrowly scoped, 50-year grand challenge with a clear blind benchmark (CASP), using an end-to-end Evoformer (AF2) / Pairformer-plus-diffusion (AF3) architecture trained on the Protein Data Bank, and by shipping the result as a free, open database and (for AF2) open code, creating massive academic adoption. Commercialisation via Isomorphic Labs and Alphabet/EMBL-EBI sustainment funded it, but AlphaFold 3's initial code/weights restriction drew a reproducibility backlash and spawned fully open rivals, and in 2026 Google reprioritised Gemini over dedicated AlphaFold research, dissolving the original team even as the database and models remain widely used.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy AlphaFold's narrow, benchmark-verifiable specialist scope with confidence scores and a free, reusable artifact/database that creates adoption, and avoid gating the core capability for a commercial spinout or letting a flagship specialist team be absorbed into a generalist product line.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AlphaFold; https://alphafold.com; https://www.aiinsiders.net/article/deepmind-broke-up-its-nobel-winning-alphafold-team-for

49. Google Med-PaLM / MedGemma — In Progress

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: Google's medical specialist LLM lineage — Med-PaLM/Med-PaLM 2 as closed research models, succeeded by MedGemma, an open-weight Gemma 3-based family (4B multimodal and 27B text/multimodal) for medical text and image comprehension.
  • Key dates: Med-PaLM arXiv 26 Dec 2022, Nature 12 Jul 2023; Med-PaLM 2 announced Mar 2023, arXiv 16 May 2023; MedGemma launched 20 May 2025 (4B multimodal + 27B text-only), 27B multimodal 9 Jul 2025; MedLM deprecated 14 May 2025, access ended 29 Sep 2025; MedGemma 1.5 4B released 13 Jan 2026; MedGemma paper in Nature Medicine 6 Oct 2026
  • Evidence: Med-PaLM scored 67.2% on MedQA (USMLE-style), Med-PaLM 2 reached 86.5% and was preferred to physicians' answers on 8 of 9 axes; MedGemma 27B text-only scored 87.7% on MedQA (4-option), 4B scored 64.4% and 1.5 4B 69.1%. MedLM (the Med-PaLM 2 cloud product) was deprecated 14 May 2025 with access ending 29 Sep 2025. MedGemma 1.5 was released 13 Jan 2026 with 3D CT/MRI, whole-slide histopathology and EHR support, and the MedGemma paper was published in Nature Medicine 6 Oct 2026; Google states MedGemma is not yet clinical-grade and requires per-use-case validation, with no FDA clearance as a diagnostic device.
  • Why: Technically it succeeded on benchmarks, reaching expert-level MedQA scores, but clinical adoption was blocked by safety, validation and regulatory barriers — Google explicitly says MedGemma is not clinical-grade. Economically, the closed, large PaLM 2-based MedLM API was superseded: frontier general models caught up or passed it on MedQA, so Google pivoted to smaller, cheaper, open-weight Gemma-based models that hospitals and developers can run and fine-tune locally. The specialist advantage therefore shifted from a proprietary API to an open, deployable adapter rather than disappearing.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the specialist-adapter pattern — domain-tune a strong general base, ship small open weights that run on-prem, and gate claims with benchmarks plus per-use-case validation — and avoid a closed, expensive single-purpose API that frontier general models can supersede.
  • Sources: https://developers.google.com/health-ai-developer-foundations/medgemma/model-card; https://cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/release-notes; https://history.itlibra.com/en/events/med-palm-2022; https://news.longevitydocs.org/technology/medgemma-open-medical-ai-model-nature-medicine

50. StarCoder / specialist code models — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: StarCoder is BigCode's (Hugging Face + ServiceNow, later NVIDIA) family of open-weights specialist large language models for code generation and completion, trained on the permissively-licensed The Stack dataset.
  • Key dates: BigCode founded Sep 2022; StarCoder/StarCoderBase launched May 4 2023; StarCoder2 launched Feb 28 2024 (instruct variant Apr 2024); The Stack v3 released Jul 23 2026; no StarCoder3 or new StarCoder model release as of Nov 2026
  • Evidence: StarCoder (15.5B, 1T tokens, 80+ languages) scored 33.6% HumanEval pass@1 at launch and matched/surpassed OpenAI's code-cushman-001; StarCoder2 (3B/7B/15B, 3.3-4.3T tokens, 619 languages, Feb 28 2024) saw its 15B model match CodeLlama-34B. GitHub: bigcode-project/starcoder has 7.5k stars with last commit Jun 29 2023; starcoder2 has 2.1k stars. No StarCoder3 exists as of Nov 2026, and StarCoder2 is now far behind rivals (HumanEval+ 37.8% base / 63.4% instruct vs 87.2% for Qwen2.5-Coder-32B-Instruct), though it remains in Ollama, SGLang and NVIDIA NIM for low-latency fill-in-the-middle completion and its lineage continued via The Stack v3 dataset (Jul 23 2026, 114TB raw, ~5T tokens).
  • Why: The model line succeeded as a 2023-2024 open code model but froze after StarCoder2: its 16K context and base-model (non-agentic) design were outpaced by Qwen2.5-Coder and DeepSeek-Coder, which offered 128K context, much higher HumanEval scores and more permissive licensing than StarCoder's OpenRAIL-M. BigCode/Hugging Face effort shifted to the underlying data and evaluation assets — The Stack v3 in July 2026 and BigCodeBench — rather than training a StarCoder3, leaving the models usable for low-latency completion but with no meaningful model release in over 32 months and no formal shutdown.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy StarCoder's transparent, permissively-licensed training data, fill-in-the-middle training and data provenance, but avoid forking a frozen specialist model line — specialize via adapters/fine-tunes on a maintained base with a defined refresh cadence and agentic evaluation.
  • Sources: https://techcrunch.com/2023/05/04/hugging-face-and-servicenow-release-a-free-code-generating-model/?guccounter=1; https://newsroom.servicenow.com/press-releases/details/2024/ServiceNow-Hugging-Face-and-NVIDIA-launch-New-Open-Access-LLMs-Help-Developers-Tap-Generative-AI-to-Build-Enterprise-Applications-02-28-2024-traffic/; https://github.com/bigcode-project/starcoder2; https://www.deeplearning.ai/the-batch/fresh-data-for-coding-models-hugging-face-updated-the-stack-the-largest-crawl-of-public-github-repositories

51. Hugging Face model hub specialist-model ecosystem — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: A git-based open hub hosting millions of task- and domain-specialized fine-tuned models and lightweight PEFT/LoRA adapters, plus datasets and demo Spaces, all loadable through a standard Transformers interface.
  • Key dates: Founded 2016 as a chatbot, pivoted to open NLP/Transformers; Hub passed 1M public models Sep 2024, 2M Aug 2025, 3M Aug 2026; Series D Aug 2023 ($235M at $4.5B valuation); NVIDIA agreed to acquire Hugging Face for $12.93B on Sep 3, 2026, expected to close H1 2027 pending regulatory approval.
  • Evidence: 3,012,377 public models as of Aug 22, 2026, adding ~2,700-3,000 models/day and 1.18M in 2025 alone; 18M+ developers, 200,000+ companies, 500,000+ datasets, 1M+ Spaces/apps, 45.4B total Hub downloads by Oct 2025, and ~$150M ARR by Aug 2026. Specialist adapters are central: 250,000+ public LoRA/QLoRA adapter combinations were reported on the Hub in 2025, with PEFT at 25M+ monthly downloads.
  • Why: It succeeded through standardization and network effects: one-line loading, safetensors, and tiny swappable PEFT adapters collapsed the cost of publishing and reusing a specialist model, while git-based versioning, search, and an open, vendor-neutral registry attracted every major lab and independent builder. The weakness is discoverability and concentration — the top 200 models account for ~49.6% of downloads while ~85.6% of models have fewer than 200 lifetime downloads — and neutrality is now the open risk, with NVIDIA ownership pending and a reported DOJ antitrust inquiry in Oct 2026.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the standardized specialist registry with tiny swappable adapters over shared bases, versioning, and quality/discovery signals; avoid a flood of unfindable specialists and a single-owner neutrality trap.
  • Sources: https://blogs.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-to-acquire-hugging-face/; https://huggingface.co/blog/ivanfioravanti/three-million-models-and-counting; https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/v4.35.0/peft

52. Modular Deep Learning / Mixture-of-Adapters (MoA) — In Progress

  • Category: MoE/Modular LLM
  • What it is: A research programme in which a frozen foundation model is adapted by composing small, swappable specialist adapter modules — Mixture-of-Adapters (MoA) routes tokens to heterogeneous LoRA, parallel-adapter and prompt-tuning experts instead of retraining the whole model.
  • Key dates: Feb 2023: 'Modular Deep Learning' survey by Pfeiffer, Ruder, Vulic and Ponti (arXiv:2302.11529); Dec 2023: Adapters unified library (EMNLP 2023); Mar 2024/2025: SEMA mixture-of-adapters for continual learning (CVPR 2025); Apr-May 2025: SMOA (NAACL 2025); 6 Jun 2025: heterogeneous MoA preprint (Zhejiang University/Tencent); 2-7 Jul 2026: MoA published at ACL 2026 in San Diego.
  • Evidence: Academic line, not a company/product, so no funding or user numbers were verifiable. Peer-reviewed momentum in 2025-2026: SEMA (CVPR 2025) reported 74.5% average accuracy on ImageNet-R with 20 tasks vs 69.5% for the next-best method; SMOA (NAACL 2025) reported reducing active adapters by over 85% while improving task performance; heterogeneous MoA (arXiv 6 Jun 2025; ACL 2026, pp. 21056-21073) reported outperforming homogeneous MoE-LoRA methods on performance, parameter efficiency, GPU memory, training efficiency and inference speed, with code at github.com/DCDmllm/MoA. Related variants in the same period include DM-Adapter (AAAI 2025) and L-MoE (Oct 2025 preprint). No shutdown notice or abandonment was found.
  • Why: It stays in progress rather than succeeding as a product because the core technical problem is real but unsolved in general form: homogeneous MoE-LoRA experts suffer representation collapse and load imbalance, and each fix (heterogeneous MoA, SEMA, SMOA, DM-Adapter, L-MoE) is a narrow, benchmark-specific variant with its own router and composition rules. Adoption is therefore academic and fragmented — many papers, no dominant standard, library or deployed system — so progress continues through successive papers rather than through a shipped, sustained implementation.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the pattern HIVE needs most — a frozen backbone plus swappable, heterogeneous specialist adapters selected by a router — but avoid inventing bespoke routing and composition from scratch, since that fragmentation is exactly what has kept this line academic rather than productised.
  • Sources: https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.965/; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2506.05928; https://www.ruder.io/modular-deep-learning/; https://openaccess.thecvf.com/content/CVPR2025/papers/Wang_Self-Expansion_of_Pre-trained_Models_with_Mixture_of_Adapters_for_Continual_CVPR_2025_paper.pdf

53. OpenCog Hyperon / SingularityNET — In Progress

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: OpenCog Hyperon is SingularityNET's open-source neural-symbolic cognitive architecture for AGI, built around the AtomSpace knowledge metagraph and the MeTTa programming language, with SingularityNET as its decentralized AI marketplace and funding/coordination layer.
  • Key dates: Founded/launched December 2017 (SingularityNET, $36M ICO); OpenCog Hyperon Alpha April 2024; March-July 2024 ASI Alliance token merger with Fetch.ai and Ocean Protocol (AGIX/FET/OCEAN -> ASI); Hyperon Alpha 1 and ASI:Chain DevNet November 2025; roadmap targets human-level AGI by 2028 (unverified).
  • Evidence: Hyperon Alpha released April 2024; Hyperon Alpha 1 and ASI:Chain DevNet launched November 2025 at Web Summit Lisbon; hyperon-experimental (MeTTa reference implementation) at v0.2.10 with commits spanning June 2025-June 2026 and ~200+ GitHub stars; SingularityNET raised $36M in its Dec 2017 ICO in under 60 seconds, announced a $53M AI infrastructure investment in 2024, and ran $1.25M in Deep Funding grants across 14 AGI challenge areas; 20+ ecosystem portfolio projects. No verified production AGI, benchmark wins, or mass adoption — still pre-alpha/research-stage, with a published 2025-2028 roadmap (Baby Hyperon Q4 2025-Q2 2026 through Young-Adult Hyperon 2028).
  • Why: It survives on sustained token-funded research rather than product revenue: the 2017 ICO, the 2024 ASI Alliance merger, and a $53M infrastructure commitment keep a small research community building, but progress is slow because the ambition is maximal (human-level AGI by 2028) while the stack is fragmented across parallel MeTTa runtimes and the AtomSpace-Scheme legacy, and the marketplace has not produced a breakout commercial application. The root cause is coordination overhead and market timing — a bespoke language and cognitive architecture must mature before adoption, while mainstream LLM agent frameworks ship usable products today.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the neural-symbolic AtomSpace + MeTTa idea of a shared, inspectable knowledge metagraph for reasoning and memory, but avoid Hyperon's trap of building a bespoke language and three parallel, non-integrated stacks before proving one working end-to-end agent loop.
  • Sources: https://wiki.hyperon.dev/Ecosystem+SingularityNET; https://singularitynet.io/deep-funding-agi-rfp-process-and-results/; https://news.dextools.io/article/what-is-singularitynet-agix-asi-alliance-decentralized-ai-guide-2026; https://github.com/trueagi-io/hyperon-experimental

54. SOAR cognitive architecture — In Progress

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: A symbolic cognitive architecture for general intelligent agents, built around a repeating decision cycle that selects and applies operators using working, procedural, semantic and episodic memory, with chunking and reinforcement learning.
  • Key dates: Begun 1983 as John Laird's thesis at Carnegie Mellon University, created by Laird, Allen Newell and Paul Rosenbloom; moved to University of Michigan (Laird joined 1986); definitive book published 2012; v9.6.4 released 2025-07-22; v9.6.5 released 2026-05-06; no shutdown, acquisition or pivot found.
