  • Evidence: Version 9.6.5 released 2026-05-06 (assets re-uploaded 2026-05-11), following 9.6.4 on 2025-07-22; manual draft dated May 5, 2026; University of Michigan publications list includes multiple 2025 and 2026 papers, including AGI-26 and AAAI 2026 work and NL2GenSym (arXiv:2510.09355, Oct 2025) translating natural language to Soar rules via LLMs. Open-source BSD-licensed C/C++ implementation at SoarGroup/Soar, historically used in military simulations, games and robotics.
  • Why: It has survived 40+ years because its fixed decision cycle, impasse handling and distinct memory systems provide a principled, inspectable substrate for planning, learning and real-time control, sustained by academic and government-funded research at the University of Michigan. It never achieved mass commercial adoption because knowledge must largely be hand-authored as production rules, onboarding is steep and tooling such as VisualSoar is aging, while LLM-based agents lowered the entry cost for most applications. Its current adaptation is LLM-modulo integration — using LLMs for language and rule generation while Soar retains control, memory and verification — rather than competing with LLMs directly.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Soar's unified decision cycle with separate procedural, semantic and episodic memory plus impasse-driven chunking/learning, but avoid hand-authored production rules as the sole knowledge-entry path — use LLM translation into rules, as in NL2GenSym.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soar_(cognitive_architecture); https://github.com/SoarGroup/Soar/releases; http://soar.eecs.umich.edu/home/Publications/; https://soar.eecs.umich.edu/soar_manual/01_Introduction/

55. ACT-R (Adaptive Control of Thought—Rational) — Succeeded

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: A symbolic cognitive architecture from Carnegie Mellon that models human cognition as interacting modules (declarative and procedural memory, perception, motor) coordinated through buffers and production rules, implemented as a Common Lisp interpreter.
  • Key dates: Roots in Anderson's HAM model (1973) and ACT theory; renamed ACT-R with rational analysis in the early 1990s; ACT-R 4.0 released 1993 (with Christian Lebiere); ACT-R 7.0 from 2015; stable release 7.21.6 on 2020-12-21; annual workshops held continuously since 1995, including the 32nd (July 2025), a special ecosystem workshop (May 2026), and the 33rd workshop (July 2026).
  • Evidence: Used in 700+ scientific publications; ACT-R-based Cognitive Tutors used in thousands of US schools; open source under GNU LGPL v2.1; annual workshop/summer school unbroken since 1995 through July 2026; May 2026 IHMC workshop on the future of the ACT-R ecosystem drew on 104 community interviews. Caveat: the official stable release is still 7.21.6 from December 2020, so core releases have slowed even as community activity continues.
  • Why: It succeeded academically because it is an empirically validated, quantitative theory of human cognition — its ~50ms production cycle and activation-based declarative memory make falsifiable predictions about reaction times, errors, and even fMRI BOLD patterns — which kept it funded and cited for three decades. Its ceiling is engineering adoption: the Lisp implementation, steep learning curve, and human-fidelity orientation make it hard to repurpose as a general AI substrate, which is exactly why the May 2026 workshop focused on ecosystem sustainability, integration, and reusability in the LLM era.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy ACT-R's activation-based declarative memory (time decay plus frequency-of-use boosts) and its strict buffer-mediated module separation for memory retrieval and auditability; avoid its Lisp-only core and monolithic interpreter — build the same ideas as Python-native, LLM-compatible modules.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT-R; http://act-r.psy.cmu.edu/workshops; https://act-r.psy.cmu.edu/?post_type=workshops&p=35394; https://hcii.cmu.edu/project/act-r

56. CLARION cognitive architecture — In Progress

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: CLARION (Connectionist Learning with Adaptive Rule Induction On-line) is Ron Sun's hybrid cognitive architecture in which every subsystem — action-centered, non-action-centered, motivational, and metacognitive — has an explicit symbolic top level and an implicit connectionist bottom level that interact via bottom-up and top-down learning.
  • Key dates: Proposed by Ron Sun in the mid-1990s (Sun 1994/1995); book 'Duality of the Mind' 2002; CLARION 5.0 specification 2003; 'Anatomy of the Mind' (Oxford) 2016; LLM-integration papers 2024-2025; pyClarion / ICAART agent paper 2026
  • Evidence: Academically sustained for ~30 years with validation on serial reaction time, artificial grammar learning, process control, and Tower of Hanoi tasks; Ron Sun published CLARION-LLM integration work in 2024-2025, including arXiv:2509.10972 (13 Sep 2025), and a 2026 ICAART paper on building agents with CLARION; the modern Python reimplementation pyClarion is explicitly experimental, with 69 GitHub stars and 23 forks and an update noted Jan 27, 2026. No commercial product, funding round, user count, or shutdown notice was found.
  • Why: It survived as an academic theory because its implicit/explicit distinction maps well onto human psychology and it was validated against many lab tasks, but it never became a widely used engineering platform: the original Java/C++ implementation aged, the community stayed small, and its rule extraction relied on simple MLPs. The LLM era bypassed hand-built architectures, so recent work is grafting LLMs onto CLARION as the implicit layer rather than scaling CLARION itself.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy CLARION's dual explicit/implicit representation, bottom-up rule extraction, and metacognitive monitoring as design principles, but avoid adopting it as a whole platform — use LLMs for the implicit layer and explicit rules/gates for the top layer instead.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CLARION_(cognitive_architecture); https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10972v1; https://github.com/cmekik/pyClarion; https://www.csauthors.net/ron-sun/

57. LIDA (Learning Intelligent Decision Agent) — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: LIDA is a University of Memphis cognitive architecture by Stan Franklin and colleagues that implements Bernard Baars' Global Workspace Theory as repeating ~10 Hz cognitive cycles of perception, competitive attention/broadcast, and action selection with learning across multiple memory systems.
  • Key dates: IDA developed for the US Navy in the late 1990s; LIDA formalised in 2006 (Franklin & Patterson) and in Baars & Franklin 2009; Franklin died 23 January 2023; last substantive spin-off activity was the neuro-symbolic LIDA paper first published online 6 August 2025 (AGI 2025).
  • Evidence: Predecessor IDA received about $1.5M from US Navy agencies and was accepted by former Navy detailers; the official Java LIDA framework on GitHub has only 10 stars, 4 forks, 0 releases and 11 tags, with no active maintenance reported after Franklin's death. Academic work continued only at small scale, including a 2025 AGI paper pairing LIDA with MobileCLIP that reported 87.33% accuracy in a context-aware media-capture proof of concept, and a 2025 Cognitive Systems Research paper by Sean Kugele; there is no commercial product, user base, or benchmark adoption.
  • Why: Its founder Stan Franklin died in January 2023 and the core Java framework was not actively maintained afterwards, leaving a research prototype that never shipped as a product. Its hand-coded codelets, non-standard non-commercial licence and contributor agreement deterred open-source adoption, while the LLM era superseded its labour-intensive, unified-theory-first approach with narrower, shippable agent systems.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy LIDA's competitive attention-codelet broadcast and three-phase cognitive cycle (understand, attend/broadcast, act/learn), but avoid its hand-coded codelets, monolithic Java framework, and restrictive non-commercial licence.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LIDA_(cognitive_architecture); https://github.com/CognitiveComputingResearchGroup/lida-framework; https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-032-00686-8_14; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Franklin

58. Sigma cognitive architecture — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: Sigma is Paul Rosenbloom's USC Institute for Creative Technologies cognitive architecture that unifies symbolic, probabilistic, and neural cognition — memory, reasoning, learning, perception, and action — in a single factor-graph substrate using summary-product message passing and gradient-descent learning.
  • Key dates: 2009: first Sigma papers; 2013: AISB Quarterly introduction; 2016: definitive JAGI paper (Rosenbloom, Demski & Ustun); 2019: last Sigma-specific papers listed by USC; 2020-04-26: PySigma created; 2024-02-02: last PySigma push; 2025: Rosenbloom's retrospective memoir 'In Search of Insight' and Professor Emeritus status (Sept 2025) — no new Sigma release.
  • Evidence: Official USC publication list ends with Sigma-specific papers in 2019; the definitive 2016 JAGI paper is 103 pages. PySigma (Python/PyTorch port, created 2020-04-26) has 455 commits but 0 releases, 5 stars, 1 fork, and last push 2024-02-02 per GitHub API — over 2.5 years before Nov 2026. Original Sigma was BSD 2-clause, won Kurzweil Awards in 2011 and 2012, and was sponsored by the U.S. Army, AFOSR and ONR for virtual-human training, but no commercial deployment, user base, or funding figures were verifiable.
  • Why: Its single-substrate elegance was also its bottleneck: everything had to go through the same message-passing machinery, making specialised fast paths and practical engineering hard, while the original Lisp system and its incomplete Python 3.7-era PyTorch port never became a usable product. A small academic team, aimed at virtual-human research rather than a commercial or open-source community, could not sustain it, and the LLM era then absorbed attention and funding that might have carried a graphical-architecture revival.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Sigma's discipline of a small set of reusable mechanisms and a fast (~50 ms) cognitive cycle, but avoid betting HIVE's core on one elegant unified formalism or a research prototype with a tiny community — keep HIVE modular with fast paths and LLM-native tooling.
  • Sources: https://cogarch.ict.usc.edu/; https://github.com/TownesZhou/PySigma; https://cogarch.ict.usc.edu/publications-new/; https://reference-global.com/download/article/10.1515/jagi-2016-0001.pdf?preview=1

59. NARS (Non-Axiomatic Reasoning System) — In Progress

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: A general-purpose AGI cognitive architecture by Pei Wang that reasons with Non-Axiomatic Logic (NAL) in the language Narsese, learning and acting under insufficient knowledge and resources.
  • Key dates: Invented mid-1980s by Pei Wang; PhD dissertation 1995 (Indiana University); NARS 4.1 in 2000; OpenNARS open-source implementations thereafter; ONA v0.9.3 on 22 April 2025; NARS Workshop at AGI-25 in August 2025; Non-Axiomatic Logic 2nd edition published 2025; no shutdown or acquisition.
  • Evidence: OpenNARS for Applications (ONA) v0.9.3 released 22 April 2025; repo had ~121 stars and ~1,753 commits, last updated 30 May 2025 per GitHub listings, with no 2026 code release found. Academic activity continued into 2026: a Frontiers in Artificial Intelligence review of Machine Psychology with NARS, a Temple PhD dissertation on genetic encoding for NARS (May 2026), and Pei Wang's updated NARS introduction (August 2026). Secondary surveys report NASA JPL collaboration/first-responder use and funding from Digital Futures, Cisco and NASA, but no commercial product, user count, or mainstream benchmark result was verifiable.
  • Why: After 30+ years it remains an academically sustained research project rather than a product: its symbolic term-logic approach never achieved LLM-scale performance or a breakout benchmark, and funding has been academic/grants-level rather than commercial. Adoption is limited to a small research community and niche robotics demos, so progress is steady but slow — code releases paused after May 2025 while theory and papers continued into 2026.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its AIKR principle and experience-grounded truth values (frequency + confidence) for resource-bounded, revisable reasoning under uncertainty, but avoid betting HIVE's core on a symbolic-only system with no LLM-scale adoption or commercial traction.
  • Sources: https://cis.temple.edu/~pwang/NARS-Intro.html; https://github.com/opennars/OpenNARS-for-Applications/releases; https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/artificial-intelligence/articles/10.3389/frai.2026.1775040/full; https://cis.temple.edu/tagit/events/workshop2025/

60. LeCun JEPA / Meta V-JEPA world model — In Progress

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: A self-supervised world-model architecture proposed by Yann LeCun in which an encoder and predictor forecast future observations in abstract latent representation space instead of generating pixels or tokens, embodied in Meta's open V-JEPA 2 video model and its action-conditioned robot-planning variant V-JEPA 2-AC.
  • Key dates: June 2022: LeCun proposes JEPA in 'A Path Towards Autonomous Machine Intelligence'; June 2023: I-JEPA; Feb 2024: V-JEPA; June 11, 2025: V-JEPA 2 released (arXiv:2506.09985); Nov 2025: LeCun leaves Meta; March 2026: V-JEPA 2.1 released and AMI Labs announces $1.03B seed round
  • Evidence: V-JEPA 2 (1.2B params, trained on 1M+ hours of video + 1M images) scored 77.3% top-1 on Something-Something v2 and 39.7 recall@5 on Epic-Kitchens-100 action anticipation; V-JEPA 2-AC, fine-tuned on only 62 hours of robot video, achieved 65-80% zero-shot pick-and-place success on Franka arms in unseen labs and plans one action in ~16s vs ~4 min for a pixel-space baseline. Code and checkpoints are open (facebookresearch/vjepa2, ~4,600 GitHub stars, MIT license, last commit 2026-03-23); V-JEPA 2.1 followed in March 2026. LeCun left Meta in Nov 2025 and his AMI Labs raised $1.03B at a $3.5B valuation in March 2026 to pursue JEPA. Caveat: on Meta's own IntPhys 2 physical-reasoning benchmark, models score at or near chance vs 85-95% for humans, and robot results are lab-only with no commercial product.
  • Why: JEPA remains a research program, not a product: it is technically promising — a 1.2B-parameter model beats far larger pixel-generating models on motion understanding and robot planning with far less data — but physical reasoning is still near chance on Meta's own benchmarks, long-horizon planning degrades, and robot control needs a goal image rather than language instructions. Its survival is driven by open weights, strong academic adoption, and massive funding: LeCun's AMI Labs raised $1.03B in March 2026 specifically to continue JEPA, and Meta kept shipping follow-ups (V-JEPA 2.1) after his departure. The risk is market timing — LLMs dominate commercial AI while JEPA has no shipped product — so its payoff depends on sustained research funding rather than near-term revenue.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy JEPA's latent-space prediction idea — predict abstract state, not pixels or tokens — for cheap, fast world-model planning in HIVE agents, but avoid betting the whole system on it until physical-reasoning benchmarks improve.
  • Sources: https://ai.meta.com/blog/v-jepa-2-world-model-benchmarks/; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09985; https://github.com/facebookresearch/vjepa2; https://quantumzeitgeist.com/world-models-lecun-post-llm/

61. DeepMind AlphaZero / MuZero — Succeeded

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: A pair of DeepMind general reinforcement-learning systems that master games by self-play plus Monte Carlo tree search, with MuZero planning using a learned model of the environment instead of being given the rules.
  • Key dates: AlphaZero: arXiv preprint Dec 2017, peer-reviewed in Science Dec 2018; MuZero: arXiv preprint 19 Nov 2019, Nature paper Dec 2020; MuZero in production for YouTube VP9 compression Feb 2022; AlphaDev (AlphaZero descendant) faster sorting/hashing algorithms announced Jun 2023; no shutdown — lineage continues via variants (MuZero Unplugged, Sampled/Gumbel/Stochastic MuZero) and DeepMind's mctx library.
  • Evidence: AlphaZero mastered chess in 9 hours, shogi in 12 hours and Go in 13 days, becoming the strongest player in history at each; MuZero matched AlphaZero in Go, chess and shogi without being told the rules and set state of the art on 57 Atari games; MuZero launched on a portion of YouTube live traffic with an average 4% bitrate reduction (6.28% compressed-size reduction vs libvpx two-pass VBR on 3,062 clips in the associated paper); AlphaDev's faster sorting/hashing algorithms are used trillions of times a day per DeepMind; DeepMind's mctx JAX library still ships AlphaZero/MuZero/Gumbel MuZero policies, and community reimplementations (e.g. muzero-general) remain active.
  • Why: It succeeded because a single general recipe — self-play reinforcement learning plus search — replaced game-specific engineering, so the same algorithm transferred across games and then to real optimization problems (video compression, chip design, algorithms). MuZero's learned latent model removed the last hand-coded dependency (the rules/simulator), making planning applicable to messy real-world settings where dynamics are unknown. The lineage stayed alive because DeepMind kept generalizing it (AlphaDev, MuZero variants) rather than shipping it as a fixed product, though it was never a standalone commercial product itself.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the core lesson — learn a compact model of only what matters for decisions (value, policy, reward) and plan/search over it, rather than reconstructing the full world or hard-coding rules — and avoid treating any single game-style benchmark as the product; the value came from transferring the planning recipe to real optimization tasks.
  • Sources: https://deepmind.google/research/alphazero-and-muzero/; https://deepmind.google/blog/muzero-alphazero-and-alphadev-optimizing-computer-systems/; https://github.com/google-deepmind/mctx; https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-020-03051-4

62. IBM Watson (Jeopardy / Watson Health) — Failed

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: IBM Watson was a DeepQA natural-language question-answering system that beat Jeopardy! champions in 2011 and was then commercialised as Watson Health, an enterprise clinical-decision-support and health-data platform.
  • Key dates: 2004-2006 conceived/developed (DeepQA); Feb 14-16, 2011 Jeopardy win; Feb 2013 first commercial use (lung cancer, Memorial Sloan Kettering); Apr 2015 Watson Health launched; 2017 MD Anderson project cancelled; Jan 21, 2022 sale to Francisco Partners announced, closed Jun 30, 2022 and rebranded Merative; May 2023 watsonx successor launched
  • Evidence: Jeopardy win Feb 14-16, 2011: Watson $77,147 vs Ken Jennings $24,000 and Brad Rutter $21,600, winning $1M prize; 90 Power 750 servers, 2,880 POWER7 threads, 200M pages / 4TB. Watson Health launched Apr 2015; IBM spent >$4B acquiring Phytel, Explorys, Merge ($1B) and Truven ($2.6B). MD Anderson Oncology Expert Advisor cost $62.1M by Aug 2016 and was cancelled in 2017 with no clinical use; STAT (2018) reported internal documents showing 'unsafe and incorrect' oncology recommendations from training on synthetic cases. IBM announced sale Jan 21, 2022 and closed Jun 30, 2022, selling Watson Health data/analytics assets to Francisco Partners for ~$1B ($1.065B cash disclosed), rebranded Merative. IBM relaunched the brand as watsonx in May 2023, with watsonx Orchestrate expanded at Think 2026, but that is a successor foundation-model platform, not the original Watson Health system.
  • Why: Technically, DeepQA's retrieval-and-ranking approach excelled at well-formed quiz clues but did not generalise to messy, heterogeneous clinical records, and Watson for Oncology was trained largely on synthetic/hypothetical cases, producing unsafe or incorrect recommendations. Economically and organisationally, IBM oversold the Jeopardy demo as near-ready cancer care while spending over $4B on acquisitions it never integrated into a working product, and hospitals found the system mismatched to real workflows and EHRs. The result was cancelled flagship partnerships, loss of clinical trust, and a fire-sale exit at roughly a quarter of acquisition cost, with IBM pivoting to the watsonx foundation-model platform instead.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Watson's narrow, measurable demo discipline, but avoid HIVE overselling a benchmark win as general competence — validate on real workflows with real data and safety checks before marketing or clinical/high-stakes claims.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watson_(computer); https://history.itlibra.com/en/events/watson-health-sale-2022; https://newsroom.ibm.com/2026-05-05-think-2026-ibm-delivers-the-blueprint-for-the-ai-operating-model-as-the-ai-divide-widens

63. Cyc / Cycorp — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: Cyc is a hand-built, logic-based commonsense ontology and reasoning engine developed by Cycorp to let AI reason like a human.
  • Key dates: Begun July 1984 at MCC; spun out as Cycorp, Inc. end of 1994; OpenCyc released 2002, withdrawn March 2017; last stable release 6.1, 27 November 2017; founder Douglas Lenat died 31 August 2023
  • Evidence: By 2017 Cyc held ~1.5 million terms and ~24.5 million hand-written assertions after ~2,000 person-years and ~$60M by 2002 (Cycorp claims hundreds of millions in R&D overall); OpenCyc 4.0 (June 2012) had 239,000 concepts, but was withdrawn in March 2017 and the last stable release listed is Cyc 6.1 on 27 Nov 2017. No verifiable public release, commit, or news in 2025-2026 was found, although cyc.com remained live with a 2025 copyright and claims 200+ hospitals use its hospital-operations software.
  • Why: Hand-encoding commonsense never scaled: estimates of 'enough' knowledge kept rising from 250,000 rules to 24.5 million assertions with no self-learning takeoff, at enormous labour cost. Statistical LLMs then answered commonsense questions far more cheaply, superseding Cyc as a general-intelligence path, so Cycorp withdrew its open version in 2017 and retreated to low-profile niche enterprise and hospital products, with its founder's death in 2023 removing its driving force.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Cyc's auditable, context-scoped symbolic reasoning and microtheories as a verification layer, but avoid hand-encoding broad commonsense — pair a small verified core with learned models instead.
  • Sources: https://cyc.com/about-us/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyc; https://www.geschichte-der-informatik.de/articles/douglas_lenat_and_cyc/

64. Wolfram Alpha / Wolfram Language symbolic AI — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: A computational knowledge engine and symbolic programming language that computes exact answers from curated data and formal rules instead of predicting text like an LLM.
  • Key dates: Mathematica launched 23 June 1988; Wolfram|Alpha launched 18 May 2009; Wolfram|Alpha Pro launched 8 February 2012; Wolfram Language named in 2013 and formally announced June 2014; ChatGPT plugin launched 23 March 2023, replaced by Wolfram GPT after plugins retired April 2024; Wolfram Language 14.3 released August 2025; Version 15 released June 2026 and 15.0.1 in July 2026.
  • Evidence: Actively maintained in 2025-2026: Wolfram Language 15 (June 2026) added built-in AI assistants in notebooks and an MCP framework letting Claude and ChatGPT call Wolfram capabilities, followed by 15.0.1 in July 2026. Wolfram Research is privately held and bootstrapped with no VC funding; third-party revenue estimates vary widely and are unverified (about $70M for Wolfram Research in 2025 per Latka-derived reporting vs ~$155M estimated for 2024 elsewhere), so no revenue figure is treated as confirmed. Wolfram|Alpha Pro is priced at $9.99/month or $5/month billed annually ($60/year) as of August-October 2026 checks, with APIs and enterprise licensing alongside it.
  • Why: Technically it survived because deterministic symbolic computation plus curated data solves exactly what LLMs get wrong — arithmetic, symbolic math, and factual lookup — making it a trustworthy verification/tool layer rather than a competing chatbot. Economically it was sustained by diversified Mathematica licensing, Pro subscriptions, and API/enterprise revenue as a private, debt-free, VC-free company, insulating it from ad-market and investor-exit pressure. Its mass-consumer ceiling came from adoption limits: a rigid natural-language parser, a proprietary language rarely used by general developers, and a narrow answer-engine scope versus general search and conversational AI, so it never became a Google killer but endured as a specialist backend.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the pattern: let LLMs propose and plan, but route math, facts, and executable checks through a deterministic symbolic/verifier tool before acting — and avoid making that verifier do open-ended conversation.
  • Sources: https://www.wolfram.com/language/quick-revision-history/; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WolframAlpha; https://intuitionlabs.ai/articles/symbolic-ai-vs-generative-ai-wolfram-chatgpt

65. OpenAI o1/o3 reasoning models — Succeeded

  • Category: Cognitive architecture/AGI
  • What it is: OpenAI's o-series of closed large language models trained with large-scale reinforcement learning to reason via extended internal chain-of-thought — and, in o3, to call tools — before answering.
  • Key dates: o1-preview/o1-mini launched 12 Sep 2024; full o1 and ChatGPT Pro launched 5 Dec 2024, o1 API 17 Dec 2024; o3 previewed 20 Dec 2024, o3-mini 31 Jan 2025, full o3 and o4-mini 16 Apr 2025, o3-pro 10 Jun 2025; GPT-5 unified GPT/o-series 7 Aug 2025; o1 API shut down 23 Oct 2026; o3 API shutdown scheduled 11 Dec 2026
  • Evidence: Shipped in ChatGPT and API to millions of users; o1 scored 83% on AIME 2024 (vs ~13% for GPT-4o) and around the 89th percentile on Codeforces; o3 scored 96.7% on AIME 2024, 71.7% on SWE-bench Verified, 87.7% on GPQA Diamond, 84.3-87.5% on ARC-AGI at high compute, and made 20% fewer major errors than o1 in expert evaluations. Full o1 launched with $200/month ChatGPT Pro on 5 Dec 2024; o3 was $2/$8 per million input/output tokens. Standalone o-series was then retired: o1 API shutdown 23 Oct 2026, o3 left ChatGPT 26 Aug 2026 with API shutdown scheduled 11 Dec 2026, replaced by GPT-5.6-sol.
  • Why: It succeeded technically because scaling test-time compute via RL chain-of-thought produced large, verifiable jumps on math, coding and science benchmarks over GPT-4o, establishing reasoning models as a product category that rivals copied. It was nevertheless superseded as a standalone line because separate o-series models were slower, costlier and harder to route than general models, so OpenAI folded the capability into GPT-5's unified router/thinking system in Aug 2025 and retired the o1/o3 SKUs in 2026 — a consolidation, not a failure of the reasoning approach itself.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy test-time reasoning with RL-trained chain-of-thought and tool use inside the reasoning trace, but avoid a standalone reasoning SKU — bake reasoning in as a dial/router inside one unified system.
  • Sources: https://openai.com/index/introducing-o3-and-o4-mini/; https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-5/; https://www.techtarget.com/news/OpenAI-o1-explained-Everything-you-need-to-know/871976/; https://Brco.dev/insights/updates/openai-api-deprecations-2026/

66. Google DeepMind AlphaEvolve — Succeeded

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: Google DeepMind's evolutionary coding agent that pairs Gemini Flash/Pro models with automated evaluators to iteratively discover and optimize algorithms expressed as code.
  • Key dates: Announced 14 May 2025; arXiv white paper 16 Jun 2025; Google Cloud private preview 9 Dec 2025; one-year impact update May 2026; general availability on Google Cloud 9 Jul 2026
  • Evidence: Announced 14 May 2025; white paper arXiv:2506.13131 (16 Jun 2025). Results reported by DeepMind: 4x4 complex matrix multiplication in 48 scalar multiplications (first improvement over Strassen's 49 in 56 years); improved state of the art on 14 of 54 matrix-multiplication targets and ~20% of 50+ open math problems; Borg scheduling heuristic in production for over a year recovering ~0.7% of Google's worldwide compute; 23% Gemini training-kernel speedup (1% total training-time reduction); up to 32.5% FlashAttention speedup. Private preview on Google Cloud Dec 2025; general availability 9 Jul 2026 on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform, with named users/customers including BASF, JetBrains, Kinaxis and Klarna; May 2026 one-year update added genomics, power-grid, quantum-circuit and drug-discovery applications. Core orchestrator is closed-source; only result notebooks are public (github.com/google-deepmind/alphaevolve_results).
  • Why: It succeeded because every candidate is machine-verifiable, so the system can run thousands of generations without human judgement in the loop — the evaluator, not the LLM, is the source of truth. It also had a built-in first customer: Google itself, where verified wins (Borg scheduling, Gemini kernels, TPU circuits) were deployed in production before external launch, proving economic value. Its main limitation is scope, not failure: it only works on problems with a fast, reliable automatic score, and the core remains closed-source and paid, so outside reproduction is limited.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the evaluator-first evolutionary loop: let LLMs propose freely but let fast, automatic, verifiable scoring select and mutate winners — and avoid AlphaEvolve's closed, evaluator-dependent design, which only works where a trustworthy automatic score exists.
  • Sources: https://deepmind.google/blog/alphaevolve-a-gemini-powered-coding-agent-for-designing-advanced-algorithms/; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13131; https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/alphaevolve-is-available-for-everyone; https://epoch.founden.co/events/alphaevolve

67. Intel Loihi 2 — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: Intel Loihi 2 is a second-generation digital neuromorphic research chip that runs asynchronous spiking neural networks with on-chip learning for ultra-low-power, event-driven edge workloads.
  • Key dates: Launched/announced 30 Sep 2021; Hala Point announced 17 Apr 2024; Lava framework archived 13 May 2026; commercialization phase announced 18 Sep 2026; no verified Loihi 3 launch date or specs from Intel as of Nov 2026.
  • Evidence: Per chip: up to 1M neurons and 120M synapses, 128 neuromorphic cores, Intel 4 process, up to 10x faster than Loihi 1; Hala Point (Apr 2024) packs 1,152 Loihi 2 chips for 1.15B neurons / 128B synapses at max 2,600 W, deployed at Sandia. Published results include the full fruit-fly connectome (~140k neurons) on 12 chips at >100x conventional simulation speed, and an Oct 2025 keyword-spotting study up to 18x faster / 250x more energy-efficient than a Jetson Orin Nano. Transition signal: Intel archived all Lava repositories on 13 May 2026 (read-only, no further development/support), while stating a next-generation Loihi and new SDK for physical AI are in development; lab director Mike Davies said 18 Sep 2026 the lab had entered a commercialization phase. Intel's own neuromorphic page still lists Loihi 2 as current and still describes Lava as open-source, so that page is stale relative to the GitHub archive.
  • Why: Loihi 2 delivers dramatic efficiency on sparse, event-driven workloads (orders of magnitude vs. conventional hardware on narrow benchmarks), but its bespoke spiking-neural-network programming model and Lava toolchain never reached mainstream ML developers, and the chip remained research-access-only with no commercial product. Intel is therefore pivoting the program from open research toward commercialization and a next-generation chip/SDK built on open-standard AI frameworks — the hardware line continues, but the open Loihi 2 research platform is effectively being wound down, as shown by Lava's May 2026 archiving.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Loihi 2's event-driven, sparse, on-chip-learning design for ultra-low-power edge agents, but avoid its bespoke programming model — build on standard AI frameworks or the ecosystem dies with the SDK.
  • Sources: https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/research/neuromorphic-computing.html; https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/newsroom/news/intel-builds-worlds-largest-neuromorphic-system.html; https://github.com/lava-nc; https://www.supercomputing.news/emerging/neuromorphic-computing-2026-spinnaker2-intel-edge-robotics

68. IBM TrueNorth / NorthPole — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: IBM's brain-inspired chip lineage: TrueNorth (2014) was a spiking neuromorphic chip with 1M neurons, superseded by NorthPole (2023), a synchronous digital neural-inference chip that co-locates memory and compute on-die to eliminate off-chip memory access.
  • Key dates: 2008: DARPA SyNAPSE programme begins; Aug 2014: TrueNorth unveiled/published in Science; 2016: TrueNorth end-to-end ecosystem; 20 Oct 2023: NorthPole published in Science; Sept-Oct 2024: NorthPole 3B LLM inference results; Nov 2025: 288-card NorthPole LLM system (arXiv 2511.15950); Air Force contract to 2027
  • Evidence: TrueNorth (2014): 1M neurons, 256M synapses, 5.4B transistors, ~70mW. NorthPole (Science, 20 Oct 2023): 22B transistors, 256 cores, 224MB on-chip SRAM; 25x frames-per-watt vs comparable 12nm GPU on ResNet-50, 22x lower latency, 5x frames-per-transistor. Sept-Oct 2024: 3B-parameter Granite LLM at <1ms/token, 28,356 tokens/sec on 16 chips, 72.7x more energy-efficient than next lowest-latency GPU. Nov 2025 arXiv: 288-card system, 115 peta-ops INT4, 3.7 PB/s, 30kW, integrated with watsonx Orchestrate; supported by US Air Force contract FA8750-19-C-1518, reported as a $48M NorthPole Hub contract running to 2027. No general-purpose commercial product shipped as of 2026; deployments remain research prototypes and government contracts.
  • Why: TrueNorth never commercialised: its event-driven spiking design and bespoke programming language/libraries had no backward compatibility with C++/standard deep-learning tooling, creating vendor lock-in and a weak software ecosystem. NorthPole pivoted to conventional DNN/LLM inference with a compiler-mapped, synchronous design, achieving large efficiency wins on narrow benchmarks, but it remains constrained by on-chip SRAM — it cannot hold frontier-scale models (Modha: 'We can't run GPT-4 on this') — so it wins only in low-latency, low-batch inference and has not yet become a general commercial product, surviving on research and defence funding.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy NorthPole's near-memory, low-precision spatial inference for low-latency, low-batch edge inference; avoid TrueNorth's spiking, incompatible programming model and vendor lock-in that blocked commercial adoption.
  • Sources: https://research.ibm.com/publications/neural-inference-at-the-frontier-of-energy-space-and-time; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TrueNorth; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.15950; https://www.ajayk.sh/blog/neuromorphic-computing-2026-microwatts-not-llms/

69. SpiNNaker / SpiNNaker2 — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: A massively parallel, brain-inspired neuromorphic supercomputer architecture from the University of Manchester (SpiNNaker1, 1M ARM cores) and its commercial second generation SpiNNaker2 from TU Dresden spinout SpiNNcloud, using many small event-driven cores to simulate spiking neural networks.
  • Key dates: Project began 2005; first chips 2009; million-core milestone 14 Oct 2018; SpiNNaker released 2019; €8M grant for SpiNNaker2 announced 24 Sep 2019; SpiNNcloud Systems founded 2021 (TU Dresden spinout); SpiNNaker2 commercially available May 2024; Dresden system and Sandia NERL Braunfels operational/delivered spring 2025; UTSA THOR opened Feb 2026; Jülich ESAB launched 2026; SpiNNext EIC project Dec 2025-Nov 2027
  • Evidence: SpiNNaker1: 1,036,800 ARM cores, million-core milestone Oct 2018, modelling up to ~1bn neurons. SpiNNaker2: 152 Arm Cortex-M4F cores/chip, 22nm, commercial availability announced May 2024; TU Dresden/SpiNNcloud system of ~35,000 chips / >5M cores went into operation Apr 2025; Sandia's NERL Braunfels system delivered Mar 2025, modelling 175M neurons; UTSA opened NSF-funded THOR hub on SpiNNaker2 Feb 2026 (~400,000 processing elements, ~$4M NSF award 2024-2029); Jülich launched 3-year ESAB neuromorphic module using SpiNNaker2 in 2026; SpiNNcloud won €10M EIC Accelerator blended finance (grant €2,499,997.50, Dec 2025-Nov 2027) for SpiNNext successor; full SpiNNaker2 chip paper published IEEE OJCAS 27 July 2026 and SDK v0.8.0 released 31 July 2026. No independent MLPerf benchmark or commercial data-centre generative-AI customer found; company claims >70,000 chips, >12 racks, >30 customers are uncorroborated.
  • Why: It survived where many neuromorphic efforts stalled because it stayed anchored to funded research use — the EU Human Brain Project, then national labs (Sandia, Jülich) and universities — rather than chasing mainstream AI workloads it cannot yet win. Its limitation is economics and adoption: it has no independently audited benchmark against data-centre GPUs, no commercial generative-AI customer, and its customers are almost entirely publicly funded research institutions, so commercial scale remains unproven. Continued EU/NSF funding (notably the €10M EIC award for SpiNNext) keeps development active in 2025-2026, but the technology is still a research platform transitioning to product, not a widely adopted computing paradigm.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its event-driven, many-small-cores sparsity model and its research-lab-first adoption path, but avoid betting HIVE on neuromorphic hardware until it has proven, independently benchmarked wins on production AI workloads rather than neuroscience simulations.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpiNNaker; https://www.manchester.ac.uk/about/news/brain-inspired-computing/; https://www.supercomputing.news/emerging/neuromorphic-computing-2026-spinnaker2-intel-edge-robotics; https://www.eiceu.com/report/spinncloud-eic-accelerator-neuromorphic-ai-chip

70. BrainScaleS-2 (Heidelberg) — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: An analog mixed-signal neuromorphic research platform from Heidelberg University's Electronic Vision(s) group that emulates spiking neural networks in physical circuits 1,000x faster than biology, freely accessible to researchers via the EBRAINS infrastructure.
  • Key dates: Prototypes publicly available 2017 (Human Brain Project); single-chip access via EBRAINS Collab June 2020, PyNN access March 2021; core system paper Frontiers in Neuroscience 2022; public research platform since 2023; multi-chip system paper Dec 2025; three NICE 2026 papers Mar 2026; EBRAINS tutorials Oct 2026
  • Evidence: Publicly available via EBRAINS since 2023 with 520+ individual researchers served, 3,718 production deployments and ~198 hardware CI/CD runs/day over six years of operation (arXiv 2506.23901). Each chip has 512 analog neurons and 131,072 synapses running 1,000x faster than biological real time; a 24-chip multi-chip system (~12,000 neurons) was presented at NICE 2026 (Mar 2026) with sub-1.3 microsecond chip-to-chip latency, alongside real-time analog sensor-to-motor demos. GitHub org electronicvisions shows repo updates into Oct 2026 (hxtorch Sep 2026, jaxsnn Oct 2026); EBRAINS ran hands-on tutorials Oct 7 and 13, 2026. Funded by EU Horizon 2020/ Horizon Europe (HBP grants 720270, 785907, 945539; EBRAINS 2.0 grant 101147319) and DFG Excellence Strategy.
  • Why: It survives as a publicly funded research platform, not a product: analog hardware is hard to scale (only 512 neurons per chip, with multi-chip scaling only arriving in 2025-2026) and analog neuron calibration is notoriously fiddly, so adoption stays confined to neuroscience and neuromorphic-ML researchers rather than mainstream AI. Its continuity depends on EU public funding — the Human Brain Project ended in 2023 and EBRAINS 2.0 now carries it — so its economics are grant cycles, not customers. The lesson is that exotic hardware wins only when wrapped in boring, standard software interfaces (PyTorch/PyNN) and shared access, which is exactly what kept it alive.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its shared-infrastructure playbook — standard PyTorch/PyNN interfaces, CI/CD-tested hardware access, and free researcher onboarding that turned scarce exotic hardware into a 520-user platform — but avoid its grant-dependent, analog-hardware-bound model: build on commodity digital infrastructure HIVE can actually scale and sustain commercially.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2506.23901v3; https://ebrains.eu/news-and-events/2026/ebrains-researchers-present-latest-neuromorphic-computing-advances-at-nice; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2512.03781; https://electronicvisions.github.io/documentation-brainscales2/latest/brainscales2-demos/tutorial.html

71. DARPA SyNAPSE (Systems of Neuromorphic Adaptive Plastic Scalable Electronics) — Failed

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: A DARPA programme (2008-c.2016) to build brain-inspired, low-power neuromorphic computers scaling toward biological levels, whose flagship output was IBM's TrueNorth neurosynaptic chip.
  • Key dates: Launched 2008 (initial $4.9M IBM / $5.9M HRL awards, Nov 2008); TrueNorth unveiled 7 Aug 2014; LLNL 16-chip system announced 29 Mar 2016; programme scheduled to run to c.2015-2016 and then ended, with TrueNorth now End of Life.
  • Evidence: DARPA funded IBM ~$53M through Phases 0-3 (over $102.6M total programme funding by 2012-13 including HRL); TrueNorth, unveiled 7 Aug 2014 in Science, had 5.4 billion transistors, 4,096 neurosynaptic cores, 1 million neurons and 256 million synapses, at ~70 mW and 46 billion synaptic operations/sec/W; LLNL bought a 16-chip TrueNorth system in Mar 2016 and the ecosystem reached 30+ universities/labs, but TrueNorth is now listed End of Life / no longer in production and was never sold as a commercial product.
  • Why: SyNAPSE was a laboratory success but a platform failure: TrueNorth proved million-neuron spiking hardware at milliwatts, yet it had no on-chip learning, required a wholly new spiking-neural-network programming model, and arrived just as GPUs and conventional deep learning won on software, benchmarks and economics. With no general-purpose workload, no commercial product and no successor chip, it was superseded and left with no active maintenance, despite seeding later neuromorphic work such as Intel Loihi.
  • Relevance to HIVE: HIVE should copy SyNAPSE's vertically integrated ecosystem (chip/simulator/language/applications) but avoid betting on exotic hardware or a new programming paradigm without a mainstream software on-ramp and a commercial customer from day one.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SyNAPSE; https://www.neurala.com/does-artificial-intelligence-require-specialized-processors/; https://open-neuromorphic.org/neuromorphic-computing/hardware/truenorth-ibm/; https://uk.newsroom.ibm.com/2016-Mar-29-Lawrence-Livermore-National-Laboratory-and-IBM-Collaborate-to-Build-Brain-Inspired-Supercomputer

72. Harvard Kilobot swarm robotics — Succeeded

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: A Harvard (Radhika Nagpal lab) swarm of 1,024 tiny, ~$15, 3.3 cm robots that self-assemble into shapes using only simple local rules and infrared neighbor communication, with no central control.
  • Key dates: Development began Nov 2010; first introduced 2011 (25 units); 100 units by 2013; 1,024-robot demonstration and Science paper 14-15 Aug 2014; licensed to K-Team; still in active research use in 2025-2026, no shutdown
  • Evidence: 1,024 Kilobots self-assembled into shapes in the Aug 2014 Science paper 'Programmable self-assembly in a thousand-robot swarm' (Rubenstein et al.), named a Science Top 10 Breakthrough of 2014; each unit cost ~$14-15 in parts. Design/software open-sourced for non-commercial use and licensed by Harvard to K-Team for manufacture. Still a standard research testbed a decade later: a July 2025 Swarm Intelligence paper ran experiments with up to 200 physical Kilobots, a July 2025 IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters paper built robotic fabrics from up to 81 Kilobot modules, and the EU OpenSwarm project (Jan 2023-Apr 2026) validated its framework on a 900-node KiloBot testbed.
  • Why: It succeeded as a research platform because radical cost reduction (~$15/unit vs. expensive individual robots) plus open-source hardware/software made thousand-robot experiments affordable and reproducible for labs worldwide, and simple decentralized rules with built-in error correction overcame each unit's unreliability. Its limit was commercial: individual Kilobots are slow, myopic, and imprecise, so it remained an academic testbed rather than a deployed product — but that was its intended role, and it still defines the field's standard platform.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Kilobot's formula: many cheap, simple, homogeneous agents with local neighbor-only communication, self-healing/error-correction rules, and no central controller — and avoid central orchestration becoming the scaling bottleneck.
  • Sources: https://seas.harvard.edu/news/self-organizing-thousand-robot-swarm; https://spectrum.ieee.org/a-thousand-kilobots-self-assemble?itm_source=parsely-api; https://link.springer.com/content/pdf/10.1007/s11721-025-00251-4.pdf; https://ercim-news.ercim.eu/en146/r-i/openswarm-coordinated-smart-devices-for-a-sustainable-future

73. Numenta HTM / Thousand Brains Theory — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: A neuroscience-first AI research program from Numenta, now the Thousand Brains Project nonprofit, building Monty — an open-source sensorimotor learning system in which many cortical-column-like modules build reference-frame models of objects through movement and vote to reach consensus.
  • Key dates: Numenta founded 2005; NuPIC/HTM open-sourced 2013; Jeff Hawkins published 'A Thousand Brains' in 2021; Thousand Brains Project announced June 5, 2024 and incorporated as a nonprofit October 23, 2024; Monty v0.47.0 released August 2026; Two-Year Report published September 24, 2026.
  • Evidence: Thousand Brains Project's September 2026 Two-Year Report records 54 public GitHub releases of Monty, reaching v0.47.0 in August 2026, plus 16 outside contributors authoring 121 merged pull requests; a November 4, 2026 update moved Monty to PyPI distribution, Python 3.13, uv and MuJoCo. Funding includes the Gates Foundation. Monty's first quantitative evaluation (arXiv July 2025) covers 3D object recognition and pose estimation on the YCB household-object dataset, still in simulated environments. The older HTM/NuPIC codebase is in maintenance mode, while community fork htm.core shows updates into 2026.
  • Why: It remains research-stage rather than commercially adopted because it deliberately rejects the transformer/scale paradigm, so it lacks the ecosystem, benchmarks, and tooling that drive mainstream adoption, and its validation is still largely simulated 3D object recognition rather than broad real-world tasks. Progress is sustained by nonprofit and foundation funding (including Gates) rather than product revenue, which keeps development active but slow and narrowly focused. The original HTM/NuPIC stack is in maintenance mode, with momentum concentrated in Monty and a small community.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy its modular, reference-frame-based voting/consensus idea for specialist modules, but avoid betting HIVE's core on this still-unproven, research-stage paradigm.
  • Sources: https://thousandbrains.org/; https://thousandbrains.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/TBP_2Year_Report.pdf; https://forum.thousandbrains.org/t/two-years-of-the-thousand-brains-project/1212; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2507.04494

74. Cortical Labs DishBrain — In Progress

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: DishBrain is Cortical Labs' 2022 system of ~800,000 living human and mouse cortical neurons grown on a microelectrode array and taught to play Pong via closed-loop electrical feedback, later commercialised as the CL1 biological computer.
  • Key dates: Founded 2019; DishBrain Neuron paper Oct/Dec 2022; $10M raise April 2023; CL1 announced March 2025, shipped summer 2025; Doom demo Feb 2026; 120-unit Melbourne facility March 2026; 20-unit Singapore prototype unveiled Aug 6/announced Aug 17, 2026
  • Evidence: Founded 2019 in Melbourne; raised $1.56M early funding and $10M in April 2023 led by Horizons Ventures with Blackbird, LifeX, Radar and In-Q-Tel, plus a $245,000 Australian Industry Growth Program grant in 2026. DishBrain paper in Neuron (Dec 2022) reported ~800,000 human/mouse neurons learning Pong within minutes via closed-loop stimulation. Commercial successor CL1 launched March 2025 at ~$35,000, shipped summer 2025, with cultures advertised up to 6 months and Cortical Cloud access at ~$300/week; Feb 2026 Doom demo used ~200,000 neurons, and Aug 2026 brought a 20-unit CL1 prototype rack in Singapore with NUS Medicine and DayOne (plus a reported 120-unit Melbourne facility in March 2026). No public evidence shows it beating silicon on a commercially relevant workload, and neuron counts per unit vary across sources (200,000 vs 800,000).
  • Why: It remains in progress rather than succeeded because, despite shipping a real product and expanding to prototype data-centre racks in 2026, it has not demonstrated wide adoption or a commercial workload advantage over silicon. The core constraints are biological: cultures live only months, need life support, and are hard to scale, standardise and replicate, while the original learning claims remain contested and unreplicated outside the company and its collaborators. Continued venture funding, government grants and high-profile demos have sustained development, but economics and verifiable performance — not just novelty — will decide whether it becomes a sustained computing platform or remains a research instrument.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy DishBrain's closed-loop, embodied feedback learning — small adaptive units improving from success/failure signals with minimal energy — but avoid building HIVE on a fragile, short-lived substrate or unreplicated claims; require independent benchmarks before scaling any bio-inspired mechanism.
  • Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortical_Labs; https://startupfortune.com/startups-are-growing-human-brain-cells-to-power-ai-instead-of-gpus/; https://khemetic.news/singapore-brain-cell-data-center-neurons-consent-ethics-2026/

75. SynSense Speck — Succeeded

  • Category: Bio-inspired/Neuromorphic/Swarm
  • What it is: SynSense Speck is an event-driven neuromorphic vision SoC integrating a 128x128 dynamic vision sensor with a ~330,000-neuron spiking CNN processor for sub-milliwatt, always-on edge vision.
  • Key dates: SynSense founded 2017; Speck/Xylo dev kits launched Dec 2022; ~$10M pre-B+ round Mar 2023 for Speck mass production; SynSense-iniVation merger Feb 2024; Nature Communications paper 2024; Sinabs 3.1.0 Oct 2025; Series B2 $14.8M 30 Jun 2026
  • Evidence: Shipped commercial SoC and dev kit (launched Dec 2022): ~330,000 neurons, 128x128 DVS + 9-layer DYNAP-CNN, 0.42 mW idle / 0.70 mW active, 3.36 µs per-event latency, <2 mW per SynSense. Peer-reviewed in Nature Communications (2024) and used in a 2025 Science Robotics robot-localization system (LENS) and a 2025 TU Delft drone thesis; Sinabs 3.1.0 released 31 Oct 2025. SynSense raised ~$101M total per PitchBook, including a $14.8M Series B2 on 30 Jun 2026, is listed as generating revenue, and merged with iniVation in Feb 2024; a ~$10M pre-B+ round in Mar 2023 was earmarked for Speck mass production. Adoption remains niche rather than mass-consumer, with no disclosed unit volumes.
  • Why: It succeeded technically by fusing sensing and spiking compute on one asynchronous chip, eliminating frame-based power waste and achieving verified sub-milliwatt operation. Commercially it survived by staying narrowly scoped to always-on gesture, tracking, and eye-tracking niches and raising sustained venture funding ($101M total) rather than chasing general-purpose AI. Its ceiling is adoption, not physics: low 128x128 resolution, inference-only operation, and a vendor-specific Sinabs toolchain keep it a specialist edge part rather than a broad platform.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Speck's sensor-plus-compute co-design and event-driven sparsity for always-on, milliwatt perception; avoid its narrow 128x128, inference-only, vendor-locked toolchain that limits general workloads.
  • Sources: https://www.synsense.ai/products/speck-2/; https://www.hackster.io/news/synsense-launches-speck-xylo-neuromorphic-development-kits-for-edge-ai-vision-and-audio-work-2897f0e6a7ac.amp; https://pypi.org/project/sinabs/3.1.0/; https://pitchbook.com/profiles/company/179658-19

76. MemGPT / Letta — In Progress

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: An open-source platform (Apache 2.0) for stateful AI agents whose LLM manages its own tiered, persistent memory, now shipped as the Letta Code agent harness with git-backed MemFS memory.
  • Key dates: MemGPT paper posted 12 Oct 2023; repo created 11 Oct 2023; rebranded MemGPT to Letta and $10M seed announced 23-24 Sep 2024; Letta Code introduced 16 Dec 2025 and made flagship 16 Mar 2026; Letta V1 Python server archived/retired 16 Aug 2026.
  • Evidence: 25,481 GitHub stars, 2,677 forks and 7,476 commits on letta-ai/letta (accessed 2026-11-19); $10M seed led by Felicis in Sep 2024 at a $70M post-money valuation, with no Series A announced as of mid-2026; original Python V1 server retired to an archive branch on 16 Aug 2026, while active development moved to letta-ai/letta-code (TypeScript), with v0.34.38 shipped 2 Nov 2026 and ~483,000 npm downloads reported in the 30 days to 29 Oct 2026.
  • Why: The original virtual-context-management idea proved influential (1,000+ paper citations in under a year) but memory management adds extra LLM calls and cost, and the first Python API server was superseded rather than sustained. Letta pivoted in 2026 to a crowded coding-agent harness (Letta Code), where it competes with better-funded memory rivals such as mem0, and it remained at seed funding into mid-2026, slowing commercial scale despite sustained open-source activity.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy versioned, self-editing persistent memory (git-backed MemFS) as a durable agent state layer, but avoid tying memory to a single whole harness or breaking API compatibility in a pivot.
  • Sources: https://github.com/letta-ai/letta; https://letta.com/; https://aiwiki.ai/wiki/letta; https://www.reviewramble.com/github-repos/letta

77. Reflexion (Shinn et al.) — Succeeded

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: A framework in which a language agent improves across trials without weight updates by verbally reflecting on task feedback and storing those reflections in an episodic memory buffer that conditions its next attempt.
  • Key dates: Submitted 20 Mar 2023 (arXiv v1); last revised 10 Oct 2023 (v4); published NeurIPS 2023; GitHub repo created 22 Mar 2023; last repo push 14 Jan 2025; no shutdown or pivot announced.
  • Evidence: NeurIPS 2023 paper (arXiv:2303.11366): 91% pass@1 on HumanEval vs 80% for GPT-4, 130/134 ALFWorld tasks, ~+20% on HotPotQA; original repo github.com/noahshinn/reflexion has 3,435 stars, 343 forks, 176 commits, MIT licence, 0 releases, and last push 2025-01-14 — dormant as a codebase, but the pattern remains in use in 2025-2026 via LangGraph's canonical Reflexion tutorial and numerous reimplementations. No funding, company, or shutdown notice — it was an academic research artifact, not a commercial product.
  • Why: It succeeded academically because verbal reinforcement was a cheap, training-free way to convert sparse pass/fail signals into actionable lessons, and its Actor-Evaluator-Self-Reflection pattern was simple enough to be absorbed into LangGraph, LangChain and other frameworks. The original repo itself is now dormant because it was a paper artifact, not a maintained product — no team or funding sustained it, and its gains depend on a reliable evaluator (unit tests/environment reward) and costly multi-trial GPT-4 calls, failing outright on WebShop and underperforming on MBPP where self-written tests were unreliable. Its influence therefore outlived its codebase, which is the expected lifecycle for a research pattern rather than a failure of the idea.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the Actor-Evaluator-Self-Reflection loop with a small episodic memory of verbal lessons and bounded retries, but only where HIVE has a reliable external verifier — avoid unbounded self-critique on unverifiable tasks.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11366; https://github.com/noahshinn/reflexion; https://papers.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2023/hash/1b44b878bb782e6954cd888628510e90-Abstract-Conference.html

78. Self-Refine — Succeeded

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: A training-free method in which a single LLM generates a draft, critiques its own output, and iteratively refines it using that feedback.
  • Key dates: arXiv submitted 30 Mar 2023, revised 25 May 2023; GitHub repo created 28 Mar 2023; published at NeurIPS 2023, 10-16 Dec 2023; last repo README update Nov 2023; no shutdown or pivot.
  • Evidence: Paper reports ~20% absolute average improvement across 7 tasks (range 5-40%) over one-step generation with GPT-3.5/ChatGPT/GPT-4, including up to 13% on code models; GitHub repo madaan/self-refine has 851 stars, 71 forks, 122 commits, Apache-2.0, 0 releases, and its last README update was Nov 2023. The pattern is now standard as Anthropic's evaluator-optimizer workflow and is still cited/discussed in 2025-2026 surveys and papers, though the original repo itself has not been actively developed since 2023.
  • Why: It succeeded academically because it was simple, required no training data, RL, or second model, and delivered large measured gains, making it easy to adopt as a baseline pattern. Its ceiling was the reliability of self-feedback: later work showed LLMs often cannot reliably self-correct reasoning or factual errors and can regress correct answers, so successors such as CRITIC grounded critique in external tools. The original code repo froze after the paper, but the idea survived and was absorbed into mainstream evaluator-optimizer loops rather than failing.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the cheap generate-feedback-refine loop with bounded iterations, but ground feedback with an external verifier and a hard stopping rule rather than trusting same-model self-critique alone.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.17651; https://github.com/madaan/self-refine; https://selfrefine.info/; https://backpropagation.ai/posts/ai-agent-collaboration-icml-iclr-neurips-survey/

79. Voyager — Stalled/Abandoned

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: Voyager is the first LLM-powered embodied lifelong-learning agent in Minecraft that autonomously explores, writes and self-verifies executable JavaScript skills via GPT-4, and stores them in a growing reusable skill library without fine-tuning.
  • Key dates: arXiv posted 25 May 2023, revised 19 Oct 2023; presented NeurIPS 2023 and published in TMLR March 2024; GitHub repo created 25 May 2023, last commits July 2023; no releases, no formal shutdown
  • Evidence: Benchmarks: 3.3x more unique items (63 in 160 prompting iterations), 2.3x longer travel, up to 15.3x faster tech-tree milestones vs ReAct/Reflexion/AutoGPT, and the only method to reach diamond tools. Adoption: GitHub MineDojo/Voyager has 7,334 stars, 710 forks, 35 commits and 0 releases (MIT); cited ~1,641 times as of May 2026 per a secondary review. Maintenance: latest commits were July 2023 per a 2026 framework survey, arXiv last revised 19 Oct 2023, and the repo is not archived with no formal shutdown notice.
  • Why: Voyager was a research prototype, not a product: it was tightly coupled to GPT-4 API cost and latency, Minecraft 1.19/Mineflayer, and a fixed 2023 stack, with no commercial path. Its authors moved on to successor embodied-AI work (Eureka, then GR00T), so the original codebase stopped being maintained even though the skill-library idea remained highly influential academically.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Voyager's executable skill library plus self-verification/critic loop so capabilities compound, but avoid building on its frozen, single-environment research codebase with expensive black-box GPT-4 calls and no maintained runtime.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16291; https://github.com/MineDojo/Voyager; https://voyager.minedojo.org; https://futureagi.com/blog/self-improving-ai-agents-open-source-frameworks/

80. Generative Agents / Stanford Smallville — Succeeded

  • Category: Multi-agent framework
  • What it is: A 2023 Stanford/Google research system in which 25 LLM-driven agents live in a Sims-like town called Smallville, remembering, reflecting on, and planning their days, producing emergent social behaviour such as organising a Valentine's Day party from a single seed instruction.
  • Key dates: Submitted 7 Apr 2023; revised 6 Aug 2023; presented UIST '23, 29 Oct-1 Nov 2023; code released on GitHub 23 Jul 2023, last commits Aug 2023; follow-up 'Generative Agent Simulations of 1,000 People' Nov 2024; Simile Series A 12 Feb 2026, Series B 30 Jul 2026.
  • Evidence: Original repo: 22,392 GitHub stars, 3,132 forks, 31 commits, created 23 July 2023, with no commits since August 2023 — a frozen research artefact, not a maintained product. Academic/commercial lineage succeeded: UIST 2023 Best Paper, widely cited (reported >5,600 citations); 2024 follow-up built agents for 1,052 real Americans from 2-hour interviews, reproducing General Social Survey answers at ~85% of participants' own two-week test-retest accuracy; spin-out Simile exited stealth 12 Feb 2026 with a $100M Series A led by Index Ventures, then raised >$200M Series B on 30 July 2026 at a $2B post-money valuation, naming CVS Health, Wealthfront, Deloitte and Gallup as customers.
  • Why: It succeeded because its simple, legible architecture — a natural-language memory stream scored by recency, importance and relevance, plus periodic reflection and top-down planning — made believable multi-agent behaviour reproducible and became the canonical reference for agent memory and social simulation. The original Smallville code itself was never productised: a 25-agent, two-day run cost thousands of dollars in 2023 tokens and took days, with fragile retrieval and spatial errors. Its value therefore migrated to the grounded-person follow-up research and to Simile, the authors' enterprise simulation company, rather than to continued maintenance of the demo repo.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the memory-stream + reflection + hierarchical-planning architecture with recency/importance/relevance retrieval, and ground agents in real interview/data rather than fictional personas; avoid shipping the unmaintained Smallville demo as-is, given its high token cost, retrieval failures, and lack of long-run coherence.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.03442; https://github.com/joonspk-research/generative_agents; https://aiwiki.ai/wiki/simile_ai

81. STaR (Self-Taught Reasoner) — Succeeded

  • Category: Memory/Verification/Self-improvement
  • What it is: A 2022 Stanford method that bootstraps LLM reasoning by iteratively generating chain-of-thought rationales, rationalizing wrong answers using the known correct answer, and fine-tuning only on rationales that yield correct answers.
  • Key dates: arXiv 28 Mar 2022 (v2 20 May 2022); NeurIPS 2022 (Dec 2022); GitHub repo created 3 Oct 2022; successors Quiet-STaR Mar 2024 and V-STaR Feb 2024; no further repo activity after 2022.
  • Evidence: NeurIPS 2022 paper: on CommonsenseQA, STaR took GPT-J 6B to 72.5% (vs 60.0% direct fine-tuning, 36.6% few-shot CoT), matching a fine-tuned model 30x larger (73.0%); GSM8K improved 5.8% to 10.7% across STaR rounds. The original repo (github.com/ezelikman/STaR, created 2022-10-03) is frozen at just 6 commits, 236 stars, 27 forks, 0 releases — but the method is heavily cited and lives on in successors: V-STaR (2024), Quiet-STaR (2024), STaR-GATE and CARE-STaR (2025), and its generate-filter-finetune loop is now a standard ingredient in RLVR pipelines behind DeepSeek-R1/o1-style reasoning models.
  • Why: Technically it worked and was simple: it needed only a few seed rationales plus questions with known answers, and it demonstrably closed most of the gap to a 30x larger model. Its limits — dependence on ground-truth labels, need for initial above-chance reasoning ability, and rationalization that can reverse-engineer answers without true reasoning — meant the standalone JAX/TPU codebase froze after 2022 while the idea was absorbed and superseded by verifier-augmented variants (V-STaR, Quiet-STaR) and large-scale RLVR/GRPO training. It succeeded as a foundational technique rather than as a maintained product.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy STaR's grounded self-improvement loop — generate attempts, keep only those verified against ground truth, retrain, repeat — but add a verifier/process check, since answer-only filtering lets spurious rationales slip through.
  • Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.14465; https://github.com/ezelikman/STaR; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2403.09629; https://github.com/goku2130/research-wiki/blob/HEAD/topics/self-improvement-and-self-play.md

82. AlphaCode / AlphaCode 2 (DeepMind) — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: DeepMind's competitive-programming code-generation system that samples many candidate programs from a fine-tuned transformer (Gemini Pro in AlphaCode 2) and filters, clusters, and reranks them by executing tests to submit only the best few.
  • Key dates: Announced 2 Feb 2022; published in Science 8 Dec 2022; AlphaCode 2 technical report released 6 Dec 2023 alongside Gemini; no standalone product launch or shutdown notice since.
  • Evidence: AlphaCode (Science, 8 Dec 2022) ranked in the top 54.3% on 10 simulated Codeforces contests and solved 34.2% of held-out CodeContests problems within 10 submissions. AlphaCode 2 (6 Dec 2023, Gemini Pro fine-tuned on contest data) solved 43% of 77 problems across 12 Codeforces contests vs 25% for AlphaCode (1.7x), reaching the 85th percentile on average, and needed ~100 samples to match AlphaCode's performance with a million samples. Its CodeContests dataset was released on GitHub; neither system was ever released as a standalone product, and its sample-and-filter pattern was carried forward into Gemini coding agents and DeepMind's AlphaEvolve (May 2025).
  • Why: It succeeded as a research milestone because massive sampling plus execution-based filtering works on well-specified problems with a fast, trustworthy test oracle. Its own technical report conceded it required extensive trial and error, relied heavily on filtering out bad samples, and was too costly to operate at scale, so it never shipped as a product and its approach was absorbed into general Gemini coding agents and AlphaEvolve rather than continued as a standalone system.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the sample-generate, execute, cluster-by-behaviour, and rerank loop with a cheap deterministic verifier, but avoid brute-force massive sampling without a strict cost/sample budget.
  • Sources: https://deepmind.google/blog/competitive-programming-with-alphacode/; https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/AlphaCode2/AlphaCode2_Tech_Report.pdf?ref=blog.mlq.ai; https://techcrunch.com/2023/12/06/deepmind-unveils-alphacode-2-powered-by-gemini/

83. Google AutoML / Neural Architecture Search — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Google's AutoML is a cloud suite that uses Neural Architecture Search (NAS) and transfer learning to automatically discover model architectures and hyperparameters for custom vision, tabular, text, and video models, now delivered inside Vertex AI.
  • Key dates: NAS paper Nov 2016 / ICLR 2017; NASNet 2017; Cloud AutoML announced 17 Jan 2018 (AutoML Vision first); AutoML Tables Apr 2019; consolidated into Vertex AI May 2021; AutoML Text training ended 15 Sep 2024, shutdown 15 Jun 2025; AutoML Video deprecated 31 Jul 2024, shutdown 31 Jul 2025
  • Evidence: NASNet reached 82.7% top-1 ImageNet accuracy; EfficientNet-B7 reached 84.3% top-1 while 8.4x smaller and 6.1x faster than the best prior ConvNet. Google reported Vertex AI AutoML as the leading AutoML framework in Kaggle's State of Data Science and Machine Learning 2021 report. It remains in use in 2026 — G2 lists 51 AutoML reviews, including August 2026 reviews of Vertex AI Vision — but scope narrowed: AutoML Text training ended 15 Sep 2024 (serving ended 15 Jun 2025) and AutoML Video shut down 31 Jul 2025, both redirected to Gemini prompting/tuning.
  • Why: It succeeded technically because NAS produced genuinely better, more efficient architectures (NASNet, EfficientNet, MnasNet) and Google productised that research as a no-code enterprise service, but the original RL-based search was enormously expensive (about 800 GPUs for weeks) and later work showed simpler methods could match it. Market timing then shifted the value from bespoke small-model search to foundation-model prompting and fine-tuning, so Google kept the still-valuable vision/tabular AutoML while retiring text and video AutoML in 2024-2025 in favour of Gemini tuning.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy bounded, cost-capped automated search with verified benchmarks, but avoid open-ended architecture search without a budget and a human-verifiable winner — HIVE should search configurations, not reinvent models from scratch.
  • Sources: https://cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/docs/deprecations; https://ai-solutions.wiki/tools/google-automl/; https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1905.11946; https://techcrunch.com/2018/01/17/googles-automl-lets-you-train-custom-machine-learning-models-without-having-to-code/

84. SWE-agent / OpenAI Codex agentic coding — Succeeded

  • Category: Specialist/Adapter
  • What it is: An agentic coding system — SWE-agent as the Princeton academic agent that autonomously fixes GitHub issues via a purpose-built agent-computer interface, and OpenAI Codex as its commercial successor: a sandboxed CLI/cloud/IDE agent that writes, tests and proposes code changes in real repositories.
  • Key dates: SWE-agent repo created 2024-04-02; paper May 2024, NeurIPS 2024; SWE-agent 1.0 Feb 2025; mini-SWE-agent superseded SWE-agent (Jul 2025, 65% SWE-bench Verified); OpenAI Codex CLI repo created 2025-04-13, cloud research preview launched 2025-05-16 (codex-1), Plus rollout 2025-06-03, GPT-5-Codex Sep 2025, generally available 2025-10-06, GPT-5.3-Codex Feb 2026.
  • Evidence: SWE-agent: 20,829 GitHub stars, 2,276 forks, 2,182 commits, MIT; SWE-agent + GPT-4 resolved 12.47% of full SWE-bench (2,294 tasks) vs 1.96% for Claude 2 with retrieval, and SWE-agent 1.0 + Claude 3.7 was SOTA on SWE-bench in Feb 2025. Codex: 135,797 GitHub stars, 21,529 forks, 13,863 commits, 1,306 releases, Apache-2.0; OpenAI reported daily usage up >10x since early Aug 2025, >5 million weekly users by Jul 2026 and weekly active users up >5x in H1 2026; GPT-5.1-Codex-Max scored 77.9% on SWE-bench Verified (500 tasks) and GPT-5.3-Codex set a new high on SWE-Bench Pro.
  • Why: Technical fit: a narrow, verifiable task — fix a real issue and pass its tests — plus a purpose-built tool interface and sandboxed execute-test-revise loop made autonomy tractable where generic agents failed. Adoption/economics: SWE-agent was open, hackable research infrastructure that defined the benchmark, while Codex paired the same loop with frontier models and ChatGPT/CLI/IDE distribution, so improvements in the underlying model directly raised agent performance. The academic original was superseded by the simpler mini-SWE-agent, showing the winning pattern was minimal scaffolding around a strong model, not a heavier framework.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy the narrow verifiable loop — purpose-built tools, sandboxed execution, and test-based pass/fail verification — and keep scaffolding minimal around the strongest model rather than building a heavy multi-agent framework first.
  • Sources: https://github.com/SWE-agent/SWE-agent; https://github.com/openai/codex; https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/; https://openai.com/index/codex-now-generally-available/

85. Ray / Anyscale — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Ray is the open-source Python framework for distributed AI compute (training, data, serving, RL), and Anyscale is its managed commercial platform founded by Ray's Berkeley creators.
  • Key dates: Ray begun 2016-2017 at UC Berkeley RISELab, released 2017; Anyscale founded 2019; Series C at $1B valuation 2021-2022; Ray contributed to PyTorch Foundation Oct 22, 2025; Nscale definitive agreement to acquire Anyscale July 30, 2026 (closing expected H2 2026, not yet verified closed); Ray 2.59.0 Oct 2, 2026.
  • Evidence: Ray 2.59.0 released Oct 2, 2026 and Ray 2.60.0 followed in Nov 2026, showing active maintenance. At its Oct 22, 2025 move to the PyTorch Foundation, Ray had 39,000+ GitHub stars and 237M+ downloads; later sources cite 41,000+ stars, 500M+ downloads and 1M+ Ray clusters/month. Users include OpenAI (ChatGPT/GPT training), Uber, Spotify, Instacart, Coinbase, Canva and Character.ai. Anyscale raised ~$259-281M total (sources conflict) at a $1B valuation; on July 30, 2026 Nscale entered a definitive agreement to acquire Anyscale, reported by Bloomberg at ~$1.65B (official terms undisclosed), expected to close H2 2026.
  • Why: Ray succeeded because its @ray.remote-style API made scaling Python across GPUs nearly frictionless, so it became the de facto standard just as LLM training/serving exploded, with OpenAI-scale adoption creating a durable ecosystem. Anyscale monetized that adoption via managed clusters and its RayTurbo runtime, while moving Ray to the PyTorch Foundation in 2025 de-risked vendor lock-in and made the ~$1.65B Nscale acquisition possible. The main friction was always commercial: many enterprises self-host free Ray, so Anyscale's standalone value was clearer as part of a full-stack GPU cloud than alone.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Ray's winning formula — one simple Python API over distributed work, an open core governed by a neutral foundation, and a managed layer on top — but avoid making HIVE's commercial fate depend on a single framework or a second scheduler that competes with Kubernetes-native standards.
  • Sources: https://www.anyscale.com/press/nscale-acquires-anyscale-enhancing-its-full-stack-ai-cloud-platform; https://pytorch.org/blog/pytorch-foundation-welcomes-ray-to-deliver-a-unified-open-source-ai-compute-stack/; https://docs.anyscale.com/release-notes/platform/2026/october; https://www.readsignal.io/article/nscale-anyscale-acquisition-full-stack-ai-hyperscaler-2026

86. Temporal — Succeeded

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Temporal is an open-source durable-execution platform that lets developers write long-running workflows as ordinary code while it persists state and automatically retries and resumes work after failures, now widely used to orchestrate AI agents.
  • Key dates: Founded 2019 by Samar Abbas and Maxim Fateev (ex-Uber Cadence); first production release Oct 2020; Series C Mar 2025 ($146M, $1.72B valuation); Series D Feb 2026 ($300M, $5B); OpenAI Agents SDK integration GA Mar 23, 2026; Series E Sep 14, 2026 ($550M, $12.55B); Server v1.32.2 Nov 2, 2026.
  • Evidence: $550M Series E on 2026-09-14 at $12.55B valuation; >$250M annualized revenue run rate, up >200% YoY; 4,300+ paying customers including OpenAI, Snap and NVIDIA, up 139% YoY; 1.9 trillion billable actions in August 2026, up >350% YoY; 43M+ open-source installs; Temporal Server v1.32.2 released 2026-11-02, ~23,900 GitHub stars and 2,192 commits in the prior 12 months.
  • Why: It succeeded by solving a pre-AI distributed-systems problem — failure handling for long-running work — that became critical as agents began running for days or months, rather than pivoting opportunistically to AI. Its code-first model, MIT-licensed open source, managed Temporal Cloud, and proven enterprise adoption created a durable moat, and the AI boom amplified demand instead of requiring a new product thesis.
  • Relevance to HIVE: Copy Temporal's durable-execution model — persistent workflow state, automatic retry/resume and human-in-the-loop signals — as HIVE's reliability layer for long-running multi-agent work, rather than rebuilding crash recovery per agent.
  • Sources: https://temporal.io/blog/temporal-raises-usd550m-series-e-at-usd12-55b-valuation-ai; https://automationatlas.io/rankings/best-process-orchestration-2026/; https://www.wsic.dev/release-radar/

87. Flower (Flower Labs) — In Progress

  • Category: Orchestration/Enterprise
  • What it is: Flower (flwr) is an open-source, framework-agnostic Python framework for federated and collaborative AI, letting organizations train and run models on data that stays distributed across devices and organizational silos.
  • Key dates: Framework created as a research project in 2020 (Cambridge/Oxford); Flower Labs founded March 2023; $3.6M pre-seed 2023; $20M Series A 15 Feb 2024; Flower 1.26 released Feb 2026; v1.43.0 released 4 Nov 2026 — no shutdown, pivot, or acquisition.
  • Evidence: Actively developed in 2026: framework v1.43.0 released 4 Nov 2026, with 10 releases in the prior 90 days (v1.29.0 on 11 Apr 2026, v1.28.0 on 2 Apr 2026). ~6,900-7,100 GitHub stars, 180+ contributors, 7,000+ developers, 2,500+ ecosystem projects built on it. Funding: $3.6M pre-seed (2023, Y Combinator W23) and $20M Series A led by Felicis on 15 Feb 2024 at a ~$100M valuation. Adopters include dozens of Fortune 500/Global 500 companies and institutions (Samsung, Nokia, Porsche, Bosch, Brave — reportedly deployed across 50M browser clients — plus J.P. Morgan, NHS, Mozilla, MIT, Harvard, Stanford, Oxford, Cambridge). Photon/FlowerLLM federated LLM pre-training reached 7B parameters (MLSys 2025).
  • Why: It succeeded because it solved a real, growing constraint — privacy and compliance blocking centralized data pooling — with a genuinely framework-agnostic, Apache-2.0 open-source design that researchers could adopt for free, while Flower Labs monetized via an enterprise platform and forward-deployed engineering. Sustained academic credibility (Cambridge/Oxford origins, MLSys-published federated LLM pre-training) and marquee enterprise adopters created a self-reinforcing community, keeping releases frequent through 2026 rather than fading after the initial funding.
